Murals Interview: How To Create A Mascot Who's Not As Annoying As Ocarina Of Time's Navi

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scott Baird is a Contributor from the UK with over a decade of video game writing experience.

Murals is an upcoming action RPG by Dlala Studios that, on the surface, looks a great deal like Hades. The concept of Murals involves twisted and humorous takes on classic fairy tales, like Rapunzel, which is an idea that’s more commonplace than the straight tellings of those stories, with everyone from Disney to Alan Moore turning fables on their head.

Originality isn’t the golden ticket to a video game being enjoyable, and in this case, the differences from genre norms are in the gameplay, not the story. While Murals might look like countless isometric action RPG roguelikes on Steam, it’s going for a linear, narrative-based progression, with crafted levels and enemies, rather than relying on randomness.

Playing it straight is a surprisingly bold move, and it runs counter to the story of Murals. DualShockers recently visited Dlala’s offices to preview Murals, where we got the chance to speak to the studio’s CEO, AJ Grand-Scrutton, about the many choices that went into making Murals.

Q: The idea of fables and fairy tales being twisted and viewed through a modern lens has been done many times before. Were there any specific examples that inspired Murals?

Grand-Scrutton: So obviously the source material was where we went to initially, the grim stuff and the Hans Christian Andersen. But I mean, I loved American McGee's Alice. I was a massive fan of that. There's a lot of like comic books as well, like Fables. I love Wolf Among Us. So I love that familiarity that you recognize the story, but enough difference that you don't feel you're just retreading old ground.

Q: Have any video games inspired the gameplay or design of Murals?

Grand-Scrutton: Obviously, Murals is isometric and it's got pretty chaotic moment to moment. So a lot of modern Roguelikes, your Hades, et cetera. I struggle with permadeath, especially as I've got older. I'm quite impatient and moody. And so kind of what I really loved was like the moment to moment in a lot of modern roguelikes, but I didn't necessarily get on very well with the permadeath loop. So that was a big influence for how murals came about.

Like, can we take some of those chaotic moment to moment experiences; those combat augmentation systems and deliver them in like a bit longer form content? And also like, you know, weird influences in there as well. You know, like a lot of our world building Psychonauts and a lot of double fine stuff was really prominent in our minds. And, you know, classic combat games like DMC, Bayonetta.

But yeah, it was really just about us trying to capture an experience we didn't feel we could fully play out there already.

We traveled to China to chat with Soulframe about his upcoming action RPG, Phantom Blade Zero.

Q: The protagonist is mostly silent in Murals, with most of the talking being done by the character’s sidekick, Loaf. How do you go about designing a talkative side character like Loaf in a way that makes them likeable. Were there any concerns they’d become another Navi from Ocarina of Time?

Grand-Scrutton: Yeah, so, I mean, we always are very aware that our style of writing can become... Every character can feel like that very quickly for people. I mean, I think if you look at Battletoads, you look at Illusion Island and you look at Murals, you can tell it's very much like the same writing team that have done them.

Loaf is kind of, in a weird way, is obviously this sentient sketch come to life. He's the most naturally written character because it's often me and Kelsey, my lead writer, just typing what we're thinking about situations or stupid commets. Like, often we'll just be on calls and we'll play through the game. And Kelsey, as well as writing, she actually is Loaf. She does the voice of Loaf. And we'll almost do our own little director's commentaries as we're playing the game to figure out where Loaf comments should go and what would Loaf say in this situation.

I think with humour, comedy in games, we're not unique. There's other people that do comedy games, but it's still quite a niche. And I think you kind of just have to lean into what you are. And I think that we are that self-referential type of humour. We do the fourth wall breaking. There is the modern label of the meta-level stuff in there. And I think it'd be easy for us to go half-arsed or half-cocked on it. But we just kind of leant into it. This is what we find funny, and that's kind of how we write the game.

Q: Why go for such a basic, sketch outline design for Loaf?

Grand-Scrutton: Yeah, I love the juxtaposition. So obviously, as you've seen from the couple of murals you've played, the characters that live in the world are hand-animated, but they've still got depth to them.

The invasive forces, which is the distorted, the enemies, and the hero, the player, are fully animated. And I love this idea that Loaf was just... the hero just made this rough sketch in their notebook or something and accidentally got brought to life. I love Doodlebob, who is definitely an influence from the SpongeBob cartoons.

I just think there's something really nice about... we've got these animated characters that are still not realistic, but almost like they make sense. And then Loaf is just a cartoon. He doesn't really have the rules of physicality that a humanoid would have. He is just who he is. And I think that just gave us some really fun opportunities, and it let us do some silly things with him.

An interview with Jonathan Blow about the making and future of Order of the Sinking Star.

Q: Is Murals intended as a linear experience, or is there more of a roguelike/Hades-style progression, with a hub area, random layouts, and upgrades sticking between runs.

Grand-Scrutton: No, it's very, very linear. Like, we're always up front about that because we know how misleading it can be when you see like an isometric action game. It's very linear. Like, the only real elements of random to it are the order you get the areas in.

So, you know, if you do a playthrough, you may get different areas to someone else doing the exact same playthrough. But the story, the quests, the enemies themselves, that is all very sculpted.

Q: In the Murals preview, there’s a very generous window for being able to dash out of attacks. Is this planned to be maintained for the final build or is the gameplay balance being tweaked still, out of fear it could make the combat too easy?

Grand-Scrutton: Yes, no, maybe to all the above. We're still tweaking. We'll be tweaking until my Tech Director pries it from my cold dead hands. We definitely have gone generous with a lot of things like this. I think the difficulty you're playing in general is probably on the easier side of what we would balance the final game.

You know, we're looking to make it customizable though, right? In terms of like enemy health, enemy damage, amount of health, you probably notice in the build you're playing right now that you always have enough health drops to get full health basically. That would probably be the generous setting on the slider for health drops. But I think the dash serves a very specific purpose in our game, obviously it's not unique at all.

But we've got a lot of boss fights, environmental hazards and other enemies as you progress through the game where giving the players a generous dash meant we could get more chaotic with the experience. I think, you know, if you look at our game and compare to some of our other peers, I think that's one of the slight difference of our moment to moment.

I think we're not a one versus one experience. We're not one versus many. We're like a one versus some is what I like to say. But we do reasonably small spaces with a reasonably large amount of enemies. And so while the play space is quite chaotic, we wanted to give the player simple enough verbs that they could control so they never felt like the game was being unfair.

DualShockers spoke with the developers of Wo Long 2: Wings of Ember about why a sequel was made and how the gameplay has changed from its predecessor.

Q: Some of the enemies have a flashing light before they attacked. Is this hinting at a parry mechanic for the full version of the game? Do you dare release a game in 2027 with no parrying?

Grand-Scrutton: I mean, we are daring to not release. No, there's no parry. They're very generous tells on some of the enemies that do more damage. We definitely discussed parrying really early on. We discussed parrying, we discussed blocking. But it just, it felt a bit like a hat on a hat for what we were making. Like, you know, we like, we believe in like, depth through simplicity. This is not a big combo based game.

You know, it's not even as combo laden as like our Battletoads was. It's a very simple amount of inputs with like varying weapons and a lot of depth comes through the power-ups. And the problem I find with parrying is like, modern games are amazing.

Like, you know, Expedition 33, all these games are absolutely outstanding. I find when I play them that my gameplay just becomes around parrying. All I do is parry. Like, in a lot of ways, although I love them, I play them multiple times.

I felt that learning to do the block timing in the Arkham games made the combat a little bit less exciting for me because I actually certainly changed my entire strategy in those games to be let the enemy come to me. And I just didn't want that for this. I didn't want the focus to be on parry timing. I wanted the focus to just be like, have a little bit of fun controlled chaos.

Q: Finally, why choose Murals as the game’s name?

Grand-Scrutton: Yeah, we are traditionally, not necessarily publicly, very awful at doing project codenames. Like, the codename for Battletoads was Frogspawn, which is not the most... And the codename for Illusion Island was Illusions because we weren't originally going to call it that.

So Project Murals was the codename for Murals because we love the idea that these worlds are like these fairy tales brought to life by through these painted murals the creator has done. But the team will tell you, I have this obsession, I think Pokémon is the greatest name for a franchise ever because I love a name that is memorable and it's also the name of a thing in the game or in the world. And I love the idea of us having murals because the levels are called murals and it's also the name and it makes sense.

So it's kind of like this dragon I've been chasing and it just fit the game and it worked for me. And there's some funniness in there as well to me through some previous titles we've worked on and stuff in the past where we've tried to put forward names for things and we've been told that the majority of the...

We had a game we were working on years ago, years and years ago, and it was around animatronics. And this was years back. I mean, it was before it was necessarily common lingo but I really wanted to call it Tronics and I was like, oh, how cool is this name? And we got told by our partners at the time that no one knows what an animatronic is so we couldn't do it. And then Five Nights at Freddy's came out a year later and I was like, everyone knows what animatronic is, look.

And we've had similar things with this, like when we'd kind of pitched this to people before the name Murals, people were like, I don't think anyone's gonna really know what a mural is. And I was like, I'm pretty sure people know what a mural is. So it was nice to kind of have that control ourselves.

The development team behind Disgaea Mayhem discusses balancing an action RPG while maintaining insane level and damage numbers.

Murals Preview: Frantic Fairy Tale Fun

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scott Baird is a Contributor from the UK with over a decade of video game writing experience.

Fairy tales have been deconstructed and shown through a modern lens so many times that subversions are almost as common as straight tellings of the original stories. Murals is an upcoming action RPG that takes a comedic, subversive look at classic kids' stories, wrapped in an addictive gameplay loop familiar to fans of the big hits of the past few years.

Roguelike action RPGs have exploded in popularity, especially on PC, with gamers becoming hooked on brutally difficult gameplay merged with random elements that offer near-infinite replayability. On the surface, Murals seems cut from the same cloth as something like Hades, being an action RPG with an isometric viewpoint, where a long hero dashes around, avoiding attacks, while unleashing an increasingly larger arsenal at their foes.

DualShockers visited developer Dlala Studios at their home base for a hands-on preview of Murals. This consisted of playing the first two chapters and experiencing an in-development build of the game that is still being tweaked and worked on. What’s here is impressive, especially in terms of the aesthetics, with gorgeous visuals on display for Murals’ fairy-tale world.

The Murals preview I played featured two stages: Little Briar Rose (the story that formed the basis for Sleeping Beauty), and Rapunzel. The story starts with the protagonist, whose appearance can best be described as “Player 2 April O’Neil,” waking up in a mysterious new world with no memory of how they got there.

The protagonist of Murals doesn't talk much, but when they do, they have branching dialogue options. Sadly, the game immediately says that these have no bearing on the gameplay and are just for flavor, so the choices may as well not exist. While I'm not expecting some Mass Effect-style consequences for every dialogue choice, it'd be nice if some of the responses had some kind of bearing on the gameplay.

The protagonist doesn't talk much because they're soon joined by Loaf, the game's mascot character. Loaf is a poorly drawn doodle that resembles an eraser with lizard-like plates on its back. Loaf quickly sets the comedic tone of the story, with Monty Python-esque retorts to the silliness of their situation, before the two are joined by Slash Gordon, the player’s trusty blade.

The chapters of Murals I experienced involved solving the locals' problems, such as helping an insomniac Sleeping Beauty or freeing a trapped Rapunzel by climbing her hair up the tower. There was also an overarching story involving a mysterious being known as The Creator, who looks like a smug art critic and seems to pull the strings from afar, guiding the protagonist along.

Twisted Tower is a terrifyingly twisted fairy tale that's done its homework.

One reliable ally the player has between battles is Magic Dave, voiced by legendary UK comedian Bob Mortimer. He’s the Resident Evil 4 Merchant of the game, providing upgrades (called Eeries) between stages, while exchanging witty banter with Toast.

The comedy in Murals is going to be love it or hate it. Saying it’s steeped in British irreverence is an understatement, and that sentence alone might be off-putting to some. A great comparison is the works of the late great Terry Pratchett: that provides a metric of Britishness the player should expect.

Dlala Studios is no stranger to pretty 2D games, having previously created the modern Battletoads game, as well as Disney Illusion Island. Murals marks the studio's departure from established intellectual properties and into publishing, which is why fairy tales were the company's next stop, since they're in the public domain.

Easily the high point of Murals is its visuals. Each level in Murals is a stunning, hand-drawn piece of artwork, where every room appears lovingly crafted and filled with little touches to admire while exploring.

Each level in Murals is a stunning, hand-drawn piece of artwork, with every room lovingly crafted and filled with little touches to admire while exploring. The same is true of the character artwork, with everything polished to perfection. The level of detail is especially impressive, considering Murals is still being developed, yet the team already has the aesthetic nailed down.

What once started out as a subgenre of NES classics has now become a typically hand-drawn set of video games that never fail to impress.

The only exception to the pretty visuals is the enemies in Murals, which are 3D models and nowhere near as visually impressive as the stages or NPCs. The monsters are quite generic and don't feel like they match the fairy-tale world they exist in, with designs that could fit into pretty much any action RPG.

The boss monsters are similarly whelming, with the giant frog faced in the Little Briar Rose stage doing the same jumping attack over and over again, only adding mobs to his move pool. In contrast, the final encounter of the Rapunzel stage is just waves of enemies, with no big boss in sight.

Fairy tales are full of creative villains who could be repurposed for use in video games. Just look at The Wolf Among Us for only one example among many. The enemies in Murals need an injection of creativity in both their design and battle strategy if the game wants to keep the player's interest.

It's impossible to play Murals and not compare it to Hades or its sequel. Just looking at a gameplay video on the Steam page, the comparisons are easy to see. It’s impossible to deny that Murals has Hades DNA, with the same basic combat loop, even though it takes a drastically different approach.

In Murals, the player has a choice of melee weapons (the sword named Slash Gordon and the hammer called the Wolfhammer in the demo), a ranged weapon with recharging ammo, and a super move that builds up over the battle, all of which are used to destroy foes. The player can dash to avoid enemy attacks and must survive waves of enemies through chains of rooms, with liberal health drops between areas.

As someone who was brutalized by Hades 2 in the past, one mechanic in Murals' combat immediately stuck out. When swinging a weapon or using a ranged attack, it's possible to perform a dash and gain invincibility frames. This makes the combat significantly easier than in similar games, as the player doesn't have to commit to attacks in the same way as they would in, say, a FromSoftware game, leaving them locked into a position where they can be countered.

Sébastien Benard's action roguelike has resurfaced with a release window after several years of radio silence.

The quick dash is more effective when using Slash Gordon, as it synergizes well with a swift weapon. This isn't to say that the Wolfhammer isn't also broken in its own way, as it has a breath weapon-style attack that can be merged with a Frost Rune to perform multiple hits that each have a chance of inflicting the Freeze status effect on an enemy, drastically increasing the chance of paralyzing them.

This makes the combat significantly easier than in similar games, as the player doesn't have to commit to attacks in the same way as they would in, say, a FromSoftware game, leaving them locked into a position where they can be countered.

Murals is still a work-in-progress, so there’s plenty of time for developers to tweak the gameplay balance. What’s here is considerably easier than many of the action RPGs on the market, but the reason for that could be related to the fact that it’s a mostly linear, story-based game, with few roguelike elements.

The levels in Murals might look like the randomly connected rooms of countless roguelikes, but the stages have been crafted and designed to tell a story, including NPC encounters, puzzles, and even side quests. While there are some random elements, such as how the upgrades/Eeries are handled, the bulk of the game is a planned affair, which explains why it’s easier than its contemporaries.

Each level in Murals is a stunning, hand-drawn piece of artwork, where every room appears lovingly crafted and filled with little touches to admire while exploring

It's a bold move to go for a linear approach in a market so consumed with randomness, especially when Murals uses a gameplay style and overall aesthetic that’s tied to roguelikes. That being said, there’s plenty of room for a more focused and narrative-driven title that uses the gameplay loop of familiar titles, especially when the levels look this good.

Murals has a ton of potential and an amazing pedigree behind it. Dlala Studios has produced some amazing titles in the past, and the fact that the game is so polished and impressive at this point in development is a feat unto itself. The hard part is going to be tweaking the combat to make it satisfying for everyone, meaning some strict difficulty modifiers could be the answer.

The comedic writing and tone in Murals are trickier, as they're so baked into the game’s identity, yet also have the potential to miss the mark. It’s the kind of thing that someone will know they’re going to enjoy within a short period of time, and hopefully, Mural’s subversive take on fairy tales is as appealing to the audience as its combat.

In a new episode of Nightdive's Deep Dive podcast releasing later today, legendary voice actor Stephen Russell spoke about his experience portraying the protagonist of the Thief series, Garrett. We also got final confirmation of something Nightdive had been teasing for awhile: Russell will be reprising the role in the new Shattered Mirror expansion coming with the Thief remaster on December 3.

I don't think I realized before this interview that Thief was Russell's very first videogame role. Before working with Looking Glass, he had experience in theater, radio and "medical reads" where he had to pronounce complex disease names in an even, doctorly cadence.

Russell said that he felt able to let loose for the first time as Garrett, channeling Clint Eastwood to nail the rogue's singular noirish rasp. Russell hadn't been much into games, with his main frame of reference being simple arcade and educational fare, so he was struck when he first saw an early build of Thief, still remembering "the incredible claustrophobia they had created."

Recalling the creative ferment of Looking Glass in the '90s, Russell said that he had "never seen so many sleep-deprived people in [his] life." He also affectionately recalled the studio's habit of pulling devs out of their office chairs to fill out games' VO roles alongside the professionals: "Having got my start as an actor in community theater, it felt very familiar," Russell quipped. "You're not painting the sets today, today you're playing the lead role!"

Even after a long career appearing in the likes of Fallout, The Elder Scrolls, and Dishonored, Russell still counts Garrett as one of his favorite roles, and said that playing both the protagonist (Garrett) and antagonist (Father Karras) in Thief 2⁠—and having so few people realize he was voicing Karras⁠—remains one of his proudest acting achievements.

And Russell seems chuffed to be returning to the role of Garrett so many years later. "The writing this time around is just as strong as the first time and honors what was created originally," Russell said, adding that it was "great to be back with the old guy."

It's honestly just lovely to see how much this role still means to Russell, and that he's as happy to be returning as Garrett as we will be to hear him. Tarnished Mirror is shaping up to be a real "our powers combined" sort of project, as development was headed up by the team behind the beloved Thief: The Black Parade mod from 2023. You'll be able to listen to the full interview on Nightdive's YouTube channel later today, and you can wishlist Thief Remastered on Steam.

Aura Ink 10 review: A digital photo frame that looks (almost) like a real picture

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Aura Ink 10 is a second-generation ePaper photo frame that’s smaller and less expensive than the original, and can display a few more colors. If you’re looking for a digital photo frame you can hang on your wall without any wires showing, this is the one to get, though it is more expensive than most other frames, and overall it's less vibrant than a traditional digital photo frame.

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

A year ago, Aura introduced the Aura Ink, the first ePaper photo frame; this device, while not as vibrant as a traditional digital photo frame, had the advantage of letting you hang it on a wall — no power cord required — for up to three months at a time without needing to be recharged.

The company is back with the Aura Ink 10, the first in the second generation of ePaper style photo frames. This frame can show more colors, and at $349, is also less expensive, albeit still well above some of the best digital photo frames. So, is the premium worth it?

The Aura Ink 10 went on sale on November 10, 2026, for $349 at Aura's website. It’s offered in just one size, and with just one matte color (black). The original only came with a white matte; I wish the company offered both options.

The frame itself is very thin, at a little less than an inch, and comes with both a stand for placing it on a table, as well as pins so you can mount it on a wall. Three buttons sit inconspicuously on the back edge of the frame, and let you switch photos and get the frame ready to go. Overall, it’s a very subtle design, and it’s unlikely that anyone would recognize it as a digital frame at a glance.

The Aura Ink 10 uses an E Ink Spectra 6 display, which can produce nine colors (black, white, red, yellow, blue, green, off-white, mustard yellow and orange), up from six on the original Aura Ink. That means it can show more nuances in pictures, though don’t expect true-to-life fidelity that you would get from a traditional LCD picture frame. The 10-inch frame also has the same resolution as the 13-inch model, which means greater pixel density.

To see the difference in Aura Ink generations, I placed the 10-inch Ink next to the 13-inch Ink, and then displayed the same photos on each.

The 10 inch model tended to show photos a little darker, but with better transitions between light and dark areas. For example, in an image of a girl jumping off a sand dune, there was less obvious pixelation from the sun-drenched stretches of the beach to shaded areas.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

In another photo of a girl in front of a blue and white wall, the blues of the wall were a bit deeper, but shadows were more pronounced, making it more difficult to make out features in her face and neck area.

The Aura Ink 10 has very gentle lighting that only turns on if it senses someone in front of the frame. Again, it’s not something you’d notice unless you see it turn on, but it helps bring a little more vibrancy to the photo.

Aura says the Ink 10 will last up to three months on a charge, assuming it changes photos just once per day. Once it runs low, you simply have to plug it into a USB-C for a little bit.

Aura also updated the manner in which the frame refreshes; on the original model, the screen kind of flashes randomly as the photo changes. On the Ink 10, there’s a wave-like pattern that starts in one corner, and ripples across the screen. It’s more intentional, and doesn’t make it look like the frame is shorting out.

One thing that Aura does well is make it easy to add photos to its devices. For starters, you can sync your iCloud or Google Photos albums in the Aura app. And, to help narrow things down, the Aura app can filter photos by person, so that you can more easily find photos of particular individuals; unfortunately, there’s no pet filter. If you want to skip the app entirely, you can also either email or text photos to the display. Handy!

As someone who tests smart home devices for a living, I suspect my house, which has smart displays everywhere, is a bit different than most. So while I appreciate traditional digital photo frames — the Alexa Photo Frame is one of my favorites, for its innovative use of AI — it’s nice to have some tech that doesn’t look like tech.

Yes, there are some tradeoffs to an ePaper photo display like the Aura Ink 10. You’re not going to get colors that really fly off the screen, and you’re going to pay a premium over other photo frames. But, in a digital-everything world, I appreciate its subtleness.

See the first images from the new ZWO Seestar S30 Plus — a smart telescope aimed at beginners

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

While rivals announce increasingly expensive smart telescopes, ZWO’s new Seestar S30 Plus makes galaxy and nebula photography surprisingly affordable.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As rival makers announce increasingly ambitious smart telescopes, ZWO has taken a different approach. Its new Seestar S30 Plus launches today and will be available online from Seestar’s website and Amazon, just in time for Christmas. Better still, we’ve been using it for a month and can bring you a first look.

Announced a few months after the flagship Seestar S50 Pro and replacing the now-discontinued Seestar S30, the new Seestar S30 Plus retains its 30mm aperture and compact chassis, but upgrades to four-element apochromatic optics with extra-low dispersion (ED) glass and two 3.7-megapixel cameras.

Both produce 1,440 x 2,560-pixel images, while a much-improved 43.5-degree wide-angle camera can capture the Milky Way, constellations, star trails and nightscapes. Built-in filters, a mosaic mode and equatorial (EQ) tracking add considerable deep-sky astrophotography potential.

For a telescope weighing just 3.64 lbs (1.65 kg), it’s impressive. It easily fits into a camera bag and could happily live on a bookshelf.

I’ve already photographed the Andromeda Galaxy (M31), the Pleiades (M45), the Dumbbell Nebula (M27) and the North America Nebula (NGC 7000). The results are excellent for such a small instrument, especially when imaging nebulas with its built-in dual-band filter, which helps reduce light pollution.

The Seestar app adds new in-app post-processing options, so you don’t have to mess around with any of that — though you can also export everything as lossless FITS files. The S30 Plus also includes a magnetic solar filter, enabling you to indulge in solar photography safely.

With Dwarflab’s $1,299 Draco (3.5-inch/90mm aperture) recently landed in stores and Vaonis’ own $4,990 Atlas (3.1-inch/80mm aperture) not available until 2027, ZWO’s decision to offer an upgraded entry-level smart telescope that buyers can get their hands on right now looks particularly smart.

With ZWO's pattern of delivering cheaper smart telescopes without sacrificing on quality, the Seestar S30 Plus could be one of Christmas 2026’s best gifts for anyone interested in astronomy — and maybe that’s the point of it. Either way, first impressions are good. A full review is coming tomorrow, so check back at Space.com to find out our full thoughts and testing.

Stanford's century-long AI study admits it's already out of date

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Five-year review charts upheaval in work, science, and public life – with little consensus on who's in control

Twelve years into Stanford's 100-year study of artificial intelligence, the project's third report raises more questions than it answers.

The 2026 AI100 Study Panel report acknowledges its limitations in its twelfth takeaway: "This report will be out of date by the time you read it."

But it's nonetheless a worthwhile exercise for those who haven't been paying close attention to the development of machine learning over the past few years and for policymakers who may benefit from a review of AI's metastatic growth.

"The last five years constitute a watershed moment in the history of AI," said Professor Sheila McIlraith, chair of the AI100 Standing Committee, in a statement. "This report examines what led to the extraordinary capabilities of today's AI systems, and how they are reshaping society. As we look ahead, we anticipate continued increase in AI capabilities alongside transformative advances in science and medicine.

"Rapid change will challenge governments and industry to support and safeguard our people, our institutions, and our critical infrastructure. Both the benefits and the risks of AI technologies require the same clear-eyed scrutiny."

Established in 2014, the One Hundred Year Study on Artificial Intelligence (AI100) is overseen by a Standing Committee of AI experts and administered by Stanford's Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence (HAI). It's charged with producing reports every five years, the first of which debuted in 2016.

The 2026 report's other key points highlight the transformation brought about by increasingly capable machine learning software.

It identifies scaling as the engine of AI's advance, but also points to the growing cost and difficulty of obtaining training data as constraints on further progress.

The report likens AI's disruptive potential to that of the World Wide Web – without dwelling on the irony that the web itself is among the things being disrupted. It points to creative industries, computer programming, and mathematics as fields once deemed beyond the reach of automation that are now being upended.

Science, the report says, is being transformed by AI as it accelerates research timelines and opens up novel challenges. At the same time, the report cautions that "we will need to understand how AI-mediated information environments reshape collective cognition and moral behavior." The recent retraction of some 1,400 scholarly papers for failure to meet academic standards highlights the urgency of that message.

Then there's the AI investment boom and "fears of a financial bubble," which won't be assuaged by recent NASDAQ volatility and revelations about Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with an alleged intelligence operative for China.

Concerns about the concentration of frontier AI development, the pressure AI puts on cybersecurity, and the active use of AI for military applications are also noted in the report.

So too are worries about what AI-generated media does to trust.

Item nine on the report's takeaway list is perhaps the most noteworthy: "AI is reshaping the lives of billions of people, yet the public has had little say in how it is developed and deployed."

The recent US midterms suggest voters are finding ways to make themselves heard about AI datacenters, even if that falls short of shaping broader AI policy.

Item ten posits that longstanding academic concerns about AI bias, ethics, and safety are beginning to converge with what until recently seemed like worries inspired by speculative fiction, such as AI agents hacking into companies without human oversight.

And item 11 frames the entire report by calling out the lack of a coherent regulatory response to AI: "AI governance is fragmented and increasingly framed in terms of national security, with little international consensus among states on its direction."

Those takeaways introduce the AI100 Study Panel's examination of 12 standing questions about what AI makes possible and how humans are handling it. Easy answers are in short supply.

But as the report's disclosure section makes clear, even the people taking the century-long view are using the technology to assess itself.

"Reflecting the progress in AI over the period this report covers, AI tools were used extensively in its production," the report explains.

"The report was written by humans, and its authors used AI primarily to transcribe and summarize meetings, support dictation, and check drafts for typos, consistency, duplication, tone, and structure. In some cases, AI suggested rewording of human-written text. AI was also used to compare this report with previous AI100 reports, identifying where expectations and reality have diverged or converged over five years. After completion, AI tools checked for inadvertent plagiarism and signs of AI-generated content." ®

Border wall decision caps busy Monday at SCOTUS

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

It was a busy Monday at the Supreme Court: the justices heard two oral arguments, released an order list, and decided the border wall dispute on the interim docket. Keep reading to catch up on these and other developments.

On Monday, the Supreme Court granted the Trump administration’s request to be allowed to resume construction of its border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson indicated, without explanation, that she would deny the request. Learn more about the dispute in the On Site section below.

Earlier on Monday, the justices heard arguments in two cases: Nielsen v. Watanabe, on whether and when federal officials can be held liable for constitutional violations; and Hoffmann v. WBI Energy Transmission, Inc., on whether the “just compensation” paid to land owners losing property to a pipeline project should be determined by referring to state law, which often provides for a higher payment than what the Constitution requires.

Also on Monday, the Supreme Court denied review in over 200 cases, including disputes involving the George Floyd protests in Minneapolis, actor Randy Quaid, and the sons of former baseball player Roberto Clemente. It also asked the United States solicitor general to share the government’s views on a case over whether U.S. terrorism victims may sue 21 pharmaceutical and medical-device companies under the Antiterrorism Act for allegedly aiding and abetting Iraqi terrorists. For more on Monday’s order list, see the On Site section.

Today, the justices will hear argument in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards, LLC, on whether the Constitution allows the executive branch – as opposed to the courts – to adjudicate an employer’s obligation to comply with employment agreements concerning temporary immigrant workers.

On Wednesday, the Supreme Court Building will be closed in recognition of Veterans Day.

Kelsey Dallas is SCOTUSblog's managing editor and the primary author of the SCOTUStoday newsletter.

This Couple Rented Out a 17th-Century Stone Village for Their Misty Cliffside Wedding in the Azores

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Kathryn Mawhinney and Grayson Judge’s first date was technically a phone call. A seven-hour phone call, to be precise. “We both grew up in the Bay Area but didn’t meet until 2024,” says Kate, who at the time was still finishing college in Atlanta. Rather than waiting until they were in the same place, the two spent six weeks talking on the phone every day. “After that first call, I knew I needed to have her in my life,” recalls Grayson. “She was unbelievably charming, funny, and warm, and I knew there would be a line of guys waiting for her when she got home.”

When they eventually had their first in-person date, it was in San Francisco, halfway between their two hometowns. Grayson planned a night out at a tequila bar (margaritas are Kate’s favorite), followed by tapas for dinner, and finally a lookout point in the Russian Hill neighborhood. “I texted one of my buddies on my way home and told him I would marry her one day,” he says.

Fifteen months later, Grayson proposed at that same lookout point. “I got her there by convincing her we were on our way to brunch with my cousin, and instead brought her back to the place where it had all started,” he says. Despite the couple having previously talked about a general timeline for getting engaged, the actual proposal caught her off guard. “The second I realized what was happening, I immediately started sobbing uncontrollably,” she says.

When the couple was deciding where to get married, they landed on an intimate gathering of family and friends in Fajãzinha on Flores Island in the Azores. (They also plan to have a larger wedding next summer in a town the bride’s family grew up visiting.) The island is among the most remote in the Azores, and is where Grayson’s maternal ancestors are from. “My Azorean heritage has always been a source of pride for my family and me,” he says. “Returning to my ancestral homeland to marry the love of my life was truly an indescribable feeling.”

They rented out the entirety of Aldeia da Cuada, a 17th-century stone village turned hotel that was able to accommodate everyone in town for the week’s festivities. “The little houses were originally built higher on the island to escape pirates, and the whole place felt like a fairy tale,” Kate says.

Kate took the lead with conceptualizing and planning, while Grayson spearheaded logistics. “My man of honor jokes that it was really just me and my 100-page Canva board against the world,” she says. Kate sourced vendors from all over the world, including Etsy stationery artists and commercial wholesalers for embroidered towels and aprons.

Among their standout vendors was their florist, Alice of Jungle Utopia, who purchased special headphones that translated French to Portuguese so she could figure out how to import the flowers they wanted. “And on top of everything else, she even foraged in the mountains for garnishes for our Palomas. She is truly such an angel,” Kate says.

Carlota, the owner of Aldeia da Cuada, was also instrumental in the planning process. “She and her team went above and beyond, building cocktail tables by hand, learning to make passion-fruit margaritas on the fly, and working with civil and parish authorities to help pull off the first wedding our church had hosted in more than 20 years,” Grayson says. “I joke that Carlota is the second love of my life,” Kate adds. “We truly could not have pulled this off without her.” A month before the wedding, they also brought on a team of two from Mason Market (Sam and Tyler) to bring everything over the finish line.

For their welcome party, Kate chose a Ralph Lauren gown circa 2001 (her birth year) and paired it with Jimmy Choo heels and her mother-in-law’s diamond studs. “The earrings were especially meaningful: They had belonged to her mother and were originally given to her by Grayson’s late grandfather, whose ashes were scattered on Flores,” Kate says. Grayson opted for a dark brown suit and tonal shirt from Todd Snyder.

The morning of the wedding, Kate slipped into a robe and slip she had thrifted in France to get ready. “We didn’t have elaborate dressing teams or glam squads for everyone,” she says. “My best friends [got] ready on the floor using their iPhones as mirrors. They joked that they experienced the purest form of girlhood.”

The ceremony took place in the Church of Nossa Senhora dos Remédios, a Catholic church constructed in 1776. Kate walked down the aisle in a long-sleeved, open-back gown that she had found while shopping with her grandmother and mother. “We customized the Sareh Nouri gown extensively, removing the bow and extra train in the back, changing the short sleeves to long sleeves, extending the train, and adding tiny buttons along the sleeves,” she says. She paired the gown with Amina Muaddi heels, vintage earrings, and a veil with a removable blusher.

“I will never forget the first glimpse I caught of her through a small crack in the wooden church doors,” Grayson says of Kate’s dress. For his own look, he wore a Saint Laurent tuxedo with a Giorgio Armani tuxedo shirt, which had been gifted to him by his mother-in-law a few years earlier.

A local priest named Júlio presided over the ceremony, which was half in English and half in Portuguese. A few of the townspeople sat in the back pews to witness the ceremony. “There was something so moving about having them there,” Kate says. “It made me feel like we were becoming part of something much bigger than just our own marriage.

After the ceremony, the two were waiting to sign the marriage certificate when Kate felt a light tap on her arm. “I looked down, and a little girl handed me a drawing and said, ‘This is for you,’” she recalls. It was a picture she had drawn of the couple. “It was the pianist’s daughter, and we were told later that it was her first wedding and she thought that Kate was a princess,” Grayson says.

The group proceeded down to the town square of Fajãzinha, where a wine bar had been set up. “Each guest had a wine glass with a Portuguese scarf tied around it, which Kate had embroidered with their names,” Grayson says. The priest, local townspeople, taxi drivers, and shepherds all joined the celebration.

When guests arrived at the reception, a handwritten letter from Kate awaited each of them at their respective place settings. “Kate is the only person I know who would handwrite four-plus-page, personal, heartfelt letters to 29 people expressing her gratitude for their role in helping her become the woman she is today,” Grayson says.

Once speeches kicked off, the couple were both brought to tears when Grayson’s mother took the microphone. “She hired a genealogist and began the speech by reading the names of Grayson’s ancestors who had lived on Flores, including multiple couples who had been married in the very same church where we had said our vows just hours earlier,” Kate says. “We had no idea, and it made the entire day feel even more special.”

The group eventually migrated to the circular stone roof of an old atafona (traditional mill) and danced under string lights, drank wine straight from the bottles, and partied into the night on the makeshift dance floor (“with a constant fight for who was on aux”). The after-party moved to the pool, where everyone swam and danced until 6:30 in the morning. “As proof that I was having the best night of my life, I managed to roll my ankle and just kept dancing,” Kate says.

Looking back, it was these moments of friendship and family they say they’ll remember most. “I think an underrated part of a wedding is that while you’re marrying the love of your life, you’re surrounded by the people who were your entire life first,” Kate says. “One of my relatives, who hadn’t flown in decades because he was afraid of flying, got on a plane because I was getting married. My mom pressed three of the groomsmen’s suits when they wandered into her little stone house looking for help… They say your wedding sets the tone for your marriage, but I hope that week sets the tone for the rest of our lives.”

Flores Island. In the foreground is the historic stone village of Aldeia da Cuada, where we held our reception, and in the distance, Fajãzinha village and the Church of Nossa Senhora dos Remédios. The last time a member of my family was married in that church was 150 years ago. —Grayson

Why You Should Pack Your Hiking Boots on a Trip to St. Croix

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The smell hit me out of nowhere: an uncanny whiff of sweaty socks. I instinctively glanced around for another hiker. My guide, local naturalist Olasee Davis, laughed and pointed to a thick brown seed pod lying on the trail. It had fallen from a West Indian locust, more commonly known as a stinking toe tree.

The culprit loomed farther down the trail, its massive limbs weaving a vaulted canopy overhead. “It tastes better than it smells,” Olasee said, picking up the pod and cracking it open on a rock to reveal the powdery pulp inside. I watched him take a bite but decided to pass. I suspected stinking toe fruit was an acquired taste, and with only a few days remaining on my trip to St. Croix, I'd save the acquiring for a future visit.

As we continued along, Olasee introduced me to other tropical evergreens, including the soursop, laden with spiky fruit, and the red manjack, much shorter and shrubbier, with brownish-red berries. Around the next bend he pointed out nonnative fruit trees: papaya from South America and mango from India. But he hadn't brought me here just for the botany. He'd brought me to understand why this forest matters.

As an ecologist, historian, and assistant professor at the University of the Virgin Islands, Olasee helped lead the effort to establish Maroon Sanctuary Territorial Park in 2025. Encompassing 2,469 acres in the northwest corner of St. Croix, the park protects part of Maroon Country, the island's steep, forested interior where people who had escaped slavery found refuge during the 1700s and early 1800s. The new park also safeguards former sugar plantations and mill sites, which have been slowly returning to nature since Denmark ended slavery in the Danish West Indies (present-day US Virgin Islands) in 1848. By that time more than 100,000 enslaved Africans had passed through the port of Christiansted on St. Croix, the largest of the islands, to be sold to plantation owners in the Danish Caribbean sugar economy.

Olasee and I were following an old plantation trail through one such former estate when he suddenly stopped and then scrambled up a steep embankment into the trees. I followed, brushing aside slender branches. Hidden beneath the understory, almost swallowed by spider lilies, was a burial ground. Most graves were marked with conch shells. Some were covered by low, weathered concrete slabs. One was marked by a headstone, its inscription softened by decades of moisture and lichen. The hidden cemetery wasn't an isolated discovery. For more than 40 years, Olasee has bushwhacked his way through Maroon Country to search for burial grounds and other traces of those who escaped slavery, documenting sites that would otherwise disappear beneath the forest. Now that it's been conserved as a territorial park, that work can continue in a more systematic, permanent way. Or as Olasee put it, “I could die in peace now.”

Travelers have traditionally come to the USVI for beach vacations and sailing charters; now Maroon Sanctuary is opening up another side to the islands. The park's founders are also building out an official trail network. The roughly 10 miles of trail that I explored with Olasee were a patchwork of former estate roads, plantation footpaths, and forgotten forest routes that he'd pieced together over decades. Soon these trails will be easier to navigate without needing someone like Olasee to lead the way. The next morning I met Kristina “Kitty” Edwards, director of the US Virgin Islands Division of Territorial Parks & Protected Areas, at a trailhead on the slopes of 1,165-foot Mount Eagle, the island's highest peak. We were about to hike the first official segment of the trail, a three-mile loop designed for hiking and mountain biking, which opened this past May. When completed, the trail system will wend 30 miles through the forested backcountry.

As excited as Kitty was to show off the new path, a first of its kind on the island, she wasn't in a hurry. “I'm a meanderer,” she said with a shrug and a smile. In the shirt-soaking heat of the tropics, I was grateful to take it slow. We ascended amid white-winged butterflies and yellow-breasted bananaquits. After an hour we reached the stone remnants of the old watchtower that marked the summit. A spur trail led us to an overlook where we paused to take in the 270-degree view, bookended by emerald peaks to our right and the inviting turquoise waters of Cane Bay to our left. It occurred to me that Maroon Sanctuary wasn't just about the land. The park's protected boundary extends all the way to the sea, helping shield Cane Bay's coral reefs and the nearby Annaly Bay tide pools from pollution and sediment.

A green sea turtle in the warm waters around St. Croix

I spent the next day reveling in those crystal waters. At Sweet Bottom Dive Center, I rented a mask and fins, and went snorkeling among the parrotfish, angelfish, and wrasse that inhabit the shallow reef just offshore. After watching a sea turtle munch on seagrass, I flipped over onto my back. Above the beach the densely forested peaks of Maroon Country rose in layers, a physical reminder of the many lives and histories held within.

The Waves at Cane Bay, an 11-room boutique hotel built into the rocky shoreline overlooking the bay, was reimagined in 2021 as a stylish oceanfront retreat with private patios, two bars, and the sustainable seafood restaurant Ama. The hotel is a four-minute walk to the beach at Cane Bay and a nine-minute drive to the new Mount Eagle trailhead.

Located directly across the road from Cane Bay, the Landing Beach Bar serves up Caribbean specialties like jerk chicken and fried plantains in a fun, convivial setting. Leatherback Brewing Company is St. Croix's first craft brewery and an unofficial headquarters for the island's mountain-biking community, complete with a full-service bike shop inside.

Editor's Letter: Introducing Our Winter Travel Issue

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It was a long and hot summer, and I welcomed the cooler weather as soon as the leaves started to turn. My closet full of coats and jackets had sat sadly neglected for many months; the new boots I bought in April on a clearance rack are only now about to get their moment. I'm excited for the Christmas lights in London and New York, the festive trees, chestnuts roasting on street corners—and yes, my Christmas wish list (featuring a dangerous number of watches and pieces of jewelry) is ready too. There is something about the winter that still feels exotic and enthralling to me. I saw snow a few times in Kashmir as a kid, but the first real blizzard I encountered was when I was a freshman in college in Pennsylvania. It was late at night, but my roommate dragged me out of bed to make snow angels. I still remember lying on my back, waving my arms around—the sensation of cold all along my body was surreal, as was the way in which white feathery bits floated down from the sky. I recall thinking, What a strange but wonderful feeling! I discovered two things quickly: One, I was a terrible skier. And, two, I loved après-ski—to be nursing hot chocolate by crackling fireplaces and snuggled in blankets.

Our story on Banff perfectly encapsulates the promise of winter: the adventure, the exhilaration, the romance of a landscape transformed overnight. But equally seductive is the prospect of winter sun. Come January I'll happily trade snow boots for sandals and fly to Tenerife, Spain, or Boracay, in the Philippines, or Brisbane, Australia. That dose of vitamin D is essential by then, when the Christmas lights have come down and everything seems gray and empty. At that point I need a beach—and something colorful and sparkly to wear on it. So often I close my eyes and return to the end-of-year trips I've made to Maldives, Indonesia, Mexico, and remembered the sunrises, the sound of the waves, the palm trees swaying in the breeze, the fiery red chiles, and fresh coconut water. It's been a strange year. But as we navigate the final months ahead, this issue is filled with reasons to feel excited about what comes next. I hope it gives you plenty to dream about—and somewhere to go.

In Winter, Canada's Banff National Park is So Much More Than a Skiers' Paradise

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

My view from the foot of gleaming Victoria Glacier was straight out of a snow globe. Two shaggy ebony draft horses pulled a vintage sleigh along a powdery path that hugged the shores of frozen Lake Louise. Lycra-clad Nordic skiers glided across the snow-dusted ice, navigating around life-size snowmen. In the far distance a ray of sun glinted off the turret of an ice-sculpted castle, a mini version of the grand Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise hotel, which stands sentinel over this unbelievably pretty patch of wilderness in Banff National Park. A soft gust of wind swirled a flurry of flakes across the scene. The winter wonderland in front of me was magical enough to make even the most die-hard snowbirds reconsider the season.

I've always felt winter could be approached in two ways: You could be lazy and binge Netflix for three months, or you could be sporty and get after it in the mountains. But a weeklong trip to the Banff and Lake Louise region of Alberta opened my eyes to a gratifying middle ground. This area is a true winter playground, where you could downhill-ski or engage with the elements in so many other ways: snowshoeing, going for a horse-drawn sleigh ride, or watching the snowflakes fall while you're soaking in an outdoor hot tub. My time ended up being more about appreciating nature than conquering it on the slopes.

A seat with a view inside Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise

I arrived in early January on the tail of one of the area's snowiest Decembers on record, though you couldn't tell from the landscape around Calgary airport. Umber-hued prairies and agricultural land surround the gateway city, and it wasn't until 40 minutes into my 90-minute drive west on the Trans-Canada Highway that the Great Plains transitioned to the Canadian Rockies and the sky started nuking snow.

Home to SkiBig3—a trio of resorts that include Banff Sunshine, Lake Louise Ski Resort, and the mom-and-pop Mount Norquay—Banff National Park was the place to be during a winter in which most of the American West suffered a snow drought. In my 20s I would have driven straight from the airport to the ski slopes. Now in my mid-40s, with an unpredictable knee, back-to-back, bell-to-bell days have lost their appeal. But the childlike joy of playing in the snow has not. And Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise was the perfect base for “soft” winter adventure.

The resort's address, in the heart of Banff National Park overlooking glacier-fed Lake Louise, envelops guests in nature. No surprise—Lake Louise was originally developed for outdoor tourism. In 1885, after the last spike of the Canadian Pacific Railway was driven in, future company president William Cornelius Van Horne built hotels along the route. Among the first, the Banff Springs Hotel was designed for horseback sightseeing. The Chalet at Lake Louise, a humble log cabin, was marketed as a destination for fishing, canoeing, and alpinism. Van Horne hired Swiss guides to teach his guests to safely navigate the mountains, and they are partially responsible for introducing locals to the sport of skiing.

A horse-drawn sleigh ride around Lake Louise

In 1925 the original Chalet, which had been damaged in a fire, was reimagined as Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise, a grand, Renaissance Revival–style resort with 539 rooms and suites. Today, a century after opening, some characteristics—like its staff dressed in lederhosen-inspired uniforms and its fondue-focused restaurant, Walliser Stube—nod to the original Swiss influence, and the lobby-level Guide's Cabin, decorated with vintage photos and stocked with books on alpine living, continues the property's mountaineering legacy. Through the hotel, guests can sign up for snowshoe hikes or Bavarian curling lessons and rent ice skates and Nordic skis.

My first morning, I met guide Kevin Kirkpatrick at the cabin for an activity called Powderfest Snowshoeing. I've long found snowshoeing a little snoozy and worried how we'd fill the allotted three hours. The activity's name, it turned out, wasn't a cutesy marketing ploy. The resort has seven snowshoe trails, but because there was a base of 61 inches and another foot of fresh powder, we went “off-roading.”

Leaving behind the groomed lakeshore trail, we trekked up the shoulder of Fairview Mountain, and all signs of civilization disappeared as we entered a frosted forest straight out of a Dr. Seuss story. “Really get in there and explore,” he encouraged as we wandered past fir and larch trees bent at cartoonish angles, their limbs heaving with snow. I followed him up a massive mound of powder only to catch the metal cleat of my snowshoe on a branch and topple backward, leaving an imprint like a gigantic snow angel.

Time flew as Kirkpatrick shared wisdom on everything from the current weather anomaly to how, in 1882, Edwin Hunter, a member of the Stoney Nakoda, first led a young European wrangler, Tom Wilson, to what the Indigenous people of this area called Ho-run-num-nay (Lake of the Little Fishes). He chose to ignore the name and anointed it Emerald Lake for its jewel-colored waters. Two years later the government changed the moniker to Louise to honor a daughter of Queen Victoria, Princess Louise Caroline Alberta.

An ice climber on the Lake Louise Lakeshore Trail

I returned to the hotel thoroughly exhausted from hours of high-stepping and grateful that I was booked for a session at Basin Glacial Waters, Fairmont's new, guests-only thermal bathhouse. Designed by Italian architect Matteo Thun, the building, with floor-to-ceiling windows that frame the lake and towering peaks at every turn, feels like part of the landscape. This is the epitome of nature bathing, I thought as I took in the views from the hot-stone room, silent salt chamber, and indoor-outdoor vitality pool.

I didn't think it was possible to distract me from the dramatic vista, but Basin's signature Aufguss sauna ritual, the conclusion to my multisensory journey, was impossible to ignore. This choreographed sauna performance popular across Europe has only recently been introduced to North American spas. For 15 minutes our sauna master, Kyan Van Haasteren, a young, blond Dutchman, theatrically waved a towel over scorching sauna stones to waft steam toward me and 32 other guests. He danced around the 185-degree-Fahrenheit space to an upbeat soundtrack of Luis Fonsi while smashing snowballs scented with aromatic oils over the hot stones to release the fragrance of Siberian fir. It felt more like a Vegas show than a wellness ritual, but the entertainment took my mind off the discomfort of the heat. I exited in a sweaty daze and willed myself to follow Van Haasteren's lead and dunk myself into the outdoor cold plunge. In seconds the tingling numbness gave way to a heady euphoria. I looked up to see the start of a steady snow spiraling down, one that wouldn't let up until late the next day.

I may not be a first-to-last-chair skier any longer, but I'm still motivated to score fresh tracks on a powder day. By 8 a.m. the next morning, I was on the complimentary Fairmont shuttle with a throng of stoked skiers for the 10-minute transfer to Lake Louise Ski Resort. With three ways up the front of the mountain, there's rarely a line. And once you're on the mountain, it's easy to spread out. With this season's addition of Richardson's Ridge, 200 acres of beginner- and intermediate-friendly glades and groomers, the resort owns bragging rights as the third biggest ski area in Canada, with 4,200 acres of skiable terrain. Throughout the day I experienced moments that felt more like backcountry than resort riding, especially in the rowdy inbounds steeps of the West Bowl.

Gearing up to snowshoe at Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise

I spent the morning lapping bowl runs off the Summit Chair, but in the afternoon the visibility disappeared, making a drop down black diamond Rodney's Ridge downright vertigo-inducing. I'd gotten my fix of SkiBig3's “Prosecco Powder” (wonderfully light, dry snow), and I won't lie—the other incentive that got me up and out early was the promise of lunch at Whitehorn Bistro. The acclaimed views from this upscale, on-mountain restaurant were obscured, but that didn't damper the deliciousness of its farm-to-table dishes, like pickled beet carpaccio with goat cheese and crispy duck with black cherry jus.

With a solid half day of riding under my belt, I left the resort to work off my lunch on the Nordic trails of the Lake Louise campground with Clare McCann, the high-energy owner of Bikescape. Her lighthearted guiding philosophy was an ideal match for my novice cross-country ski skills and the tricky conditions. Heavy wet snow would cement our skis to the ground the minute we stopped propelling forward. “Shimmy, shimmy, shimmy, glide,” she repeated like a mantra as we haltingly made our way around the four-mile Bow River Loop. The trail was so silent that the only sound was my retro SkiBig3 pass flapping on the side of my jacket.

Those words stuck with me as I drove 50 minutes southeast to the town of Banff. Compared with Lake Louise, it's a bustling tourism hub, but thanks to strict design guidelines, the businesses downtown—even the Starbucks and the McDonald's—are housed in intentionally rustic spaces built from local stone and wood. Over the last few years, new businesses have enlivened the downtown area. Some of these arrivals include locavore-focused restaurants Good Folk and Farm & Fire, and the 67-room boutique Kenrick Hotel, a hip remodel of the former High Country Inn that added a third-wave coffee shop, a thermal circuit spa, and a stellar Italian restaurant, The Fat Ox. Even the classic sticky-floored late-night club, the Dusty Boot Saloon & Dance Hall, has been reinvented as Banff's take on Jackson Hole's Million Dollar Cowboy Bar, complete with a shiny, boot-shaped disco ball and a karaoke room.

I kept things old-school and checked in to Fairmont Banff Springs, nicknamed the Castle in the Rockies for its Scottish-baronial-style architecture and perch overlooking the town. The hotel was another vision of railway executive Van Horne, who famously said, “If we can't export the scenery, we'll import the tourists.”

Today Banff National Park sees more than 4.25 million visitors every year; nearly two thirds come during summer. But a week into my trip, I began to think that winter might be the more enchanting season. On my final day I joined Discover Banff Tours on an excursion to Johnston Canyon for its Icewalk experience. In the parking lot our guide, Jeff Doherty, outfitted our group of six in Yaktrax—ice-gripping cleats that attach to shoes—then had us briskly hike a half mile over slick paths and bridges to the lower falls before the big bus tour groups arrived.

The canyon was pure quietude, and we had it to ourselves. When the pace slowed, I was able to admire the feathery moss suspended in frost on the forest floor, the icy old man's beard lichen dangling from the trees, and, most of all, the frozen ribbons of electric blue cascades. About a mile of slippery uphill trail led us to the upper falls, a stream of water plunging behind a veil of ice. I stood in front of Mother Nature's ice sculpture in awe. “It's like seeing the Mona Lisa alone in the Louvre,” said Doherty. “In summer you'd be elbowing iPhones out of the way for a view, let alone a photo.”

A few weeks after I'd returned home to Colorado, I read that Alberta had been hit by a warm spell that doused the mountains in rain, turning the slopes into sheets of ice. Some friends had taken their family on a ski trip per my recommendation. When I asked about their vacation, they told me they hadn't skied once, but they had the best time dogsledding, night tubing, and ice-skating. In an age when weather is unforgivingly unpredictable, Banff, with its buffet of activities, might be the closest thing to a guaranteed winter escape, no matter your preferred pace of adventure. My trip showed me that winter isn't just about high-voltage powder days at the ski resort. It can be enjoyed at a slower pace. I was even inspired to buy a pair of my own snowshoes.

Direct flights to Calgary International Airport are available from more than a dozen major US hubs. Banff National Park is an easy 90-minute drive west on the Trans-Canada Highway.

Lakefront grande dame Fairmont Chateau Lake Louise offers every imaginable snowy activity, from horse-drawn sleigh rides to curling. In winter an outdoor bar built from 240 blocks of ice serves mulled wine and spiked cocoa. Only hotel guests have access to Basin Glacial Waters, a sprawling thermal bathhouse. Don't miss the excellent hotel bookshop, curated with out-of-print editions on the area's mountaineering history.

One of the park's first hotels, Fairmont Banff Springs, recently added a new wing of larger, family-friendly suites with bunk beds and adjoining rooms. An on-site skating rink has a pop-up sugar shack that serves maple treats, and the adventure center rents fat-tire bikes and arranges guided evening snowshoe tours. Of the 13 dining venues, 1888 Chophouse, known for its Alberta Wagyu, is a standout.

A refreshing boutique alternative to Banff's palatial resorts, the reimagined Kenrick Hotel has an outpost of Calgary's Analog Coffee, a guests-only hydrotherapy spa that stays open until midnight, and one of the town's top tables, Alpine Italian–inspired Fat Ox.

Tucked away on Sulphur Mountain, 320-room resort Rimrock Banff, Emblems Collection debuted a sleek makeover last summer. The first property in Accor's new über-luxe Emblems Collection features a bathhouse of saunas, glacial plunges, and infinity pools, plus a cocktail-forward vinyl listening room and in-house guides who can take guests on glacier treks and ice canyon walks.

Reservations are a must at white-tablecloth Dining Room at the Post Hotel and Spa, known for its 25,000-plus-bottle wine cellar and European-inspired menu of dishes like roasted chateaubriand and Dover sole with saffron beurre blanc.

The house-made sourdough alone makes it worth pausing on a powder day for lunch at ski-in, ski-out restaurant Whitehorn Bistro off the Grizzly Gondola of Lake Louise Ski Resort. On a bluebird day request a patio seat.

The Dusty Boot Saloon and Dance Hall was recently spiffed up and turned into an Old West saloon with a stage for live music and a menu of excellent cocktails, like the Cattleman's Old Fashioned, and elevated bar food, including addictive popcorn chicken and shrimp spiked with wasabi peas.

At Good Folk, hearty breakfast dishes like banana caramel French toast will fuel your day's adventure. In the evening the menu showcases the local bounty in dishes like Alberta wild boar chop and a decadent bison burger topped with smoked cheddar and pepper bacon.

Johnston Canyon, a labyrinth of limestone rocks and cascades, sees a fraction of the visitors in winter than in summer. But to make sure you have the frozen falls to yourself, book the morning Icewalk experience with Discover Banff Tours.

Local outfitter Bikescape runs beginner-friendly Nordic ski tours on the trails within Banff National Park as well as electric fat-tire bike tours that include a backcountry lunch.

Sit back and enjoy the views of Lake Louise from the seat of a horse-drawn sled. Brewsters Lake Louise Stables runs hour-long trips that follow the lake path; evening tours under the stars are extra special.

SkiBig3's resorts offer something for everyone. Family-friendly Mount Norquay has night skiing and a thrilling tubing park. Every chairlift on the front side of Lake Louise Ski Resort has both an easy (green) and challenging (black) way down. Sunshine Mountain Lodge at Banff Sunshine Village has the area's only ski-in, ski-out lodging, and its big alpine views make you feel like you're downhilling in Europe.

For the perfect detour en route to Calgary International Airport, Everwild Canmore, a new Nordic spa at the Everwild hotel in the town of Canmore, features a DIY outdoor thermal circuit as well as five-elements rituals, ranging from an herbal steam cleanse to an Aufguss ceremony. The spa lounge serves a shareable charcuterie and cheese board for two along with cocktails.
