Exclusive: ‘Breaking Bad’ Star RJ Mitte, in His Own Words

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

For those of us who managed to land a role on Breaking Bad, the show is a formative part of our lives. It represented a chance to work on what is now considered one of the best shows TV has ever seen, and the cast and crew forged an unbreakable bond. RJ Mitte was part of the show’s heartbeat. When I spoke to him for this book, we reflected on our days shooting in New Mexico fondly.

I’ll always remember him as a young kid—bright, talented, and a real sweetheart—who was so thrilled to be on set with us that it reminded us how lucky we all were. We'll all miss him dearly. Rest in peace, RJ. —Dean Norris

The last person to join the White family was RJ Mitte, cast in the role of Walt Jr., aka “Flynn.” RJ, who was only fourteen years old at the time, joined the project after we had already begun filming the pilot, following multiple rounds of auditions stretching out over several months. You might wonder why casting Walt Jr., a comparatively minor character among the core cast, took so long. Well, it was about verisimilitude. As written, Walt Jr. had cerebral palsy, albeit a relatively minor case. It was a fundamental part of the character, and Vince was adamant that the role not be played by a nondisabled actor.

VINCE GILLIGAN, creator: I was worried at the beginning that people wouldn’t connect with Walt. They’d say, Why is this guy doing this terrible thing? And so I kind of loaded him up for bear in terms of, you know, he had a son with cerebral palsy. You felt bad for him. People bullied him.

SHARON BIALY, casting director: We had a lot of autonomy with casting, once we knew what Vince was looking for, once we all agreed that, aesthetically, we were talking about the same type of actor. A case in point is RJ Mitte’s role. Vince only wanted actors who had CP. So, we conducted a nationwide search.

RJ MITTE, Walt Jr.: Walt Jr. is actually based on a real person, a friend of Vince’s from college who had cerebral palsy. So, Vince was really particular about the role of Walt Jr. and who was going to play him.

Experienced actors who also have mild CP is not the deepest pool, of course, but there are more candidates than you might expect. And RJ Mitte’s casting has to be considered something of a surprise, given his relative lack of experience and a variety of other challenges he faced while growing up.

RJ MITTE: We were living in Houston when my sister got discovered at a water park, and that’s what brought us to LA. My sister got booked on a job at Universal Studios for an ad campaign. She was one and a half at the time. We really didn’t have a lot going on. Things were tough. My mom was a single mom, and she had recently been partially paralyzed in a car accident, and my grandparents, who I was very close to, were both sick and dying, so when we got this offer to go to LA, we took it. It was a paid trip. They put us up in a motel in Hollywood—there was a strip club on one side, and a Ralphs and a library on the other side. That’s LA.

Everything was covered for six months, so I just started acting to meet kids my own age and have a little extra cash while we were there. And it ended up turning into a career for me. I started working at a young age, not in the acting industry, but with my uncle and family jobs and stuff to make money as a young kid. I would like to think I grew up with a strong work ethic. So, when we got to LA, I hit the ground running. Within the first six months, I was on shows like Hannah Montana, Everybody Hates Chris, Weeds, Seventh Heaven, Drillbit Taylor, Epic Movie—­ all as an extra. I think that really helped prepare me for Breaking Bad, just with the quality of work and the type of professionalism that the set had. I’m very grateful, because if I did not get Breaking Bad, I wouldn’t have an acting career.

At any number of points along the journey, RJ’s dream could have died. It’s hard enough for a known actor to land a role in a show of this quality, but for a newcomer, it must have been daunting, although you never would have known it by the way he carried himself on set.

RJ MITTE: Funny story: My very first audition ever in my career was an anti­ meth campaign for the state of California. It was a wild PSA, and I ended up not getting it. A year or so later, I started auditioning for Breaking Bad. The description of the role literally said, “dark hair, big eyebrows, and mild cerebral palsy.” And I was like, that’s me!

Sharon Bialy called me in, and I already had been on quite a few auditions by that point, and I didn’t book any of them, so I knew what rejection felt like. I had a sense of, Don’t hold your breath. But I’m a firm believer that you go in owning it and when you walk out, don’t look back. Every callback was a little more like, Wait, I’m actually still in the running.

Walt Jr. had a somewhat more severe case of CP than RJ, who did not use assistive devices for mobility in real life.

RJ MITTE: When I went in for my first audition, Sharon was there, and she had hurt her foot. So, she actually handed me her crutches that she was using for the audition. Six months went by, four callbacks, four auditions, and in that final one, they’re like, Okay, we’re sending you. You’re going to New Mexico. The next day I was on a 6 a.m. flight to audition for Vince and the team. Then he excused me for a moment and brought me back in to do my test screening with Anna and Bryan.

Think about that. RJ was fourteen years old and trying to land a job that would change his life, a job that would help him support his entire family. Talk about pressure.

RJ MITTE: I think in life I’ve always had pressure. It really didn’t stand out to me too much. I can only do what I can do. And so as long as I’m doing my best, that’s what matters. But it always kind of felt like I was part of the team to some degree, even when I wasn’t. And so when it came to doing the scene with Anna and Bryan, it really felt like a family. It was like, Oh, yeah, this feels right. Apparently, they must have had the same feeling because they hired me.

Like everyone else, RJ made the most of his opportunity and crafted a fully formed portrait of an adolescent at the center of a family storm; one whose disability may have helped shape him but did not define who he was.

RJ MITTE: This was the first time I think people really saw a character with a disability who wasn’t disabled. There’s nothing he couldn’t do that other kids were doing. It wasn’t about the disability, and that’s something that I think Vince really stressed: it’s not about his disability; it’s about being a part of the family and living with it.

Six seasons in, Breaking Bad reached its apotheosis with the monumental episode “Ozymandias,” in which Walter White’s house of cards comes crashing down. It’s also the episode where Flynn learns the truth about his father’s double life, and that Walt has led my character—his brother-in-law, Hank—to his death. The episode builds to a climactic moment where Flynn’s mother, Skyler, grabs a knife and brandishes it at her husband.

RIAN JOHNSON, director, “Ozymandias”: The actual fight itself, I remember being surprisingly fun to shoot—­just making sure to give Anna and Bryan the space that they needed to find it. I think RJ also did a terrific job in that moment—­ he’s kind of the linchpin of the whole thing.

It is indeed Flynn who rises to the occasion. When Walt wrestles the knife away from Skyler, his son jumps on top of him and throws him across the room. He’s a big kid, strong enough to handle Walt despite his disability; strong enough to protect his mother.

RJ MITTE: It was very intense to shoot. At the same time, we’re professionals. Everything was very choreographed. There was one moment when we first started, I got so heated, and so into it, that I kind of accidentally grabbed Bryan the wrong way and kind of like tossed him . . . wrong. And everyone was like, “Whoa, RJ! You can’t throw him like that.” And I was like, “Oh, I’m so sorry. I just got caught up in the moment.”

RIAN JOHNSON: When you see Walt Jr. turn, then you know it’s truly over. And Bryan’s reaction to that just breaks my heart every time I see it.

In the series’s penultimate episode, Walt clandestinely calls Flynn at school. A package is on its way, he says, to be delivered to the home of one of Flynn’s friends. The package contains money, one hundred thousand dollars—­the most he could stuff into one box. “I wanted to give you so much more, but this is all I could do,” Walt explains, leaning into the pay phone as Flynn listens, wordlessly, on the other end.

RJ MITTE: I remember at the time we were shooting that scene it was in this school-slash-doctor’s-office that we were using. Bryan was in the room with me when we did this part. It definitely was heartbreaking to have to feel that way about someone that I admire. And I think that’s what Junior felt as well. I’m a believer that in life, there’s always a moment where you have to choose to either be an adult or to stay a child. This moment comes in many different forms, but it comes down to that moment where you say, Am I going to accept this and give them what they want and be submissive, and still have that child aspect of who I am? Or am I going to take charge and become the person that I need to be—­ the man, the adult?

RJ does some terrific wordless acting in this scene, wrestling with this decision. Then he speaks: “Just shut up! Just stop it. Stop it. I don’t want anything from you.”

“You need this money,” Walt says. “Your mother needs this money. It can’t all be for nothing.”

Yes, it can, as Flynn makes abundantly clear.

RJ MITTE: A lot of the decisions I made for Junior were based on my own experiences as a young adult. At the time I was still a child, but I utilized those moments from my own life of having to stand my ground and make that decision—­ realizing who I am and what I need to do to take care and provide for my family. In the show, Walt was no longer doing that. He was actually creating more danger for his family.

I think it’s very powerful that Walt Jr. had the ability to stand his ground, and to put those boundaries in place: “You’re out. This is my family now, and you’re not a part of it.” I find a lot of young men and women have to take that stance at some point, especially with parents or family figures that live in a volatile world, a world that is corrupt.

Later, in the series finale, Walt is standing over a crib, caressing the hair of baby Holly as she naps. He leaves and then watches from a distance as Flynn gets off a school bus and walks into the apartment.

VINCE GILLIGAN: With Walt, you want him to have a goodbye scene with his wife. We were sad we couldn’t have a goodbye scene with his son, Walt Jr., because RJ is a wonderful actor. We just didn’t have the room.

For those of us who were fortunate enough to be a part of it, Breaking Bad remains a seminal event in our lives, both personally and professionally—­ an opportunity to work on one of the greatest television shows in history, alongside people who became lifelong friends.

BRYAN CRANSTON: I knew when it ended that this was the role of my life.

AARON PAUL: I think I can speak for everyone involved in the show: It changed all of our lives.

RJ MITTE: I will be eighty years old in a nursing home and there will probably be someone looking at me through a window, saying, “There’s Walt Jr.” And I’m okay with that.

Rocco’s Mini Mini Fridge Is Here (2026)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you've always coveted Rocco’s vintage-inspired beverage fridges but haven't been able to swing the $1,999-plus price tag, here's some news you might like: Today, the brand released the Mini Mini, a desktop version of its legendary mini fridge. It comes in Green, Graphite, Cream, and new Stainless Steel. (Only Stainless Steel is available right now.)

We haven't tested it yet or even seen it in person, but Rocco says the Mini Mini can hold 12 standard 12-ounce cans and gets down to 41 degrees Fahrenheit. If you've ever tried other fridges of this size, you'll know this is an impressive stat. (Sorry, mini Frigidaire—you're cute but warm.) Rocco calls the Mini Mini “the first countertop fridge with real fridge standards.” It's got 360-degree ventilation with the refrigeration mechanics underneath the unit. That means it can sit flush against a wall, wherever that might be.

The only note is that, unlike the larger fridges, like the Classic and the Essential, this Rocco does not have a smart-beverage recognition feature—it's just for looks and performance only.

It's available for $295, and while it’s not currently on sale, unlike the brand's larger fridges, you can get it for $50 off when you buy a full-sized Rocco Classic or Essential.

4DX Movies Are Entering Their Thirsty Romance Era

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Following the temporary collapse of the fan fiction website A03, extremely horny millennial women are on the hunt for other ways to get their rocks off—and Regal Cinemas execs are more than happy to help.

On Tuesday, the movie theater chain announced that for one night only, on November 16, it will be screening 4DX versions of episodes 5 and 6 of Heated Rivalry, the smash-hit gay hockey show adapted from the bestselling novels by Rachel Reid that first aired on the Canadian network Crave but blasted into the mainstream when it came to HBO Max. The screenings will be at 59 theaters across the US, as well as a handful of theaters in Canada and the United Kingdom.

The screenings are intended to give fans the opportunity “to go to the cottage and really experience” Hollanov all over again, says Jack Grimes, senior director of alternative business development at Regal.

The 4DX screenings are upping the ante for the fan-favorite Heated Rivalry screenings, which have been selling out in movie theaters across the US for the past month. Though the series is widely available to stream, fans have flocked to theaters to shout along to t.a.T.u’s “All the Things She Said” during the club scene in episode four and post photos of the Heated Rivalry–themed popcorn buckets featuring stars Connor Storrie and Hudson Williams in a sweaty, shirtless embrace. Thus far, such public displays of down bad-ness have been incredibly lucrative: as of November 8, Regal has earned $1.4 million from its limited theatrical release, per Variety’s reporting.

Still, Heated Rivalry isn’t an obvious candidate for a 4DX release. Originating in South Korea in 2009, 4DX technology provides an immersive experience for the viewer, such as motion-enabled chairs; environmental effects like wind, snow, and rain; or even scents, such as the smell of gunpowder or explosives during battle scenes. It’s mostly been used for big-budget action-adventure movies and thrillers such as The Odyssey and Avatar: The Way of Water.

Recently, however, there’s been increasing demand for 4DX big-screen releases for not just conventional action movies but also straightforward dramas, comedies, and even romances. Put another way, there’s evidence that a not-insubstantial number of the filmgoing public would like nothing more than a two-hour license to hump their seats.

For instance, as the result of what was essentially an extremely online in-joke, last month the film distributor Black Bear hosted 4DX screenings of the romantic black comedy Wicker, starring Olivia Colman as a woman who falls in love with a straw-plaited man (Alexander Skarsgaard). The screening featured such immersive special effects as water splashing in the audience’s faces when Colman’s character empties out her chamber pot, as well as synchronized motion-activated “thrusts” in the audience’s seats during sex scenes.

Grimes tells WIRED that the idea to produce 4DX Heated Rivalry screenings was in part inspired by the intense social media response to Wicker.

“We think the demographic overlap is really significant between the two,” he says. “They’re both very female-driven, in terms of what the viewership looks like.” The sales numbers for the Heated Rivalry screenings “emphasized that we needed to bring this to life,” Grimes says.

In general, filmmakers are increasingly becoming more creative about how they incorporate 4DX effects into their work. According to a Vulture interview with Jaehyun Jeong and Jaesang Yoon, two producers for the special effects company CJ 4DPlex, Paul Thomas Anderson specifically requested “sexual movements” for a scene in One Battle After Another in which Sean Penn’s character masturbates.

When asked about which specific effects will be used for Heated Rivalry, Grimes says CJ 4DPlex is in the process of coding the 4DX version, so he couldn’t say for sure. But he says it will likely “lean a lot into the motion effects,” albeit “where it makes sense to do so and where it's going to be tasteful to do so.”

Does that mean the chairs will thrust during sex scenes, as some randy fans have speculated? “I mean, it's, it's something that we saw in Wicker, so I wouldn't say that it's out of a stretch of imagination to say it's going to happen here,” Grimes says.

Unfortunately, the effects will perhaps not be quite as explicit as they were in Wicker, as it’s worth noting that episodes 5 and 6 are significantly less graphic than those earlier in the season. But it’s still tempting to consider how, exactly, Regal plans to incorporate special effects into the screening. Chirping crickets during the cottage scenes? Ice chips flying around when Kip rushes down to the rink to make out with Scott to the adrenaline-pumping percussive strains of “I’ll Believe in Anything”? An unidentified substance spraying in the audience’s faces when Ilya finishes off Shane while he’s on a call? The mind reels.

This New Linux-Based Desktop Operating System Is Built for AI Nerds

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Kirt McMaster knows that his new operating system—Kora—won’t be for everyone, and he’s fine with that.

Kora, announced on Tuesday, is designed as a desktop alternative to Windows or macOS for “the AI native, not mass market,” he says. McMaster, the company’s CEO and cofounder—who also cofounded Boost Mobile—likens his strategy to how Steve Jobs targeted creators when overhauling desktop publishing with the Mac.

This “next-generation” desktop OS can integrate context from user interactions across multiple AI tools. Targeted to creators and developers, Kora’s central idea is to bring AI models together at the system level and help power users interact more seamlessly with multiple providers, rather than siloing everything off into desktop apps and browser tabs. It’s scheduled to launch sometime in 2027.

By tapping into your data and context at a machine level, Kora could streamline some of the current speed bumps of using AI tools on a desktop. “All your context, all your memory is on your machine,” says Franck De Girolami, one of Kora’s cofounders. He gives the example of someone designing a project with an OpenAI model inside Kora's interface, switching to Anthropic’s tools to deploy code, and leaning on a local model to save money on AI tokens.

Kora is a standalone operating system built on the Linux kernel that runs directly on hardware. The startup has partnered with Qualcomm to bring Kora to Snapdragon PCs next year, but it also supports x86-64 architecture, so you can likely install it on current Intel- or AMD-based PCs.

To bridge the software gap, built-in compatibility layers will allow users to run standard Windows apps. The company is also developing its own native apps for basics like contacts, calendar, and email, alongside a software development kit (SDK) for third-party developers. Along the bottom of the home screen is a dock, as you would expect on a desktop OS. This is where users can switch between email, web browsing, and launching games.

The OS is designed around what the startup calls a "neural file system.” Based on example screenshots, the user experience may feel like messaging a chatbot or AI agent. File data is indexed by meaning so you can ask questions about the broad context of files by typing into an OS search bar, without necessarily looking up specific file names. For example, you could search for a business contract saved somewhere on your device based on a small detail you remember.

Kora grew out of McMaster’s startup Playtron, which launched its own gaming OS, GameOS, last year. GameOS is primarily built to run on portable handhelds, like a Steam Deck, with PC support as well. That architecture now lives inside Kora as GameFrame, letting users run PC games—provided their hardware can keep up.

The broader pitch caters to PC gamers and power users. “That's always our thing—next-generation creators who are using these tools daily to build new applications and services, but they want everything else that they can get from a Windows PC or what's currently available on macOS,” McMaster says.

Taking on the giants is difficult, especially with a team of under 100 people, but Kora’s leadership knows a thing or two about launching operating systems. Kora’s three founders—McMaster, De Girolami, and John Lagerling—each bring their own expertise. McMaster cofounded and helped lead Cyanogen, an open source Android fork that gained millions of users before shutting down around a decade ago. De Girolami helped develop Playtron’s GameOS. Lagerling previously worked on Android at Google.

Kora isn’t the first to rethink the desktop operating system in the AI age. Google launched its Googlebook OS and compatible laptops in October, designed around Gemini AI. Even so, McMaster doesn’t see that OS and its Android support as satisfying power users who want to run AI models that require serious chip power. The company is optimizing Kora for desktop configurations with at least 64 gigabytes of RAM, which is twice the memory capacity of the top Googlebook.

“We can evolve much faster than macOS and Windows,” McMaster says. “When you're a billion-user incumbent, it's impossible to tip the boat, man. It's just really hard to make big changes. Over time, there's this event horizon where building a new kind of OS for intelligence can become something very compelling.”

Julia Stoschek Collection to Celebrate 20 Years with ‘Selfish in Bed’ Exhibition and Düsseldorf Reopening

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Sarah Lucas doesn’t appear particularly apologetic in her 2000 self-portrait Selfish in Bed II. Standing with her hands on her hips, the British artist wears a black T-shirt with the words “SELFISH IN BED” printed across the front. Next April, that cheeky photograph will set the tone for a major group exhibition celebrating the 20th anniversary of the Julia Stoschek Collection in Düsseldorf, Germany.

Aptly titled Selfish in Bed, the exhibition opens on April 10, 2027, and will bring together 52 works by 43 artists, including Tracey Emin, Bill Viola, Wolfgang Tillmans, and Bruce Nauman. Selfish in Bed marks the inaugural exhibition of the reopened Julia Stoschek Foundation’s Düsseldorf headquarters, which closed for renovations in April 2025. Related Articles This LA Exhibition of Julia Stoschek's Video Art Collection Is the City's Hottest Ticket  Sarah Lucas Wins New Museum's $400,000 Sculpture Prize

Curated by Krist Gruijthuijsen, the director of Finland’s EMMA – Espoo Museum of Modern Art and former director of Berlin’s KW Institute for Contemporary Art, the show looks at how we construct our identities, express our sexuality, and present ourselves to the world.

The exhibition takes the bed as its starting point. It’s a place increasingly of contradictions: It’s private and vulnerable, yet inter-connected, where we spend hours scrolling through the images and opinions that shape how we see ourselves. The show explores that tension through works spanning the late 1960s to the present, including several that the foundation has never exhibited before.

There are more than a few highlights. Emin’s Why I Never Became a Dancer (1995), Simon Fujiwara‘s Joanne (2016/2018), and Hannah Wilke’s Through the Large Glass (1976), all explore women’s relationships with their bodies, sexuality, and public image. Meanwhile Monica Bonvicini‘s Destroy She Said (1998) challenges traditional domestic roles, and Ed Atkins’s Us Dead Talk Love (2012) examines questions of identity and authenticity in the digital age.

The exhibition is set to span two floors, and include video projections, mirrored surfaces, glass walls, and television monitors creating an immersive environment. Works by Viola, Tillmans, and Rosemarie Trockel will be presented together in a grouping that explores the human experience from birth onward.

For Stoschek, whose collection now includes more than 1,000 works by over 300 artists, the anniversary is also an opportunity to reflect on two decades of collecting.

“I am a very emotional collector,” Stoschek said in a statement. “An artwork has to get under my skin. I need images that stay with me.”

The Düsseldorf headquarters, housed in a converted industrial building dating to 1907, has undergone an extensive renovation by Berlin architecture firm Kuehn Malvezzi, which originally adapted the space for exhibitions in 2007. In Berlin, the foundation closed its exhibition space at the end of October after a decade of programming, shifting its attention toward Düsseldorf and international projects.

A publication marking the collection’s 20th anniversary will accompany the exhibition, with contributions from writers and critics including Rachel Cusk, Chris Kraus, Dean Kissick, and McKenzie Wark.

Selfish in Bed will run from April 10 through December 19, 2027, with an opening reception on April 9.

Nov. 10, 1861: The birth of Robert Innes

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Born Nov. 10, 1861, in Scotland, Robert Thorburn Ayton Innes would reshape our understanding of our cosmic neighborhood when he discovered our nearest stellar neighbor, Proxima Centauri, in 1912. Innes suspected that Alpha Centauri, a binary system made up of Alpha Centauri A (Rigil Kentaurus) and Alpha Centauri B (Toliman) and then thought to be the nearest stars to Earth (besides the Sun) – had a third member.

The director of the Union Observatory in Johannesburg, South Africa, Innes began a systematic survey for the missing companion star, comparing five years’ worth of photographic plates using a blink-microscope. He noted that a small, faint, red star seemed to move in concert with Alpha Centauri; further research revealed it was in fact closer to Earth than its companions. Innes announced the find in 1915, and later named the body Proxima Centauri – proxima meaning “nearest” in Latin.

Elisa Neckar is senior production editor of Astronomy magazine, and the editor of the Today in the History of Astronomy feature on Astronomy.com.

Circle Makes the Case for AI Agent Payments, With Spending Controls at the Center

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Circle is arguing that stablecoins could become a useful payment layer for AI agents that buy software, access services or settle transactions on behalf of people and businesses. A November 4 explainer from the USDC issuer sets out what it calls agentic commerce, a concept that would allow authorized software to initiate purchases within predefined rules.

Circle is arguing that stablecoins could become a useful payment layer for AI agents that buy software, access services or settle transactions on behalf of people and businesses. A November 4 explainer from the USDC issuer sets out what it calls agentic commerce, a concept that would allow authorized software to initiate purchases within predefined rules. It is a vision for a developing market, not evidence that an autonomous AI payments network has already reached mass adoption.

The basic idea is to let a software agent complete a purchase after receiving instructions and permission from a human or business. An agent might compare pricing, obtain a computing service, pay a provider and record the transaction without asking a person to click through each step. This differs from a chatbot that only recommends products or from an automatic recurring subscription. Stablecoins are appealing in this setting because value can move over internet-native rails and settle across borders, but those features are useful only when an agent can prove what it is authorized to do.

The central design challenge is delegated authority. A business needs to specify which agent may spend, how much, on which categories of supplier and for what period. It also needs authentication, records of the instruction that triggered a payment and a way to revoke authority. Without those safeguards, automated payments can turn minor software errors or malicious prompts into real financial losses. Circle's discussion points to the infrastructure opportunity, but a stablecoin transfer alone cannot determine whether the underlying purchase was legitimate or met the user's expectations.

USDC and other dollar-linked tokens could allow small cross-border payments and programmable transfers that are difficult to reproduce with traditional card infrastructure. However, blockchain transfers can be difficult to reverse. Conventional card payments often involve dispute rights, fraud monitoring and customer-support processes that do not automatically accompany an onchain transfer. A workable agent payment service therefore needs explicit handling for refunds, incorrect goods, failed delivery and disputes. It must also address sanctions checks, identity requirements and jurisdictional limits where relevant. Technology that moves money quickly cannot replace those business obligations.

Developers are experimenting with AI systems that interact with tools, APIs and online marketplaces. As those systems gain the ability to perform tasks rather than simply produce text, a reliable method of paying for digital services becomes more relevant. Stablecoin issuers and payment processors see potential demand, but practical uptake depends on merchants accepting the method and users trusting the controls. The most credible demonstrations will involve narrow, verifiable use cases rather than broad claims that intelligent agents can safely manage unlimited funds.

Payment providers will need to show who bears liability when an agent makes a mistaken purchase, how permissions are audited and how customers recover from fraud. They will also need to show cost advantages after fees, compliance and integration work are included. Circle's essay is best read as an articulation of a developing architecture, not a new product launch. The long-term test is whether automated payments become easier for ordinary businesses without making financial control harder.

Primary source: Circle analysis of agentic commerce. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s report on a consumer-facing USDC payment integration.

Mini Review: Pikmin 4 - Nintendo Switch 2 Edition + Dandori Academy (Switch 2) - Pleasant But Perplexingly Piecemeal

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The latest addition to the pantheon of obscenely long-titled Nintendo Switch 2 Editions is a strange one. Pikmin 4 - Nintendo Switch 2 Edition + Dandori Academy takes the Switch 1 game that I have long considered ripe for a new hardware touch-up and does...not a lot with it. It's a chance to revisit one of Nintendo's finest games from the last generation, and the upgrade's price of admission (£7.99 / $9.99) is admittedly a small one, but on the 'new' front, I'm left wanting more.

Let's start with the usual 'Switch 2 Edition' bells and whistles. Pikmin 4 now plays in stunning native 4K resolution docked, and there's added GameShare support for those who want to play some cross-console Dandori Battles with a pal.

If you ask me, Pikmin 4 was already one of the best-looking titles on Switch 1, and the resolution bump doesn't feel transformative. That said, there's no denying that it's even more of a looker on the newer hardware.

Review Pikmin 4 (Switch) - Relaxed, Refined Real-Time Strategy, Ready To Crack The Mainstream

Strangely, the typical Switch 2 givens of a 60fps boost and Mouse Mode are not included this time around. While the former is disappointing, the latter downright confuses me. Nintendo has crowbarred this control scheme in everywhere, but it's absent from a game with a stick-controlled pointer? Even in the co-op Pebble Pitcher mode? Madness!

There's also a new Howl-Out feature that lets you shout commands to Oatchi via the Switch 2 microphone. It's a sweet, if occasionally inconsistent, little bonus that I quickly turned off after realising my real-world dog was getting miffed at me shouting "Good Pup!" without giving him a stroke on the head.

The big newbie is the titular Dandori Academy, where you're tasked with clearing a stage's treasure as quickly as possible. You practice during the week, then take a weekend 'exam', unlocking various medals and in-game rewards in the process. The Dandori stages were a highlight of the base game, and it's great to have even more of them to play here. The three difficulty options provide a welcome challenge in the later stages, and with 72 of them arriving over the next year, it's a wonderful excuse to return to one of my favourite Switch 1 games.

The mode's rollout, however, is a little confusing. 72 new stages sound great on paper, but that means only six new challenges are available each month. If you happen to ace most of them on the first exam weekend, you're left with three weeks of nothing new. Sure, it gives me a prime reason to come back to Pikmin 4 on the regular in 2027, but it does leave things feeling rather slight at launch.

Overall, £8/$10 is a small price to pay for some minor new features and a fun little mode that'll keep me entertained over the coming year, but it's more of a budding shoot right now than the full-bloom upgrade I had dreamed of.

Here's What Xbox Owners Need To Know About 'Moving To New Zealand' For GTA 6

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

So, it's finally almost time for GTA 6. You've probably preloaded the game on your Xbox Series X and Xbox Series S already, and now you're eagerly awaiting the official launch that takes place on November 19th.

However, there are more than a few whispers going around about the "New Zealand" trick — something we've seen time and time again over the past decade-plus on Xbox — and it looks set to have a big influence again with GTA 6.

But what is it, and most importantly could it get you banned? Let's take a look at the facts:

On this page: Here's What Xbox Owners Need To Know About 'Moving To New Zealand' For GTA 6

A game like GTA 6 obviously has a set release date of November 19th, but it lands at "midnight local time" depending on the region you're in. That means New Zealand ends up being the first region to get access, and their "midnight" corresponds to 3am PT / 6am ET / 11am GMT on November 18th.

Therefore, if people want to play the game as early as possible, they'll need to be in New Zealand - and the "New Zealand Trick" refers to changing your region in your console's settings, providing slightly early access to GTA 6.

There's always been a lot of chatter online about how Xbox doesn't really care about you "moving to New Zealand" to get around release times, but that's technically not the case. Xbox's terms and conditions specifically state that trying to "circumvent regional content restrictions" is prohibited, and this is mentioned a few times in documentation:

"Attempting to circumvent restrictions can affect both customer privacy and the integrity of Microsoft or partner services" [...] "Make sure that you're only changing your country/region if: You've moved to a different country. You're already in a region different from the one that was set for your Microsoft account and/or XBOX console."

The official answer is yes - Xbox could take whatever kind of actions they wanted against an account that was found breaking the rules to play GTA 6 early on Xbox Series X and S. It could result in a warning, a suspension, a ban, or anything else they see fit.

You might also get away with it, as have the majority of people with many other games in the past, but it's still something you should only do at your own risk. We don't recommend breaking the rules just to play a game a few hours early, but we're also not naive enough to think that people won't be trying it come November 18th!

Something to also keep in mind with this is that spoilers will likely begin hitting the web from the New Zealand release time on November 18th (if not earlier). At that point, we expect all kinds of footage and screenshots to begin flooding social media, so you may want to have a bit of a digital detox until the 19th if you don't want to see anything.

Anyway, we know this is a hot topic, and feel free to share your thoughts about it in the comments below. The decision is up to you - but just know that you are running a risk by "moving to New Zealand", however small it might appear to be.

Trisha Paytas Is Finally Opening Up About the ‘Frenemies’ Split With Ethan Klein

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It’s been a rough few weeks for Ethan Klein. The cohost of The H3 Podcast made headlines in October after his wife and podcast co-host Hila Klein dropped bombshell news accusing him of having a six-month-long affair. “I am living in a nightmare I can’t wake up from,” she wrote on Instagram. “My husband has been having an affair. I have been fed lie after lie after lie for months. I am tired of lies.”

Now, the release of a memoir from his other former podcast cohost, Trisha Paytas, probably isn’t going to help.

Out today, Crying on the Kitchen Floor is a sprawling memoir, starting with Paytas’ birth in Riverside, California, and encompassing her early attempts at YouTube virality, sex work, and multi-faceted career as an online troll. But it’s also her first no-holds-barred recollection of the fallout of her podcast Frenemies, which she made with now-ousted H3 host Klein. (Paytas is married to Moses Hacmon — Hila’s brother — a marriage that complicated her already complex relationships with Klein.) In the book, Paytas says the Frenemies split started as a miscommunication and led to Klein waging all-out war against her — all in an effort to make more money. (Klein did not respond to Rolling Stone’s request for comment.)

After the allegations of infidelity broke online, the crew of The H3 Podcast released a statement saying they were “shocked, angry, and heartbroken,” and announced they would no longer work with Klein, placing the podcast on an indefinite hiatus. Klein, 41, did not outright confirm the allegation but announced he would “remove [his] ownership” from H3 and all its associated podcasts and projects. “I know a lot of people are understandably looking for a comment from me, but I ask that you please respect my family’s privacy and do not contact them looking for me or asking for their comments,” Klein said. “They have nothing to do with this and share in your outrage and disappointment.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Timing is everything. When the news first dropped, Paytas said she was “relieved” that H3 was finally over. But in Crying on the Kitchen Floor, Paytas gives her unbarred thoughts about her initial thoughts of Klein and why her own podcast with him came to an abrupt end.

“I’d believed that, no matter what superficial conflicts Ethan and I got into, at the end of the day, we were genuinely friends. I cared for him. I thought he cared for me too,” Paytas writes. “Maybe I should have known that as soon as I was no longer useful, Ethan would drop me. What I never expected was that he and Hila would try to destroy me.”

Paytas first appeared on H3 in 2019 as a guest. At the time, she was a legacy fixture on YouTube, but had struggled to solidify a steady following. Mostly, she used her social media accounts to troll other internet celebrities, discuss her work as a stripper and a sex worker, and promote her OnlyFans. In a past episode, Klein had dismissed her content as annoying, but fans responded well to her appearance. Paytas felt good about the invite, but less than impressed with their setup.

“Though the set itself was giving Howard Stern chic, the rest of the office felt very haphazard and amateurish, like a bunch of dudes were just pretending to run a media company. Which, as it turned out, wasn’t far from the truth,” she writes. “Ethan and I weren’t going to be best friends, but I recognized him as a kind of kindred spirit. At this core, he was a troll. He stirred the pot.”

His team contacted her again when they wanted to make a new podcast with a female co-host. In September 2020, Frenemies was born. It was a weekly podcast where Paytas and Klein could talk rapid-fire about any cultural topic that came to mind, but mostly riffed on their often-contentious relationship. When Paytas signed onto the show,  she agreed to a 55-45  revenue split on the main episodes and sponsors, allowing Klein to keep all the revenue on the H3 highlights channel. At the height of the series, Paytas writes that she was making $180,000 a month. But the highlights channel was making almost four times the podcast revenue — and Paytas claims she didn’t see a dollar.  Related Content Trisha Paytas Admits Feeling ‘Relief’ Over Ethan Klein’s ‘H3’ Exit After Cheating Scandal Ethan Klein Confirms He'll ‘Remove Ownership’ From ‘H3 Podcast’ After Wife Accuses Him of Cheating Hillary Clinton to Break Down the ‘High Stakes’ of the 2026 Midterms on New Podcast Hunter Biden Talks Crack, Family, and the Owl That Saved His Life

Things came to a head nine months into the show, when the two filmed the 39th episode. In the now infamous recording — which is still up on YouTube — Paytas and Klein start their usual sparring but it got out of hand. Paytas asked to stop filming, verging on tears, and accused Klein of “gaslighting” her over money.

In Crying on the Kitchen Floor, Paytas writes that she left the recording feeling overwhelmed, but assumed that she and Klein would work it out. When she posted on her Instagram that she was “stepping away” from Frenemies, she says, it was a joke. But she was shocked when Klein went live and blamed Paytas for the show’s problems — including a claim that she had attempted to get the crew fired, which she writes is entirely false. Ethan’s videos started a tidal wave of harassment against Paytas, which, she writes, triggered a mental health crisis that left her despondent and spiraling. But when the two managed to film a final episode of Frenemies, one Paytas describes as a “beautiful experience”; Klein refused to release it. Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Trump Finally Gets Around to Claiming the Midterms Were Rigged

“Even though Ethan and I had been attacking each other like rabid dogs for the better part of three weeks, when we sat down for one final rodeo in the Frenemies studio, we were able to talk more as friends than enemies,” she writes. “After that last closure episode, we’d been so close to being okay. We’d squashed the beef but for some reason, a few days after recording, Ethan said he wasn’t going to release it. It seemed more likely to me that the real reason Ethan didn’t want to release the episode was because he realized keeping the beef going was better for business.”

Paytas writes that after recovering from the split, she’s now focused on living a better, less reactive life, for her husband and three children. “Ethan could only affect me if I let him,” she writes. “And goddamnit, I couldn’t give him that power anymore. Not if I wanted this life to be my own.”

Fremantle’s Wildstar & Filmmaker Dan O’Neill Team On Wildlife Venture

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: A natural history filmmaker and Fremantle label Wildstar have partnered on a venture in which travellers will head out on excursions while having their journey documented by a crew.

Dan O’Neill and luxury travel specialist Steppes Travel have launched Wildstar Expeditions with Fremantle’s Wildstar Films, which makes the likes of the Ryan Reynolds-narrated Underdogs for Nat Geo and Disney+.

Wildstar Expeditions will take travellers “seeking exceptional experiences” on a journey with O’Neill and a Wildstar film crew. The crew will document every stage of the trip, making a film of the experience that the guests can then watch afterwards. Related Stories Acquisitions Skydance-Owned 5 Buys UK Rights To 'Wentworth' Sequel Series Executives Fremantle Global Entertainment Exec Chris O'Dell Retires After 19 Years At 'Got Talent' Maker

O’Neill said: “What excites me about Wildstar Expeditions is being able to take people to places most people simply can’t access, using the same expedition planning and field logistics that make serious research and filming possible in difficult terrain. This is a completely new concept, and there’s something so exciting about inviting people to be part of it from the very start, experiencing that access for the first time in this way.” Watch on Deadline

Wildstar COO Rebecca Wootton added: “This is more than a luxury holiday. We’re offering guests the rare opportunity to step behind the scenes of wildlife filmmaking, travelling alongside our award-winning film crews and expert guide Dan O’Neill. They’ll discover how some of the world’s most celebrated natural history programmes are made, gain unique insights from leading filmmakers and conservationists, and experience extraordinary wildlife encounters in places few people will ever visit.

The Team, Formerly Wasserman, Unveils New Board

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

LA-based global sports, music and entertainment company The Team, formerly Wasserman, has launched a new board to oversee strategy and lead its next phase of growth.

The group comprises multiple members of current leadership including Chief Executive Michael Watts; Elizabeth Lindsey, Global President, Brands and Properties; Jason Ranne, President, Global Talent Representation; Kevin Shivers, Co-President, Music; Matthew Vandrau, President, EMEA & APAC; Elena Rochelli, Chief Financial Officer; and Jon Liebman, Chairman and CEO of Brillstein Entertainment Partners. Related Stories News Wasserman Buyout Moves Forward With Investment From Providence Acquisitions Wasserman Rebrand Full Steam Ahead As Formal Bidding For Embattled LA28 Chief's Empire Begins

Each will continue serving their leadership role within their respective business units.

They are joined by Davis Noell, Senior Managing Director, Co-Head of North America; Greg Brown, Operating Partner; Scott Marimow, Managing Director; and Ahana Maken, Senior Vice President at Providence Equity Partners. Watch on Deadline

Providence, the company’s majority owner, will name additional outside board members in the coming months.

Wasserman formally stepped down from his namesake management company last summer when he sold his stake in the business to Providence Equity, adding to its 60% holding. It was rebranded The Team. The deal followed an auction process that saw a number of Hollywood players kick the tires from WME, CAA, Range Media Partners, UTA, Goldman Sachs and a smattering of other private equity firms.

“This is an important moment for our company,” said CEO Watts. “The members of this board have shown the qualities we value most: embracing our culture, supporting their colleagues and delivering exceptional results for our clients. This group will build on our momentum and help us accelerate growth by focusing on the ways we can best support our clients’ goals and aspirations.”

“The Team has an incredibly talented leadership team, and we welcome the expertise and perspective that this board will bring to the business,” said Noell on behalf of Providence. “We’re excited about the growth opportunities ahead and will continue to provide capital and strategic partnership to help expand The Team’s capabilities across sports, music and entertainment.”

The company provides representation and marketing services to talent, brands and properties in sports music and entertainment across 28 countries and 70+ cities worldwide. Its sale process was precipitated after files related to Jeffrey Epstein revealed flirtatious emails Wasserman sent in the early 2000s to close Epstein associate Ghislaine Maxwell.

Nicolo Tresoldi could have already been an AC Milan player now, given that he was very close to signing a few years ago.

Tresoldi has found himself in the Milan orbit for a number of reasons in recent weeks. His continued declarations of love for the Rossoneri – his boyhood team – already had some fans clamouring to try and sign him, and that was before his Champions League hat-trick against Inter at San Siro.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

In the past few days, the speculation has ramped up regarding an attempt by Milan. Not put off by the painful negotiations to sign Charles De Ketelaere and Ardon Jashari, it seems Gerry Cardinale wants to try and sign yet another Brugge star.

Matteo Moretto spoke during a video on Fabrizio Romano’s YouTube channel, and he explained that the young striker was even close to signing for Milan previously, while waiting to see what the future holds.

“In the summer of 2021, Tresoldi arrived at Casa Milan because he had every intention of signing for Milan. Milan had already closed on him,” he said (via MilanNews).

“At the time, the Maldini-Massara duo were the ones at the helm. The deal was done, all that was missing was the signatures. Tresoldi didn’t sign that day: he returned to Germany, and Milan never closed the deal.

“In the summer of 2021, Milan came close to signing Tresoldi, but he then extended his contract with Hannover. We’ll see if he can become a popular option for the Rossoneri in the future.”

McTominay: ‘Napoli gave me the foundation to focus, and I have to thank Conte’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scott McTominay has thanked Antonio Conte for his role in his success at Napoli and described the club’s Scudetto-winning night as the best moment of his career.

According to Rivista Undici, via IlNapolista, the Scotland international has settled quickly into life in Italy since joining from Manchester United in 2024. He won the Scudetto and the Supercoppa Italiana under Conte.

“Napoli and Italy have given me the foundation I needed to focus on my work. Of course, I also have to thank Antonio Conte, who helped me a great deal. I had a very good relationship with him and the rest of the club from day one,” McTominay said. “We’re all a family: all the coaches, the kit men, the physios, the doctors.”

McTominay scored in the 2-0 win over Cagliari that clinched Napoli’s fourth league title in 2025, and the memory still gives him goosebumps.

“There was tension in the air, but when I think back to that game… I still get shivers today. Living through moments like that makes you want to achieve even more, because those are the ones you remember most fondly. It was without doubt one of the best days of my career. In fact, it was the best of all. I want to experience another night like that.”

The midfielder has become a hero for Napoli supporters, but he plays down the label.

“People shouldn’t see me that way. They should see me as someone who works hard, and that has nothing to do with football or what you achieve on the pitch. My aim is to come in, work, go home and rest.

“I think that throughout my career I’ve always risen to the occasion in big games, in the most important moments. That kind of pressure excites me.”

He also credited his father for his work ethic.

“If you don’t treat your career almost like an obsession, you’ll never reach the top level. What counts is what you do every day when you arrive at the training ground, and how you do things away from the pitch.”

The Team Unveils New Board as Firm Regroups After Rebrand From Wasserman

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Team has unveiled an 11-member board to guide the sports, music and entertainment agency that was rebranded in July following the sale of Casey Wasserman’s stake in his marketing and representation firm.

Providence Equity Partners acquired Casey Wasserman’s 40% interest in the firm that he founded as Wasserman Media Group in 2002. The private equity firm purchased a majority stake in Wasserman in 2022. The firm went through a rough patch earlier this year amid the pressure that descended on Casey Wasserman with the uproar over his years-old association with the disgraced and now-convicted Jeffrey Epstein associate Ghislaine Maxwell. Related Stories Will Elon Musk Help the Ellisons Pay Down Skydance's Massive Debt? John Malone Predicts Tech Mogul's SpaceX Could Invest up to $10 Billion Into Media Company ‘Musk’ Doc Gets International Release Dates

The Team relaunch was unveiled in March. The newly named board members are: Michael Watts, CEO of The Team; Elizabeth Lindsey, global president of brands and properties; Jason Ranne , president of global talent representation; Kevin Shivers, co-president of music; Matthew Vandrau, president of EMEA & APAC; Elena Rochelli, chief financial officer; and Jon Liebman, chairman and CEO of Brillstein Entertainment Partners. Brillstein operates autonomously under the Team banner. Each board member will continue to serve in their leadership role within their business units.

Board members representing Providence Equity are: Davis Noell, who is senior managing director and co-head of North America for the private equity firm; Greg Brown, operating partner; Scott Marimow, managing director; and Ahana Maken, senior VP. Providence will appoint additional external board members in the coming months, the company said.

“This is an important moment for our company,” Watts said. “The members of this board have shown the qualities we value most: embracing our culture, supporting their colleagues and delivering exceptional results for our clients. This group will build on our momentum and help us accelerate growth by focusing on the ways we can best support our clients’ goals and aspirations.”

The new configuration encompasses an “incredibly talented leadership team, and we welcome the expertise and perspective that this board will bring to the business,” Noell said. “We’re excited about the growth opportunities ahead and will continue to provide capital and strategic partnership to help expand The Team’s capabilities across sports, music and entertainment.”

“The Later Daters” is back with Season 2, and this time it’s celebrities seeking love later in life.

The Netflix reality series, executive produced by Michelle Obama, has been renewed for another season, which premieres on Dec. 30 on the streamer. The show will follow five celebs — Christie Brinkley, Margaret Cho, Vivica A. Fox, Caroline Rhea and Pebe Sebert (with daughter Kesha by her side) — as they open their hearts to a whole new dating world, guided by Harvard-trained behavioral scientist and relationship coach Logan Ury. “Whether navigating the highs and heartbreaks of modern romance for the first time in years or getting a second chance at love, these women prove that finding a real connection has no expiration date,” per the logline.

The first season of “The Later Daters” premiered on Netflix in November 2024 and followed six older singles as they embarked on blind dates with potential romantic partners. Each person would check in with Ury, who helped them navigate the unfamiliar world of modern dating, as well as their adult children and close friends, who provided sage advice.

“The Later Daters” hails from the Obamas’ production company Higher Ground and Boardwalk Pictures. Alongside Michelle Obama, the executive producers are Ethan Lewis, Andrew Fried, Jonathan Hausfater, Dane Lillegard, Sarina Roma, Ailee O’Neill, J.C. Begley and Cian O’Clery.

The unscripted series is one of many projects from Higher Ground to come out this year. This past summer, the company produced Larry David’s HBO sketch comedy series “Life, Larry and the Pursuit of Unhappiness,” which even starred Barack Obama in a cameo.

From ‘Dil Se’ to ‘Ramayana’: How Indian Cinema Shattered the U.K., Ireland Box Office Glass Ceiling

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

When Mani Ratnam’s romantic thriller “Dil Se..” debuted in August 1998, it achieved what was then considered unthinkable for South Asian cinema: entering the official U.K. and Ireland top 10 box office chart at No. 8. Powered by Shah Rukh Khan’s burgeoning overseas stardom and A.R. Rahman’s ubiquitous soundtrack, the film proved that Indian releases could cut through mainstream British multiplexes on limited screens.

That watershed moment initiated a 28-year ascent across Bollywood and South Indian regional industries, culminating this weekend when Sony Pictures’ mythological epic “Ramayana: Rise of a Legend” debuted at No. 1 with £2.1 million ($2.8 million) including previews, officially claiming the territory’s box office summit. Related Stories Taylor Swift, Inducted Into Nashville Songwriters Hall of Fame, Says 'There Is No Music Community Like This Music Community. I Know, I Checked All the Communities' Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss Reunite at Travis Kelce’s Chiefs Game Against Jets

Before “Dil Se..,” Indian cinema’s U.K. and Ireland footprint was largely confined to single screens and community halls in South Asian diaspora hubs such as Southall, Wembley, Leicester and Birmingham. While films like “Hum Aapke Hain Koun..!” (1994) and “Dilwale Dulhania Le Jayenge” (1995) generated significant receipts – eventually crossing the £1 million ($1.6 million) mark over prolonged runs – they operated in an isolated distribution ecosystem outside mainstream computerized reporting.

The breakthrough of “Dil Se..” transformed the market landscape. Just two months later, Karan Johar’s directorial debut “Kuch Kuch Hota Hai” built on that foundation, bowing inside the top 10 and amassing £1.7 million ($3 million). In 2001, Johar’s multi-starrer “Kabhi Khushi Kabhie Gham…” rewrote the record books, reaching No. 3 with £473,255 ($681,000) in its first week from just 41 screens and finishing its run with nearly £2.5 million ($3.6 million). Over the next decade, a string of major Bollywood releases – including Sanjay Leela Bhansali’s “Devdas” (2002), Yash Chopra’s “Veer-Zaara” (2004) and Johar’s “Kabhi Alvida Naa Kehna” (2006) – cemented Hindi-language cinema as a reliable weekend fixture in the U.K. top 10.

The threshold moved higher in 2010 when 20th Century Fox released Karan Johar’s “My Name Is Khan,” starring Shah Rukh Khan and Kajol, on 91 screens. The drama debuted at No. 6 with £936,000 ($1.4 million) and concluded with £2.6 million ($4.1 million). Three years later, Aamir Khan’s action franchise entry “Dhoom 3” (2013) raised the bar further, bowing with £884,567 ($1.4 million) and becoming the first Indian film to cross £2.7 million ($4.4 million) in the territory. Aamir Khan continued to push commercial frontiers with “PK” (2014), which grossed £2.3 million ($3.7 million), and “Dangal” (2016), which delivered £2.5 million ($3.2 million), alongside Salman Khan’s “Bajrangi Bhaijaan” (2015), which reached £2.6 million ($4.1 million).

Yet for all of Bollywood’s commercial muscle, an unyielding glass ceiling remained: chart positions consistently hovered between No. 3 and No. 10 against Hollywood tentpoles. Furthermore, South Indian cinema remained hindered by fragmented distribution.

That began to shift in April 2017 with S.S. Rajamouli’s “Baahubali 2: The Conclusion,” starring Prabhas. The film released across several languages and crossed £1 million ($1.3 million) in the territory within 10 days. However, fragmented reporting hindered its peak chart placement. The official chart, compiled by Comscore, listed each language version separately: the Hindi-language version opened at No. 6 with £221,452 ($286,000) from 50 sites and the Tamil-language version at No. 9 with £194,522 ($251,000) from 39 sites, while the Malayalam and Telugu-language versions charted lower. Even combined, its roughly £455,000 ($587,000) would have placed it only No. 6, behind “Their Finest” (£630,544; $813,000) and far below the £13.1 million ($16.9 million) bow of “Guardians of the Galaxy Vol. 2.”

The ceiling truly began to crack when Rajamouli’s “RRR” landed in March 2022. Driven by massive event demand, the Telugu epic debuted at No. 2 with £650,204 ($858,000) from 160 locations, including previews, held off from the summit only by Warner Bros.’ “The Batman.”

The subsequent wave of pan-Indian spectacle gradually united reporting streams and brought Southern regional industries into the mainstream multiplex fold. Mani Ratnam’s Tamil historical epic “Ponniyin Selvan: I” (2022) delivered a £745,000 ($834,000) opening weekend across 119 U.K. sites, charting at No. 5 and finishing with £1.2 million ($1.3 million). In March 2024, Blessy’s survival drama “Aadujeevitham – The Goat Life,” starring Prithviraj Sukumaran, set benchmarks for Malayalam cinema, debuting at No. 5 with £556,514 ($702,000) and grossing some £717,000 ($903,000) across its run. That summer, Nag Ashwin’s sci-fi epic “Kalki 2898 AD,” also starring Prabhas, released by Dreamz Entertainment across 450 U.K. sites, opened at No. 3 with £886,366 ($1.1 million) and went on to pass £1.5 million ($1.9 million).

Shah Rukh Khan starrers matched the historic No. 2 benchmark of “RRR” twice in 2023. In January, Siddharth Anand’s spy thriller “Pathaan” opened at No. 2 with a £1.4 million ($1.74 million) three-day weekend (reaching £1.97 million, or $2.44 million, over five days), behind James Cameron’s holdover “Avatar: The Way of Water,” on its way to a then-record U.K. total of £4.38 million ($5.4 million). In September 2023, Atlee’s action thriller “Jawan” mirrored the feat, debuting at No. 2 with £1.35 million ($1.67 million). Ranbir Kapoor’s violent action drama “Animal” (2023) opened lower, at No. 6 with £719,000 ($906,000), but legged out to £2.37 million ($3 million).

In March this year, Aditya Dhar’s spy sequel “Dhurandhar: The Revenge” became the fourth Indian film to open at No. 2, with a then-record £1.6 million ($2.1 million) Friday-to-Sunday weekend, or £2.1 million ($2.8 million) including previews. It went on to overtake “Pathaan” as the territory’s highest-grossing Indian release, with more than £4.4 million ($5.9 million).

South Indian cinema hit its own high-water mark in October this year, when Nelson Dilipkumar’s Tamil-language action sequel “Jailer 2,” starring Rajinikanth, opened at No. 3 with £1.1 million ($1.5 million) from just 228 sites, the highest site average of the weekend. Within 10 days, it had become the highest-grossing Tamil film in the territory with £1.6 million ($2.1 million).

Until this week, No. 2 remained the undisputed ceiling for Indian cinema in the territory.

That boundary fell with “Ramayana: Rise of a Legend.” Directed by Nitesh Tiwari (“Dangal”) and produced by Namit Malhotra’s Prime Focus Studios and DNEG in association with Yash’s Monster Mind Creations, the film is the first of a two-part adaptation of the Hindu epic. Ranbir Kapoor plays Rama, Yash plays the demon king Ravana and Sai Pallavi plays Sita, with Sunny Deol as Hanuman and Ravie Dubey as Lakshmana. The story pits the prince against the vengeful king and ends with Ravana’s abduction of Sita, stopping short of the war between them. Backed by a wide, format-heavy rollout across 364 sites that included premium 3D and large-format screens, the film’s £2.1 million ($2.8 million) debut – a new opening record for an Indian film in the territory – outstripped competing releases, including Disney’s “Wild Horse Nine” (£1.48 million; $1.97 million) at No. 2 and Anime Ltd.’s “Godzilla Minus Zero” (£1.3 million; $1.8 million) at No. 3.

From Mani Ratnam’s political romance sneaking into the lower rungs of the top 10 in 1998, through the trailblazing runs of “Baahubali 2,” “RRR,” “Pathaan” and “The Goat Life,” to a mythological tentpole conquering the weekend chart in 2026, Indian cinema has traveled from diaspora counterprogramming to the top of the U.K. and Ireland chart. However, the full-run numbers still lag well behind. The highest-grossing Indian release in the territory, “Dhurandhar: The Revenge,” has taken just over £4.4 million ($5.9 million), a fraction of what the biggest Hollywood and British releases earn. “Project Hail Mary,” which kept “Dhurandhar” off the top spot in March, passed £15 million ($20 million) in two weekends, while “Avatar: The Way of Water” was above £70 million ($86.8 million) by its seventh. “Ramayana” has broken the ceiling for one weekend. Breaking it over a full run is the next test.

THE•TEAM Forms New Board Following Sale Finalization

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The board's formation follows the finalization of Providence Equity Partners' purchase of the booking agency.

Talent agency THE•TEAM has formed a new board of directors, the company announced Tuesday (Nov. 10), marking the first time it has had a public-facing board.

THE•TEAM, formerly known as Wasserman, was sold by founder and former CEO Casey Wasserman to Providence Equity Partners in July following an outcry after Wasserman’s name surfaced in the Epstein Files. The deal was finalized in October.

Joining the new board are Michael Watts (CEO), Elizabeth Lindsey (global president, brands and properties), Jason Ranne (president, global talent representation), Kevin Shivers (co-president, music), Matthew Vandrau (president, EMEA & APAC), Elena Rochelli (chief financial officer) and Jon Liebman (chairman and CEO, Brillstein Entertainment Partners). Each business leader will continue serving in their roles at the company.

Rounding out the board are Providence Equity Partners’ Davis Noell (senior managing director, co-head of North America), Greg Brown (operating partner), Scott Marimow (managing director) and Ahana Maken (senior vp).

“This is an important moment for our company,” Watts said in a statement. “The members of this board have shown the qualities we value most: embracing our culture, supporting their colleagues and delivering exceptional results for our clients. This group will build on our momentum and help us accelerate growth by focusing on the ways we can best support our clients’ goals and aspirations.”

Noell added, “THE•TEAM has an incredibly talented leadership team and we welcome the expertise and perspective that this board will bring to the business. We’re excited about the growth opportunities ahead and will continue to provide capital and strategic partnership to help expand THE•TEAM’s capabilities across sports, music and entertainment.”

THE•TEAM’s music roster includes Chappell Roan, Coldplay, Ed Sheeran, SZA, Kendrick Lamar and Tyler, The Creator, among others.

Webby Awards Launches IMDB-Like Credits Platform to Provide Verified Recognition for Creators Amid AI Growth (Exclusive)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The long-running initiative celebrating the best of the internet will allow winners, nominees and other honored individuals to claim their work, with Shonda Rhimes and Adam Scott among the founding members.

After celebrating its 30th anniversary earlier this year, the Webby Awards is making it easier for winners, nominees and other honored individuals to make sure they’re properly credited for their work.

The IMDB-like Global Webby Credits Registry allows creators, developers, producers, writers and actors to claim verified profiles documenting their contributions. Though the Webbys have long included these names at the bottom of project pages, the profiles bring together a career’s worth of recognized work regardless of whether the people behind these projects move between different companies. Related Stories Movies Ridley Scott Will Miss Academy's Governors Awards, Accept Honorary Oscar Remotely (Exclusive) Movies 'You Can See Everything' Sweeps Critics Choice Documentary Awards

“The people behind great creative work on the internet have never been harder to find, and they’ve never mattered more,” Webby Group executive director Jesse Feister said in a statement. “The Global Webby Credits Registry puts 30 years of Webby recognition in the hands of the more than 150,000 individuals who earned it, and the thousands who will earn it in the years ahead, giving them verified credentials to build reputation in the AI era.”

As AI becomes a bigger tool in both the discovery of information and the generation of creative work, the Webbys argue these verified credits provide a better way to ensure people are recognized for what they’ve made.

“AI is changing how we discover information, including information about people and their work. Search results are increasingly summarized by AI, and as the technology evolves, we expect more of the ways people discover and evaluate creative professionals to be mediated by AI tools,” Feister tells The Hollywood Reporter. “That makes credible, independent records of people’s work more important than ever. A credit on an award-winning project is a meaningful professional credential, but only if that credit is documented somewhere it can be found and trusted. And as AI makes it easier than ever to produce creative work, having a credible record of what you’ve actually contributed to becomes more important, too.”

A number of Webby-recognized individuals, like Shonda Rhimes and Adam Scott, have already published their own verified profiles as founding members. The launch of the Webbys’ credits platform is also being supported by companies that have won Webbys in the past, including PBS, iHeartMedia and R/GA.

“Behind every great PBS digital experience is a team of talented people bringing our mission to life,” PBS’ vice president, multiplatform marketing and content, Amy Wigler, said in a statement. “The Webby Credits Registry gives those individuals recognition that stays with them throughout their careers. We’re excited to help celebrate their contributions and make their work more visible.”

To claim a profile, eligible individuals can search the Webby Credits database for projects they contributed to, claim the credits associated with their work, verify their identity through LinkedIn and publish their profile. As new work is recognized, the people behind those projects can claim their credits as well.

‘Heated Rivalry’ Gets Hands on With 4DX Screenings

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The hockey romance series is currently screening in theaters to celebrate a year since the show's initial release.

Ever want to immerse yourself in the world of Heated Rivalry? You’re in luck — the hit hockey romance series is officially headed to 4DX.

Regal Cinemas is partnering with CJ 4DPLEX to bring the final two episodes of Heated Rivalry‘s first season to fans in their immersive 4DX beginning Nov. 16. The 4DX experience features motion-enabled seating and synchronized atmospheric effects, so those wondering what Shane Hollander felt like getting checked by Cliff Marleau in the show’s penultimate episode are in for a treat.

The special event will screen at all 59 Regal 4DX auditoriums nationwide, along with other participating locations in other U.S. theaters. Internationally, 4DX screenings will be available at Cineworld in the United Kingdom and Cineplex in Canada. Related Stories Movies Connor Storrie in Talks to Join Ryan Gosling in Marvel's 'Ghost Rider' TV Gay Immigrant Comedy 'One Baby, Please!' to Hit OUTtv Next Month as Latest Queer Series From Canada

“Fans made it loud and clear that they wanted more Heated Rivalry on the big screen, so we’re turning up the heat,” Regal senior vp U.S. content Brooks LeBoeuf said in a release.

“Episodes five and six bring years of tension between Shane and Ilya to a boiling point, and 4DX takes it all to another level. Together with CJ 4DPLEX, we’re giving fans a chance to feel every hit on the ice, every stolen embrace, and every pulse-pounding moment in between,” LeBoeuf continued. “You may know these episodes by heart, but you’ve never experienced them like this.”

Beginning on Nov. 6 and going through Nov. 30, fans of the show have been able to revisit the series across three paired episode screenings: episodes one and two (“Rookies” and “Olympians”), episodes three and four (“Hunter” and “Rose”) and episodes five and six (“I’ll Believe in Anything” and “The Cottage”). Tickets for the regular release run at $9.99 per screening; however, pricing for the 4DX experience is unclear.

Based on Rachel Reid‘s novel of the same name, Heated Rivalry focuses on the complicated situationship between Shane Hollander, played by Hudson Williams, and Ilya Rozanov, played by Connor Storrie. The pair are generational talents in the world of professional hockey and sworn on-ice rivals, who meet as teenagers.

The first novel of Reid’s book series, Game Changers, which the novel Heated Rivalry hails from, centered on François Arnaud’s character Scott Hunter and Robbie G.K.’s character Kip Grady; it served as secondary material for the first season. The show’s season one cast was rounded out by Christina Chang, Dylan Walsh, Sophie Nélisse and Ksenia Daniela Kharlamova.

Season two of the series is filming in Toronto, and features a slew of new cast members. Williams and Storrie return to front the series, with Justice Smith, Charlie Gillespie, Daniel Diemer and Shaheen Jafargholi joining them in leading roles for the second season.

Tickets for Heated Rivalry‘s 4DX theatrical event are available at the box office and Regal’s website and mobile app.
