Pikmin 4 cemented the Switch 2 as the best console for clearing out my backlog

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Both as a matter of personal interest and professional obligation, I try to play as many first-party Nintendo releases as I can. But sometimes even the best ones fall through the cracks.

That's what happened to me when Pikmin 4 launched in 2023. I'd never really been able to vibe with the franchise's fascinating blend of plant-based task management and time-limit stress, so even though the fourth entry garnered rave reviews, I never made proper time for it.

I feel like a big old idiot now because, after playing a few hours of the new Switch 2 upgrade, I can see that Pikmin 4 is kind of incredible, even with a fairly modest set of upgrades for Nintendo's new console. My biggest takeaway from this experience, though, is that there really isn't a better current-gen console for catching up on old games and clearing out your backlog than the Switch 2.

There are two things I want to note right off the bat. First, the official title for this release is Pikmin 4 – Nintendo Switch 2 Edition + Dandori Academy, which is a comically tedious thing to type out more than once, so I won't be doing that. Second, this is just a $10 upgrade for those who already own Pikmin 4 on Switch 1, and the changes this new version brings are not exactly paradigm-shifting.

As with every "Switch 2 Edition" release of an older game, Pikmin 4 has received a boost in resolution. This was already a gorgeous game on Switch 1, with varied and colorful environments, expressive lighting, and a generally warm and welcoming atmosphere, but the Switch 2 version is sharper and pops even more on a nice TV. Unfortunately, the frame rate, which was capped at 30 frames per second on Switch 1, has not been upgraded at all for Switch 2. That's a bummer, but not a deal-breaker.

Players can also use the Switch 2's microphone to bark voice commands at Oatchi, their loyal space dog friend, and this is a feature. I turned that off almost immediately because it's impossible to hold a conversation with someone while playing the game that way. The beefiest addition is Dandori Academy, a mode with a series of new challenge levels. I haven't had time to really dive in yet, but Pikmin 4 veterans looking for something new to do will probably welcome it.

The thing is, aside from the visual boost, I'm not really concerned with what's new in the Switch 2 version of Pikmin 4 because the whole game is new to me. And what a delight it is! Pikmin is all about plucking dozens of cute little plant dudes from the dirt, color-coded according to their unique abilities, and ordering them to do things for you. They can clear obstacles, open up shortcuts, defeat enemies, and most important, carry treasures you find back to your spaceship so you can unlock upgrades and become even more efficient at managing this whole operation.

Strategy games are typically not my cup of tea, but Pikmin scratches a substantial itch for me in that it lets me plant the seeds (literally, in this case) for efficient, automatic processes to play out, and then lets me sit back and observe. It's eminently satisfying to order 10 Pikmin to go open up a shortcut while 10 others defeat an enemy and use the shortcut to carry its corpse back to your ship for harvesting.

And while the decision to give Pikmin 4 no overall time limit might be a detriment to series die-hards, it's a huge boon to me, a guy who stresses out easily. Even after just a few hours, I can see that this is one of the coolest games Nintendo has made in the past five or six years, and the Switch 2 version helped me arrive at that conclusion.

Gaming is an expensive and time-consuming hobby, and it can be nearly impossible for anyone who doesn't review games for a living to play most new releases and participate in the zeitgeist around them. What I've come to realize since the Switch 2 came out, though, is that playing older games all the time instead of feverishly speeding through every new release actually kicks ass, and for my money, this is the best current console platform to do that on.

Nothing will ever beat a PC in this regard, but if you don't have one of those, the Switch 2 is your best bet right now. The PS5's ability to play games from before the PS4 generation is limited at best, and while the Xbox Series consoles can play some games from every Xbox generation, Microsoft has more or less stopped adding new old games to the console backwards compatibility list.

Meanwhile, the Switch 2 can play most Switch 1 games, including countless re-releases, remakes, and remasters of games from across all of gaming history. You can play (versions of) almost every mainline Final Fantasy game on Switch 2. You can play many Resident Evil games on Switch 2, and in fact, that's how I finally learned to love Resident Evil 4 earlier this year. Thanks to Capcom's preservation efforts, I can also play basically every Mega Man game on this machine.

That's before you even get into more obscure stuff that's been preserved on Switch, like the Arcade Archives collection, or quality modern ports of weirdo games like Tail of the Sun and Oddworld: Stranger's Wrath. There's just so much to dig into.

A lot of the above is also true of PS5 and Xbox, but those machines lack decades of first-party Nintendo games that exist either as native Switch 1/2 releases, or as part of the Nintendo Classics collection. With Nintendo Switch Online, I can turn on my Switch 2 right now and play dozens of NES, SNES, and Game Boy games. The Expansion Pack adds N64, Genesis, Game Boy Advance, and GameCube games to the mix, and I've greatly enjoyed digging through those libraries since the Switch 2 came out.

All of that, combined with Nintendo's "Switch 2 Edition" releases, which give some Switch 1 games a sorely needed upgrade, has given me my old games mojo back after it went missing for a few years. I realize it sounds strange to recommend a $500 machine on the basis that it's great at playing old games, but, well, here we are. Adobe Firefly AI Assistant can now take on recurring tasks for you

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The company is also bringing an agent to the Premiere mobile app.

Few companies have embraced artificial intelligence quite like Adobe. Between Photoshop, Premiere and Illustrator, the company has brought its Firefly assistant, and other generative AI features, to much of its Creative Cloud suite. Adobe is showing no signs of slowing down. At its Max conference in Miami today, the company announced a significant expansion to Creative Cloud's agentic capabilities.

To start, taking a page from Anthropic and OpenAI, Adobe is dipping its toes into the world of proactive agents. An updated version of its Firefly AI Assistant can now carry out multi-step tasks. When you give the AI Assistant a job, a new planning mode allows you to review and edit the agent's proposed workflow before giving it approval to move forward. You can also select a specific model to orchestrate the tasks or leave that decision up to Adobe's software. At the same, the company is giving users more fine control over a few different customization options. For example, you can now define colors and brand guidelines that will apply across projects the assistant takes on for you.

In addition to making AI Assistant more capable, Adobe is also previewing new always-on agents that can take on recurring tasks for you. In a preview the company shared with Engadget, it demoed a user prompting Firefly AI Assistant to sort through footage as it was being uploaded to a project. The assistant offered to organize the raw files, automatically apply LUTs and clean up audio, as well as back up the files, which the user approved in the new planning mode. After organizing the footage, the user tasked AI Assistant with preparing a social media promotion campaign for the project.

Separately, Adobe is bringing a new agent to the Premiere mobile app, which first debuted on iOS last year before later arriving on Android. As you can imagine, the agent is designed to help users make edits using natural language. Adobe is first making this tool available in private preview before rolling it out more broadly. Lastly, the company is testing a new set of MCP connections that allow you to give your favorite chatbot local access to Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere and After Effects so that it can do things like edit layers for you.

As Adobe took me through many of its Max announcements, the one thing I kept thinking about was if the company's end goal was to eventually bypass the creatives that have been its customers for so long. After all, the number of people who might be skilled with Photoshop and Premiere is far smaller than the group might want to use its capabilities.

To point, in one demo, Zorana Gee, the company's senior director of agentic product, mentioned how entrepreneurs could use Firefly AI Assistant's capabilities to create social posts for their small business. "Ultimately, we're a creative company serving creatives," she said when I asked her about that question. "Depending on where you are on that continuum is where we will meet you, but ultimately it's about how do we allow anybody to hone their craft."

I also asked Gee about Artcraft, the free Adobe alternative that was made possible by the growing sophistication of coding models from Anthropic and OpenAI. Gee said she felt the "professionalism, security and reliability" of Adobe's products would be what allows the company to keep users invested in its ecosystem.

Spotify adds new voices for its AI DJ

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

You can also now listen to a DJ mix without the commentary.

Spotify is adding more ways to customize the experience of using its AI-generated DJ. The feature began rolling out back in 2023 as a way to get a radio-like, lean-back listening experience tailored to each listener's individual tastes with an AI voiceover offering context or chatter about the selections. DJ has since expanded to taking requests and supporting additional languages.

Listeners can now also choose a personality for the voice talking them through the song selections. Spotify is rolling out seven personas in English and two in Spanish. Each one has a name and a different approach, ranging from "laid back" to "gregarious." The English language character lineup has three US voices, one "UK," one Irish, one Australian and one Indian. Spanish listeners will have two Mexican voices to choose between.

Spotify has also added an option for turning off the DJ commentary, which means the AI voice will not offer any chatter between songs. It will only speak to introduce the listening session or when it's directed to change up the music choices. Photoshop, Lightroom, and Premiere are making it easier to remove things

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Here are the new editing and design tools coming to Creative Cloud apps and Adobe’s Firefly suite.﻿

Here are the new editing and design tools coming to Creative Cloud apps and Adobe’s Firefly suite.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Adobe is expanding its Creative Cloud apps and Firefly AI platform with new capabilities that aim to make design and editing projects faster and less complicated. Some of the announcements from Adobe’s Max event today include features for automatically removing objects, blemishes, and other unwanted distractions in Premiere, Photoshop, and Lightroom, alongside new generative AI tools and collaborative experiences across Adobe’s apps and Firefly suite.

In Premiere, Adobe is introducing a new Generative Remove tool in beta that lets you erase unwanted objects and distractions like boom mics and background clutter directly on the video editing timeline. Users can mask and track an object, click Remove, and then Premiere “fills the area with realistic detail” using generative AI, according to Adobe, while the rest of the footage surrounding the erased area remains untouched. We saw a similar capability called Project Frame Forward demonstrated in last year’s Sneaks showcase, which is what Adobe calls the experimental features it’s developing.

Premiere is also getting new sound editing tools that help to quickly clean up messy recordings and separate various audio sources — including dialogue, music, ambience, and sound effects — into individual tracks and elements. The Generative Media tool Adobe introduced earlier this year is now generally available, alongside the new Color Mode color editing experience that Adobe launched in beta in April.

Photoshop now has an updated Remove Tool, which is better at detecting and reconstructing surrounding elements associated with the objects it’s erasing, such as reflections, shadows, and footprints. Features that are new to Photoshop include Quick Compare, which gives you a before-and-after canvas split-screen slider to review changes and edits without jumping between layers, and Relight, which adds new light sources that you can position around the canvas to dynamically adjust the lighting of images, with support for customizing hue and brightness.

The blemish removal tool in Lightroom on mobile is now coming to the desktop version, allowing you to retouch skin in photographs with a single tap. Open Eyes is also coming to Lightroom in public beta, which fixes blinking mishaps by replacing closed eyes with “natural-looking” open eyed results by matching a reference photo of the subject from your image library.

Adobe’s Firefly AI creative suite is getting new tools for video and image editing, and Quick Actions — ready-to-use workflows that specialize in automating specific tasks, such as making video thumbnails and product ads — are now generally available. Some new beta experiences are also launching in Firefly, including the Firefly Design combined edifying space for illustration, image editing, animation, 3D, and print. There’s also Firefly Graph, which “helps creators build and iterate on custom, repeatable workflows,” according to Adobe, and looks rather like the Weave platform that Figma introduced last year. And in private beta, Adobe introduced a Firefly Builder tool that’s designed around prototyping websites and apps — which also shares some similarities with Figma Make.

We’ve called out the most interesting announcements from Adobe Max here, but it’s just a fraction of the updates being showcased at the event. You can find the full list of all the new AI and design features announced today on Adobe’s blog.

Adobe is going all in on agentic AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Third-party AI agents can use Creative Cloud apps for you, and Firefly’s AI Assistant can run your social media accounts.

Third-party AI agents can use Creative Cloud apps for you, and Firefly’s AI Assistant can run your social media accounts.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Adobe is committing itself to the notion that chatbots and AI agents could be your next design assistant, or even automate creative workflows and repetitive tasks on your behalf. Some of the tools and features announced today at Adobe’s Max conference in Miami, Florida, include allowing your preferred third-party AI agents to access and change Creative Cloud projects, new capabilities for Adobe’s Firefly AI Assistant, and the ability for AI agents to keep perpetually running recurring work in the background.

Local Model Context Protocol (MCP) integrations for Creative Cloud tools were previewed at the event, which aim to give you a choice between using Adobe’s own AI assistants, or the third-party agents you already use everyday. The integrations enable external AI agents from providers like OpenAI, Google, and Anthropic to work locally in Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere, and After Effects, where they can make direct changes to native project files on your computer.

You can take over from the agents at any time to manually refine the work because all layers, vectors, bins, and sequences remain editable. Agents that are connected to other MCP-capable tools can also include them in the same workflow, such as asking the AI agent to pull reference documents from Google Docs, design a project around them in Adobe’s tools, and then share the results with colleagues or clients in Slack. Creative Cloud local MCP integrations aren’t available yet, but Adobe says a private beta will be launching in early 2027.

“Always-on” agents were also previewed at Adobe’s Max event, which can perform recurring tasks continuously in the background, and allow you to focus on other work or step away from your computer entirely. You can assign them a job, such as organizing incoming assets, ongoing quality checks, or preparing content, and then review the agent’s plan and approve the final outcome. For example, an agent could be assigned to automatically turn all incoming images into promotional graphics for different social media channels, allowing you to check in daily and select the best results to publish. Adobe hasn’t specified when this preview will launch, but says it’ll be coming to Firefly AI Assistant first.

The Firefly AI Assistant beta, which is powered by Adobe’s creative agent, is also getting some upgrades. New capabilities include a Plan Mode that enables users to review, edit, and approve the agent’s plan before it starts running a task, a Model Selector that lets you specify which AI model to use for a project or leave it on auto-select to automatically assign the model based on the required results, and a new integration with Firefly Boards. Firefly AI Assistant now also lets you customize preferred formats, colors, brand guidelines, characters, and products, and choose to automatically apply those preferences across every project.

The creative agent has been used to stick AI assistants into Premiere, Photoshop, Illustrator, InDesign, After Effects, and Frame.io since it was introduced earlier this year, and now it’s expanding further into video editing. In Premiere, it can now be used to track masked subjects through a frame and apply effects or adjust color and exposure across entire clip sequences. The agent is also being added to Premiere mobile editing app, allowing you to make conversational video edits while on the go.

If it feels like Adobe is slapping agents and chatbots into all of its products then you’d be correct. That’s all according to the plan the company’s creativity head David Wadhwani laid out in April when he said the Adobe creative agent will eventually surface “in virtually everything we develop.” Whether it’s developing its own agentic solutions or throwing open the doors to third-party bots, AI agents will play a key role in Adobe’s future.

Spotify’s AI DJ gets new voices, personalities, and an option for less chatter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Spotify’s AI DJ, one of the company’s earlier efforts in embracing AI technology to connect people with their favorite music, is getting an upgrade. On Tuesday, the streamer announced that users will now be able to customize their DJ more like they can an AI assistant, by choosing between different voices and personalities. Those who like the feature, but not all the chatter, can also now opt for an experience with less commentary in between sets.

Since first launching the AI DJ in 2023, Spotify has leveraged AI across to improve all areas of its app. Most recently, that has included updates that allow users to chat with a conversational AI agent about music, fine-tune their recommendations with prompts, create playlists with AI, and work with third-party agents like ChatGPT and Claude. However, the DJ remains one of Spotify’s most popular AI features, as it’s used by over 94M Spotify Premium listeners.

Now, it’s revisiting one of its first major AI features, taking a cue from the personalization options other AI assistants routinely offer.

The different voices will have their own unique personalities as well. For instance, users can pick between three American voices, one of which is laid-back and warm (Casey), another that’s direct and has an edge (Tess), and a third that has a Southern accent and a youthful energy (Lane). In addition to the other various regional accents, there are also two new Spanish voices, one that’s more down-to-earth (Nico) and one that’s more witty and friendly (Fabi).

The voices will not replace the AI DJ’s original voice, “X,” which was based on that of a Spotify employee’s real voice recordings. However, DJ Fabi and Nico will now be the only Spanish voices available, replacing the earlier “Livi.”

The other change allows users to toggle off the commentary between songs, reducing the DJ’s interruptions. While typically, the AI DJ introduces different selections of music, explaining what it picked and why, users can now limit it to brief acknowledgements when they first start the DJ feature and when they turn it off. Spotify says this would allow people to use the DJ at times they may have turned to a playlist instead, like when hosting a party where they want music in the background, but not the chatter.

To access the DJ, users can search for the term “DJ” in the search bar of the Spotify app on their device, then tap the three-dot menu to pick their voice and commentary settings. To change genres, artists, or recommendations while the DJ is driving things, you simply tap the DJ button again.

Spotify says the new voices begin rolling out today, November 10, to Premium users in the markets where DJ is already supported. Currently, that includes over 95 global markets, where the DJ is supported in English, Spanish, French, German, Italian, and Brazilian Portuguese.

Peak Design’s Roller Lite Carry-On is bigger on the inside — and cheaper, too

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

More efficient and more affordable, but less charming, too.﻿

More efficient and more affordable, but less charming, too.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When we sampled Peak Design’s Roller Pro last year, we loved almost everything about the camera gear powerhouse’s first attempt at a proper suitcase. Everything, that is, except for the price. Back then, during its crowdfunded launch, the Pro could be had for as little as $425. Today, you’re looking at $599.95 for the same roller bag.

Peak Design has taken that feedback to heart in creating the Roller Lite Carry-On, part of a new series of suitcases that bring many of the Pro’s features and design concepts into a hard-sided clamshell design that’s more traditional and, crucially, more affordable, starting with the $449.95 Lite Carry-On.

It’s part of a new Lite family of suitcases. The slightly larger Lite Carry-On Plus costs $469.99 and measures 22.5 x 15 x 9 inches, while the $549.95 Lite Trunk is 30 x 16.1 x 13.8 inches. The former should still squeeze in most overhead compartments, while the latter is better off checked. Both, though, come in cheaper than the Pro.

That cost-cutting does result in bags with fewer features and less originality, but I still found the new Lite Carry-On to be a great travel companion, first for a two-day whirlwind journey to Rome, then for a week in Tucson, Arizona.

I travel a lot, spending far more time escorting my carry-on around airports than I do driving my car at home, so no surprise that I’m picky about what I drag through terminals and haul up into overhead compartments. I wouldn’t say that aesthetics are a primary concern, but everybody prefers a good-looking suitcase, and the Roller Lite passes that first test. It’s understated yet sophisticated in black, but the emerald green option called cypress might be even better. There’s also a hue called stone for those more into earth tones.

Regardless of color, I think the Lite Carry-On’s hard plastic shell looks better than the soft-sided Roller Pro, and I’d hazard a guess it’ll stand the test of time better, too. The second trip I took with the Lite started in a tiny puddle-jumper of a plane. The flight attendant gate-checked any bag with wheels, even though the Lite Carry-On (21.7 x 13.8 x 9 inches, slightly smaller than the Pro) would have easily fit in the overhead.

It came back to me from its trip to the belly of the aircraft with a few new scuffs and scratches, otherwise looking only a little worse for wear.

Durability was a big selling point on the Roller Pro, and that continues here. The Lite is even more repairable thanks to its easily replaced strap handles. They sit flush without taking up any room in the suitcase itself, yet are quite comfortable to use.

Both the Pro and Lite share the same carbon fiber handle, also easily replaceable. It’s my favorite feature, rising smoothly and dropping into place without a hitch. I especially love the dry carbon weave that’s exposed when it’s raised.

My only complaint is that the handle on top is too bulky, making it a clumsy fit through the luggage passthrough on my backpack. But otherwise, the carbon construction makes for a sturdier feel than the handle on my Tumi 19 Degree Aluminum roller, which costs three times as much.

While both share the same handle, the Lite’s design is a substantial departure from the Pro. The Pro offered a so-called “hybrid” layout, with the top opening up and swinging back into a single large compartment. The Lite is a more traditional clamshell with a zipper running the full circumference, splitting the case in two like a book.

When it comes to packing camera equipment and other gear, I think the Pro’s single main compartment is easier to live with, especially if you’re hauling some bulky telephoto glass.

But when I’m packing more items of clothing than camera gear, I actually prefer the Lite Carry-On’s two smaller compartments. I can separate clothes from everything else, which is useful when living out of a suitcase.

The Lite Carry-On lacks the Pro’s trick external pockets for laptops, passports, and the like. That doesn’t bother me, as that stuff always goes in my backpack, but if you’re looking for a single-bag travel experience, the Pro is better suited. The Lite is also missing the Cord Hook feature of the Pro, potentially useful for strapping a tripod or the like to the outside.

Inside, the right half of the Lite has a zippered flap cover with two large mesh pockets that don’t look or feel as good as the inner pockets on the Pro. You’ll still find a hidden pocket for tucking away an Apple AirTag tracker, though.

The other side has the same elastic-strap holding system as the Pro. You load it up with stuff, then stretch the two hooks across to latch on the other side. It’s a basic, flimsy-looking solution, but it works. When the agents at Leonardo da Vinci International Airport wanted to rummage around in my bag, those little bungee hooks kept everything from scattering over the floor in Fiumicino.

The Lite Carry-On has an integrated, TSA-approved lock, something missing on the Pro, and though it’s slightly smaller on the outside (21.7 x 13.8 vs. 21.8 x 14), removing that front pocket makes it substantially bigger on the inside: 38 liters vs. 34 on the Pro, expanding to 45 liters vs. 39.

The 2-inch expansion on the Lite is a great emergency solution if you picked up a few too many souvenirs in Seville, but the Lite feels loose and floppy when rolling around with its expansion zipper open. I don’t recommend it.

Otherwise, when fully zipped and packed, I really enjoyed wheeling the Roller Lite around. It was an easy glider over smooth terminal floors and didn’t rattle obnoxiously over rougher asphalt.

The Roller Lite Carry-On is undoubtedly a simpler suitcase than the Roller Pro that preceded it, and because of that, it doesn’t feel nearly as special to use or purpose-driven as Peak Design’s offerings typically are.

But then it’s $150 cheaper, roomier, is easier to pack, and, in my estimation, will prove even more durable. It may not be the ultimate roller bag for pro photogs, but it’s my new favorite suitcase for under $500. I’d even put it ahead of options from Tumi, Briggs & Riley, and others that cost many times more, aren’t as easily repaired, and don’t come with a lifetime warranty.

After challenging Google’s Android with Cyanogen, its co-founder takes aim at Windows for AI era

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Almost a decade after Cyanogen shut down its Android operating-system business following a years-long, unsuccessful effort to loosen Google’s dominance, its co-founder, Kirt McMaster, is now targeting Microsoft’s Windows with a new operating system designed for people who rely heavily on artificial intelligence in their everyday work.

McMaster’s company, Kora Systems, on Tuesday unveiled a Linux-based desktop operating system that integrates AI models and autonomous agents directly into the computing experience, allowing them to work across applications, access files, and retain context between tasks. The software is expected to launch in 2027 as a free-to-use desktop operating system, with support for existing Intel- and AMD-powered PCs as well as machines running Qualcomm’s Snapdragon chips.

Kora is developing the eponymous operating system with a team of about 50 engineers, relying on AI coding tools including OpenAI’s Codex and Anthropic’s Claude. McMaster told TechCrunch that these tools have accelerated development by at least fivefold, with most of the gains coming over the past six months.

Microsoft has long dominated the PC operating-system market, making it hard for newer players to establish a foothold. Windows held about 77% of the worldwide desktop operating-system market in October, compared with nearly 10% for Apple’s OS X, according to StatCounter’s web traffic data.

However, McMaster believes Kora does not need to compete for Windows’ entire user base. He is rather targeting people who use AI extensively and are willing to pay for access to powerful models and services.

“Microsoft can keep its billion users,” McMaster said, estimating that 45 million to 50 million people currently account for much of AI usage and revenue. He expects that market to exceed 200 million users by 2033.

McMaster told TechCrunch that his experience at Cyanogen, where he spent over five years, taught him the dangers of relying on the very company he was trying to challenge. Cyanogen remained heavily dependent on Google Mobile Services despite offering features beyond Google’s version of Android. That ultimately restricted its ability to build an independent ecosystem.

“If you are trying to compete with an incumbent but are still reliant on them, you have no chance,” he said.

Kora, McMaster said, builds on an idea he pursued toward the end of his time at Cyanogen, when he began exploring ways to integrate third-party services directly into the operating system. With Kora, he wants AI models and agents to work across applications and access files without being confined to individual apps.

Previously known as Playtron, a gaming operating-system company led by McMaster, the Palo Alto-based company rebranded as Kora in July 2025 as it shifted its focus to AI. The decision came as early companies including Perplexity and OpenAI were moving into AI-powered web browsers, McMaster said. He believed browsers would not give AI agents enough access to a computer to carry out more complex tasks, making an operating system a better starting point.

The shift also allowed Kora to build on nearly three years of work developing Playtron’s Linux-based gaming operating system, called GameOS, instead of starting from scratch.

Kora is designed to let users work with different AI models and agents across applications, while keeping track of conversations, documents, and other information. It also allows AI agents to operate in separate workspaces with restricted access to files and other resources.

While McMaster has drawn on his experience at Cyanogen, none of Kora’s engineers worked there. Instead, the company recruited heavily from the Linux gaming community, he told TechCrunch.

Kora’s software is built on Linux and uses Nix. It replaces the Fedora Silverblue foundation of Playtron’s earlier GameOS.

While Linux and Nix are open source, McMaster said Kora’s own technology, including its Cortex AI engine and desktop interface, remains proprietary. The company, he said, intends to support AI models and services from different providers, although it has not committed to making its own software open source.

Gaming also remains part of Kora’s plans. McMaster said the company intends to introduce AI-powered tools that would allow developers to create, publish, and play games directly within the operating system. He added that support for Android apps is also on its roadmap.

McMaster told TechCrunch that Kora’s team has worked closely with Qualcomm’s engineers for nearly a year to optimize the operating system specifically for Snapdragon-powered PCs. The collaboration includes support for Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X2 Elite chips.

Alongside Qualcomm, Kora is working with Saudi Arabia’s AI company Humain on an enterprise version of the operating system, called Humain OS, which is expected to run on Snapdragon-powered laptops in 2027. Kora also plans to announce partnerships with PC manufacturers at a later date, though McMaster did not identify any companies or provide a specific timeline.

Kora plans to generate revenue through subscriptions that bundle access to AI models and agentic services. Moreover, its Cortex engine is designed to run AI tasks locally when possible and use cloud-based models for work requiring more computing power.

Kora’s investors include Samsung Next, Square Enix, Alumni Ventures, Polychain, VanEck, and Circle, all of which previously backed Playtron. The company, McMaster said, has raised additional funding since shifting its focus to AI. However, he declined to disclose the total amount raised or its valuation. Kora has enough funding to operate for several years, he said.

Aura’s next color E Ink photo frame is smaller, cheaper, sharper, and more colorful

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The new 10-inch version of the Aura Ink can display three new colors that help skin tones look more natural.﻿

The new 10-inch version of the Aura Ink can display three new colors that help skin tones look more natural.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Aura just launched a smaller version of its color E Ink frame with a handful of improvements and a more affordable price. The new Aura Ink frame features a 10-inch E Ink panel that can display more colors at a higher pixel density. It’s also differentiated from the original 13-inch model with a black frame and matching mat board that helps photos stand out.

The Aura Ink 10 is now available in the US for $349, $150 cheaper than the 13-inch version, which still costs $499. The original Ink’s price made it a tough sell over Aura’s other frames that are available with an LCD screen as large as 15 inches for $299. The new 10-inch option makes color E Ink more accessible, and it carries forward one of the Ink’s biggest advantages: a rechargeable battery so the frame can hang on a wall without a dangling power cord.

Although color E Ink displays have been available in e-readers like Kindles and Kobos for a few years, those devices use a color filter over a standard black-and-white e-paper panel with limited color reproduction. The Aura Ink instead uses E Ink’s Spectra 6 technology, which creates patterns of black, white, red, yellow, blue, and green ink droplets to reproduce a much wider color spectrum.

For the new 10-inch version, Aura worked with E Ink to add three custom base colors created by mixing those six inks in new ways: off-white, mustard yellow, and orange. The goal was to improve the company’s proprietary dithering algorithm to produce more natural-looking colors on faces, add detail to textures like grass, and to help smooth gradients so photos of sunsets, for example, look better. The 10-inch panel also has a higher pixel density — 200ppi, up from 150ppi on the 13-inch model — allowing it to produce slightly sharper results.

Both are welcome improvements, but in practice the color reproduction isn’t drastically better than the original. You’d need to compare the same photo on both models side by side to really see a difference, and the upgrades don’t overcome one of the major shortcomings of the Spectra 6 technology. While E Ink estimates it can reproduce around 65,000 different colors — and potentially even more with Aura’s enhancements — LCD displays can produce millions.

That can make it hard to predict how your photos will look on the new Aura Ink. Rich colors — like blue sky, green trees, and bright red flags — really pop, but skin tones can still sometimes look too warm or cold. I sent to the frame a photo of a bright-yellow water taxi photographed on a sunny day and expected it to appear well saturated, but that particular shade of yellow came out disappointingly subdued.

The technology still has its challenges, but the benefits of E Ink’s color displays make the new Aura Ink a tempting alternative to the company’s LCD-based frames, particularly for those who don’t want another glowing screen at home. Photos displayed on the Aura Ink look like a printed photo, even with a subtle front light that automatically adjusts to boost the lighting in a room.

It would fit right in on a wall of printed photos, and Aura estimates you’d need to take it down every three months to charge. But that’s if you update the displayed photo once every night. You can increase the frequency through Aura’s mobile app to up to 12 automatic refreshes per day, or manually change it using a set of buttons hidden on the back if you don’t mind charging the Ink more often. Just be mindful that unlike an e-reader that refreshes a page of text in less than a second, it takes the Ink about 30 seconds to change to a new image after a series of flickering, psychedelic patterns.

I’ve been testing it as a standalone picture frame using a removable support that props the Aura Ink up in landscape or horizontal orientations. Moving it around the house without hunting for an outlet first is a big plus. And as with all of Aura’s digital photo frames, the new 10-inch Ink also benefits from the polished mobile app that has helped make the company’s products so popular. You can send photos to the Ink through a Wi-Fi connection with free, unlimited cloud storage or create shared albums that friends and family can view and contribute to. It also works with Google Photos and Apple Photos. If you don’t have access to the app, you can text or email photos directly to the frame.

If you plan to gift an Aura frame during the holidays, the company’s LCD models are still more budget-friendly, with 10-inch options starting at $149. The new version of the Aura Ink still feels like a device for early adopters who completely understand the tradeoffs of E Ink’s color screens. But if you prefer the look of printed photos over a glowing LCD, the smaller Aura Ink is now one of the most affordable ways to try out this tech.

Aura’s newest e-ink photo frame is even better and more affordable than its last

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you’re into interior design, decorating, or just love displaying your family photos, Aura‘s latest product line will feel magical. Aura’s cordless Ink photo frames have the versatility of a digital photo frame, but their e-ink displays don’t even look digital, blending seamlessly into the rest of your framed photos.

A follow-up to its 13-inch, $499 Ink frame, Aura is unveiling a new product that’s a little less pricey: a 10-inch Ink frame that is now on sale for $349.

“Ten years ago when we were first starting Aura, we were interested in making an e-ink picture frame. We just wanted a frame that looked like a printed photo and magically changed, but the tech was not there at that point,” Aura co-founder and CTO Eric Jensen told TechCrunch. “So it’s great to be able to see it moving forward.”

Aura uses E Ink’s Spectra 6 display for its Ink frames, which can only render six different colors. What sets Aura apart is its proprietary dithering algorithm, a technique that blends a limited color palette into patterns that the eye reads as smooth gradients.

While the 13″ Ink frame looks beautiful, it is obvious that the colors are not exact to the original photo. For the new 10″ Ink frame, Aura worked with E Ink on expanding its Spectra 6 technology to incorporate three new proprietary colors: off white, mustard, and orange.

“The net effect is that the edges are smoothed a bit more,” Jensen said. “You can see if you zoom in on a face, the places where we would’ve had to previously put white next to red, or something a little more jarring, we can now fill in with the right color, and it effectively makes it a little bit higher resolution.”

With the extra three colors, the Ink 10″ is getting truer to representing an original photo, but the colors still appear less saturated on the digital display than in a printed image.

For both Ink frames, Aura paid special attention to rendering skin tones accurately, since most people use these frames for family photos. (Personally, I think the slight variations in color give scenery photos a cool, vintage look). It may not matter as much if your kid’s bright pink shirt looks closer to red on the Ink, but if her skin is unnaturally grey, then it would look pretty weird, hence Aura’s meticulous calibration of these colors.

The 10″ frame only comes in one colorway: a black frame with a dark grey linen-textured mat. It looks a bit jarring at first, and I wouldn’t hang it on a gallery wall next to ten other white-matted photos, but it looks great on its own.

“We intentionally made it dark so that it didn’t have as much contrast with the e-ink display next to it,” Jensen said.

Like Aura’s other frames, the Ink 10″ is compatible with Aura’s app, where customers and their family and friends can easily upload photos from their phone to an album of photos that display on their frame.

“On average, there’s about four people sharing to each frame,” Jensen said, meaning that it’s common for customers to share their Aura albums with others. If your mother has an Aura frame, for example, she can grant you permission to add photos to her frame, letting you easily drop the newest photos of her grandson at the park.

On Ink frames, the company recommends setting the frame to only update once per day, since changing photos consumes battery life (and it’s a bit odd to watch the dithering algorithm in action).

Aura comes with hanging hardware, a stand, and a USB-C charging cable, and the company claims that the Ink 10″ can last three months on a single charge. In my experience with the Ink 13″, I’d say the battery actually lasts about a month. But this is perhaps because I had displayed it in an area of my home that gets a lot of light, so the front light is probably lit more often than if I had placed it elsewhere.

The new Ink frame still doesn’t generate perfect, one-to-one reproductions of your photos, but the precision of color doesn’t matter that much. What matters more is that, if you aren’t expecting it, you could easily be fooled into thinking that the Ink 10″ is just an ordinary framed photo.

Ring’s wheeled and flying robots will patrol your home

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Some people use a whole bunch of fixed cameras to monitor their homes, but Amazon has just announced a more flexible solution through wheeled and flying robots.

Both the $250 Drone Cam and $1,000 Rover Cam can respond to alarms or door and window sensor alerts in order to go check out what’s happening and send live video …

Amazon says they are the first two Ring Robotics products, with more likely to follow. Both are autonomous.

Ring Drone and Ring Rover are both powered by AI Pilot, a custom-built system that uses the devices’ cameras, specialized vision models, on-device sensors, and cloud processing to see, think, and navigate autonomously.

AI Pilot simplifies the customer experience, starting with setup. Simply carry Ring Drone through your space or let Ring Rover autonomously explore, and it turns what it sees into a floor plan. It intelligently identifies rooms and suggests patrol points: spots worth extra attention. This could be a side door, windows, a register, or a long corridor, helping eliminate guesswork while also allowing you to add additional points customized to your space and security preferences.

When a patrol or flight is initiated, AI Pilot plans the most efficient route to get to a preset point based on the map it’s created. From there, it reacts in real time—autonomously navigating around furniture, shelving, and any obstacle it encounters.

You can set either device to patrol on a fixed schedule, as well as activate them manually, but they will also automatically respond to sensor alerts and alarms.

When a Ring camera, sensor, or alarm is triggered, [either device] automatically [moves] to previously set patrol points to investigate […] Continuous person motion detection means Ring Rover can alert you and start recording the moment it detects motion in its view—even when docked.

The Ring Rover Cam will begin shipping on December 16 for $999.99, but you can preorder it today. The Drone Cam starts shipping in late January for $249.99, but there’s no preorder yet.

Buy These 6 Perennials “Bare-Root” This Fall – You'll Save Money and Enjoy Gorgeous Flowers for Years

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

The 7 Best Native Perennials for a Garden That Gets More Beautiful Every Year

Plant These 7 Flower Seeds in November for Gorgeous Early Blooms Next Year

These Tulips Look Just Like Peonies – and Their Ravishing Flowers Last for Months, Not Days

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

They might not look like much right now, but these unassuming bare roots hold the promise of a spectacular garden. Plant them now or in spring for years of joy.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When shopping for new perennials, you’ll often find the choice of potted or bare-root plants. Bare-roots are dug up while dormant and shipped without soil around their roots. Since they’re lighter, they’re easier and less expensive to ship – which is why many plants sold online arrive bare-root.

But bare-root planting isn't all about saving money. Planting during dormancy lets plants focus more energy on developing strong, healthy roots. Cooler temperatures and often rainier conditions are ideal for many bare-root plants, minimizing stress during the transfer.

While there are many reasons to add bare-root plants to your garden in fall or spring, it's best to give them four to six weeks to establish roots before the ground freezes. This is later than your first frost date, as the soil usually takes longer to freeze. Check when the ground is likely to freeze in your area before choosing and buying bare-root perennials.

If you're out of time for the season, late winter or early spring is another great time to do this. However, it's worth ordering your plants in fall, as many nurseries sell out of popular varieties well before spring. You can often order now for delivery at the right planting time.

When your perennials arrive, their roots should be wrapped in moist material. Keep them wrapped until you’re ready to plant to prevent the roots from drying out or becoming stressed. First, choose the right location based on the plant’s soil, moisture, and sunlight needs. Then, prepare the soil by pulling weeds, loosening it, and adding compost.

Once you’re ready to plant, unwrap the roots and inspect them. The roots should be firm and plump. Trim off any mushy, brittle, or moldy roots, but avoid cutting into healthy roots. If the roots aren’t very moist, soak them in water for 30 to 60 minutes before planting.

Plant the roots in a hole large enough to fit them and let them spread out, following the recommended planting depth for each plant. Before planting, sprinkle a mycorrhizal fungi inoculant, such as Xtreme Gardening Mykos Mycorrhizae from Amazon, directly over the roots and in the planting hole. These beneficial fungi form partnerships with the roots of many perennials, helping them absorb water and nutrients.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Backfill the hole with soil, gently firming it around the roots to remove any large air pockets. Give the plant a thorough watering to help settle the soil, and continue watering as needed to keep it moist until the ground freezes. Finally, apply a light layer of mulch around the plant to help retain moisture and protect the roots from temperature fluctuations over winter.

Peonies bloom with large, beautiful flowers in late spring or early summer, and they are among the longest-lived of all garden perennials. This makes bare-root peonies an excellent investment. Plant peonies in an area with fertile soil, excellent drainage, and full sun.

Fall is a good time for bare-root peonies. Plant them so the eyes, the small growth buds, sit 1 to 2 inches (2.5 to 5.0 cm) below the soil surface. Planting them too deeply will prevent flowering.

Eden Brothers currently has a sale on peony roots, and my pick is this beautiful Pastel Mix Collection. Or try the Jumbo Mix for the most flamboyant flowers.

Another popular flowering perennial, daylilies are adaptable, forgiving, and easy to grow. They flower in summer, with each bloom lasting only a day, but with an ongoing parade of new flowers for weeks.

Eden Brothers' Fragrant Rebloomer Mix is a great place to start, or for containers, the Stella de Oro Daylilies from Burpee are small but impactful.

Plant daylilies in full sun or partial shade in well-drained soil. They generally do well when planted as bare roots in fall, but allow them a few weeks to establish before the ground freezes over. Otherwise, wait until spring. Plant the crown no deeper than one inch (2.5 cm) below the soil.

This classic perennial blooms in late spring and early summer with some of the garden's most striking flowers. Their frilled petals on top of tall stalks come in many colors – I adore these pastel coral-pink Beverly Sills Irises from Burpee, as well as these pale blue Sea Double irises.

Bare-root bearded irises come as root rhizomes that you can plant four to six weeks before the first hard freeze. Plant them in full sun and soil that drains very well. If you live in a region with cold winters, timing is essential. If it’s too late now, wait until spring. When planting, lay the rhizomes horizontally in the soil, with the upper surface just barely covered.

Like irises, oriental poppies offer frilled, colorful, and striking flowers on stately stems. They add drama and structure to beds with late-spring and early-summer blooms in shades of bright orange and red, and even pink and white. They grow best in full sun and well-drained soil.

As long as they have time to establish, you can successfully plant bare-root poppies in fall, though these stunning Harlem Oriental Poppy Roots from Jackson & Perkins will ship in the spring. Take care when choosing a location for poppies. They develop long taproots that don’t transplant well.

Astilbe plants are known for their plumes of feathery flowers that bloom in shades of red, purple, pink, and white in summer. Astilbe thrives in partial shade, or even more shade, and rich, moist soil. Younique Pink Astilbe from Burpee is a lovely compact variety for containers, while Mighty Chocolate Cherry Astilbe from Jackson & Perkins reaches over 6 feet, making it a striking border option.

You can plant bare-root astilbes in fall if they have enough time to establish, or in the spring. Plant to a depth that keeps the crown at soil level.

This is another shade-friendly perennial suited to woodland gardens and beds with partial or full shade. Bleeding heart blooms in spring with characteristic heart-shaped flowers that hang from gracefully arching stems. Choose a spot with moist but well-drained soil.

Bleeding hearts don’t tolerate being moved very well, so choose your spot carefully. Get them in the ground in fall with plenty of time to establish. The crown should be one to two inches below the soil surface with the eyes facing up.

Eden Brothers sells Bleeding Heart Roots Mix to preorder for spring, or pick up a bleeding heart bare-root from Spring Hill Nurseries at Walmart for fall planting.

Bare-root perennials are a good investment if you take the time to plant them right. They cost less than potted plants and can give you years of reliable blooms with good care. If you’ve missed the fall window for planting these in your region, make your selections over the winter and order them for a spring planting, another excellent time for most bare-root plants.

Mary Ellen Ellis has been gardening for over 20 years. With degrees in Chemistry and Biology, Mary Ellen's specialties are flowers, native plants, and herbs.

Football Manager 27 Wonderkids: Best Young Players to Buy in FM27

You've loaded up a new game on Football Manager 27 and you have a vision: build the best young team in the world and dominate for years. Unless you have one of the best academies in football, the only way you can do this is by recruiting as many wonderkids as possible.

A wonderkid is a young talent who possesses high potential and, with the right attitude, support and training, will evolve into a world-class player. You're looking for the next Cristiano Ronaldo or Lionel Messi, two of the greatest football players of all time. You're scouting for youth prospects who have what it takes to become the next big thing in football, whether that's in the mould of a swashbuckling centre-back such as Paolo Maldini, a formidable goalkeeper like Manuel Neuer or an all-action midfield dynamo akin to the Ballon d'Or-winning Rodri.

Here, GIVEMESPORT brings you the best wonderkid goalkeepers, defenders, midfielders and strikers, aged 20 and under, based on potential ability.

(Key: CA = Current Ability, PA = Potential Ability)

German duo Dennis Seimen and Florian Hellstern are the two best young goalkeepers in Football Manager 27, closely followed by Japanese prospect Rui Araki. Of that trio, Araki is the most affordable, and could mature into one of the best goalkeepers in the world if nurtured well by the right manager.

Dutch shotstopper Quenten Attigah has a very high ceiling, but may take some time to reach his potential given that he is only 17, but if you want to land a talented young netminder who can do a job straight away, you should consider scouting Pierce Charles.

Romario Cunha would be a decent signing for a team looking to climb divisions and would be affordable too, while Brazilian youngster Joao Pedro (not that one) is also a bargan at under £1 million.

To get you started on your journey to world domination in EA Sports FC 27, here are the best young players to buy in the game

It will come as little surprise to learn that Pau Cubarsi is top of the pile when it comes to wonderkid defenders in FM27 and he is virtually not for sale with Barcelona placing a mammoth valuation on his head. So it will take a lot of money and a lot of convincing to land his services in the game.

Brighton's centre-back Luka Vuskovic, one of the nominees for the 2026 Golden Boy award, is a more achievable target, but he will still command a hefty fee. If you're limited when it comes to budget, have a look at Inter Milan's Leondaro Bovio or Coventry City's Stephen Mfuni. If you're in the market for a left-back, do what you can to sign Saba Kharebashvili, who is primed to be one of the best left-backs in the world, according to FM's stats gurus.

Who will be taking Desire Doue's crown in 2026?

Lamine Yamal is the player with the highest potential in the game, and the best midfielder, but, much like his compatriot and team-mate Cubarsi, is practically unattainable, unless you're a manager who is capable of unsettling big-name stars and convincing them to join your crusade. Yamal is joined by the likes of Estevao and Yan Diomande, as well as Rio Ngumoha and Ayyoub Bouaddi, all among the best young players in world football, at the top of the list.

A little bit further down in terms of potential ability is Luca Williams-Barnett, who is more suited to an attacking role, while Mahdi Nicoli-Jazuli could morph into one of the best Number 10s in the game. Reggie Walsh, meanwhile, will be up there with Bouaddi as one of the best defensive midfielders around, should he reach his potential.

Aston Villa's imposing young striker Brian Madjo looks well placed to grow into the best out-and-out strikers in the game alongside Endrick, who has been a fixture of these lists for the past few years. It is worth looking to France and Italy for upcoming wonderkid strikers, with 16-year-old Montpellier prospect Lacine Megnan appearing particularly impressive. Roma have Antonio Arena in their ranks, while AC Milan's Francesco Camarda boasts similar potential.

If you are managing a club that is somewhat strapped for cash, here is a selection of the top wonderkids valued at £10 million or less.

The aforementioned Megnan features, but the best of the bunch looks to be Joshua Abe, Liverpool's exciting young winger. The United States is represented by Christopher Cupps and Gabriel Wesseh, both of whom would represent decent business.

Of course, in some cases, a loan move might be the only option, but there is satisfaction to be had in playing a part in a player's development. Indeed, if a young starlet has a positive experience at your club, who is to say they won't be inclined to stick around, or return at a later date?

Visa adds Monad to stablecoin settlement network options

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Visa issuers and acquirers can now use Monad for stablecoin settlement, with privacy features planned for the coming months.

Payments company Visa has added the Monad blockchain network as an option to its stablecoin settlement program, allowing participating issuers and acquirers to settle through the network.

Monad joins a number of blockchains Visa already supports for the program, including Avalanche, Ethereum, Solana, Stellar, Circle's Arc, Base, Canton, Polygon and Tempo. Visa announced support for the latter five in April.

Blockchain settlement allows funds to move on weekends and holidays as well, compared with traditional card settlement, which runs on a five-business-day banking schedule, the Monad Foundation said.

"Our goal is to give our partners the flexibility to settle on the blockchain networks that best meet their needs," Dan Roesbery, Visa's vice president of global onchain partnerships and GTM, said in a statement. "Adding support for Monad expands the options available to our partners and is another step toward building a multi-chain settlement infrastructure for stablecoin-enabled payments."

Visa reported an annualized stablecoin settlement run rate of $20 billion as of September 2026, up more than 15x year over year. It also had more than 225 stablecoin-linked card programs live globally.

Monad is developing a privacy feature for institutions, expected to launch in the coming months. Called Private Settlement, it will let institutions keep sensitive transaction details private while allowing them to share those details with auditors or regulators when needed, the foundation said.

The feature is being built directly into Monad, so private transactions can use the same liquidity as other transactions on the network, the foundation said, adding that Monad can support this without slowing down settlement.

"The next phase will be defined by whether institutions can keep sensitive flows confidential without leaving open networks," Keone Hon, co-founder and general manager of the Monad Foundation, said in a statement.

Visa has also been exploring private settlement on Canton. In June, it announced a proof of concept with stablecoin issuer Brale to test settlement using its dollar-backed SBC token. The initiative focuses on how institutions can settle onchain while controlling who can see sensitive transaction data.

Steam now shows average Steam Deck framerates when browsing games, but Valve says it's considering expanding the feature to other hardware

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By providing average data for each game, customers now have a more complete picture".

Valve has released a new Steam update that adds Steam Deck framerate data to a game's store page, giving potential buyers an indication of how well a game runs on the handheld.

As per Valve's announcement post, average framerates are now listed in the Steam Deck Compatibility section of a game's store page, but you'll need to click Learn More to see it. Additionally, the feature is only available for games that have been tested for Deck compatibility - those with Verified, Playable, or Unsupported badges - so while it's not a complete list, it still covers thousands of titles.

According to Valve, average framerates are derived from players running the game on their Steam Decks, assuming they've opted in to provide framerate data. And the store widget also includes another helpful addition: a histogram displaying the percentage of data samples. This shows how often a particular framerate has been reported, and is useful in determining if the game's framerate is sporadic, such as due to stutters.

"The current Steam Deck Verified compatibility rating is based on standardised test criteria, but it may not match every customer's bar for acceptable gameplay," Valve explained of the new feature.

"This is especially true around performance - a game with an Unsupported rating due to unstable performance may be considered playable by some customers, and a Verified game may not reach a framerate that some customers prefer to play at. By providing average framerate data for each game, customers now have a more complete picture of how the game performs on Steam Deck and can make more informed purchase and play decisions."

Currently, Steam's framerate data feature only covers performance on Steam Deck, and for a very good reason: Valve's handheld has standardised hardware, so the results will be the same for all players. However, Valve says it intends to provide framerate data for more devices in the future, but it's not clear if that's ever going to include desktop PCs, considering the complex nature of their hardware configurations.

Atmos Global Concierge: Alaska Airlines’ New Invitation-Only Elite Status

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Alaska Airlines is increasingly becoming like the “big three” US carriers when it comes to its route network, as the airline expands globally, following its takeover of Hawaiian Airlines. Now the airline is doing another thing you’ll find at competitors, which is introducing an invitation-only elite status. Let’s take a look at what we know so far about Atmos Global Concierge, which is the name of this new status.

Some Atmos Rewards members on Reddit are reporting receiving an invitation for the carrier’s new status tier that has no published qualification requirements, and it’s above the Titanium tier. Those who have been awarded the status can keep it through December 31, 2027.

So, what are the perks of Atmos Global Concierge status?

We’ll have to of course wait some time to get firsthand reports of the experience, but as you’d expect, this is a lot more about better “soft” treatment, plus having top priority for everything. There’s nothing particularly remarkable about this status otherwise, but that’s not surprising, since that’s typically not the focus on invitation-only elite status.

It’s anyone’s guess what exactly it takes to get an invitation, as it’s a bit of a mystery, much like with American Concierge Key, Delta 360, and United Global Services. It’s possible that criteria differ based on where you mostly fly out of. It does seem like frequent international travel in business class is something that increases odds of eligibility.

Based on the reports so far, it almost seems like this status is being dangled as a “carrot” for those who have maybe taken a few long haul business class trips, in hopes of keeping them flying on Alaska and Hawaiian.

There’s a reason that airlines offer invitation-only elite status, and it’s kind of brilliant. The idea is to keep people on the “hamster wheel,” and keep them booking high yield tickets on an airline over competitors.

Airlines of course have elite tiers with published requirements, and once you qualify for your planned tier for a year, you might consider branching out and booking another airline. But when there’s invitation-only elite status, you don’t know what you actually need to earn it, so you’re more likely to keep flying with the airline. And if you’re spending other people’s money, you’re also more likely to make sure that ticket is expensive as it should be.

At the end of the day, these kinds of status tiers aren’t really about the exact details of the perks. On some level, they’re intended to stroke the egos of travelers, and that works. People get excited when they get to board a flight first, etc., and this whole concept plays into that.

Alaska Airlines has introduced a new invitation-only elite status, named Atmos Global Concierge. The idea is that this is targeted primarily at those flying a lot internationally, and it offers perks like concierge assistance, upgrade priority, some partner elite perks, and more.

With the way Alaska has grown, it’s not surprising to see the airline also copying the “big three” with a status tier like this.

What do you make of Atmos Global Concierge?

Membership is free and takes no more information than commenting as a guest. Earn status points for good contributions, and see fewer ads as your status grows.

Your feedback is important in helping us keep our community safe.

It's really weak, but Alaska doesn't have much to offer currently. None of their new lounges are open, which I would expect them to offer access to. Many of their partner lounges are not ideally located for their flyer base on West Coast either. Delta offers D1 lounge access domestically for FC tickets, AA gives Flagship lounge access. Skydance Earnings: Wall Street Is Skeptical So Far

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Analysts react to the first post-Warner Bros. acquisition quarterly results of David Ellison’s Hollywood giant, with one wondering how this merger can succeed when "so many other major media deals have failed."

Skydance’s first quarterly earnings report after the mega-acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery (WBD) is in. And Wall Street’s take on the results and especially the outlook for David Ellison’s Hollywood giant is … still under review.

The results, posted after the market close on Monday, came a year after Paramount’s first financials under the leadership of Ellison. On the earnings call, the Skydance chairman and CEO expressed bullishness on the creative potential of AI. And he addressed the surprise leadership change at DC Studios, with Marvel alum Jeremy Latcham replacing James Gunn and Peter Safran.

Skydance shares were down more than 2 percent to $8.50 in pre-market trading. So, what did Wall Street analysts take away from the entertainment conglomerate’s first financial and strategic update since the WBD deal close? The Hollywood Reporter is compiling key commentary and thoughts below.

Analyst: Laurent Yoon, BernsteinStock rating and price target: underperform, $6Takeaways: “Trailer Looks Good… Waiting for the Movie” was the title of Yoon’s report following the latest results. “The third quarter was more of a prologue than Chapter 1 of Skydance’s journey following the WBD acquisition,” he wrote. “Pro forma financials with WBD won’t be available until sometime before the fourth-quarter print, leaving us on the edge of how this story could unfold. There are more questions than answers, but we come out net positive.”One of the key questions, or developing stories, if you will, is the studio. “Legacy WBD seems likely to be a drag near-term, particularly in the fourth quarter,” the analyst concluded. “Studio performance has underperformed, with management flagging an up to a around $200 million fourth-quarter profit shortfall from WBD theatrical performance. WBD’s larger linear exposure also remains a likely drag, though it represents meaningful opportunity for operating expense synergies.”So, what will investors need to get more bullish? “The raw ingredients are there to build something better than the individual parts. And we wait for Chapter 1.”

Analyst: Michael Morris, Guggenheim SecuritiesStock rating and price target: neutral, no price targetTakeaways: “Skydance’s first report since closing the WBD acquisition showed continued progress at legacy Paramount and limited incremental detail on the combined company,” Morris wrote in his report entitled “Awaiting Takeoff.” He currently doesn’t have a price target on the stock, citing “the lack of historical pro forma disclosure and no third-quarter operating results for legacy Warner Bros. Discovery.” His rating “balances potential upside from synergies and streaming scale against execution risk and the combined company’s higher leverage.”The analyst also outlined what he is looking for now. “We believe the market has heard the strategy and needs evidence of progress in three areas: 1) a content slate that bolsters confidence in consumer demand for core franchises, 2) the net financial impact of combining HBO Max and Paramount+, beginning with the early 2027 bundle, and 3) management of the declining but cash-generative linear business.”Morris highlighted that Warner Bros. is a work in progress, highlighting that management called the studio on track “with the exception of the near-term film slate.” Noting an up to $200 million fourth-quarter profit impact from films already released this quarter and those still to come, the analyst described the DC Studios leadership change as “the first structural response.” And he added: “The 2027 Warner Bros. slate (Godzilla x Kong: Supernova, Man of Tomorrow, A Minecraft Movie Squared, the Ocean’s Eleven prequel, The Lord of the Rings: The Hunt for Gollum) is where Paramount’s greenlight and marketing discipline should show up.”

Analyst: Doug Creutz, TD CowenStock rating and price target: hold, $8Takeaways: Because the WBD deal closed after the end of the third quarter, the latest results reflect “Paramount Skydance standalone results,” the analyst emphasized, summarizing: “Paramount’s standalone third-quarter adjusted EBITDA of $1.0 billion beat our $947 million estimate on stronger DTC and Studios profit, though revenue of $7.03 billion was below our estimate of $7.13 billion.”The TD Cowen expert also noted that management reiterated its target of at least $6 billion in deal synergies within three years and “gave the first breakdown by category,” namely at least $2.2 billion from corporate overhead, real estate, and facilities, $1.4 billion from technology, $1.4 billion from procurement and vendor management, and $1.0 billion from marketing. About 30 percent of those synergies should be in place by the end of 2027.All that said, Creutz continues to have doubts, sharing this investment thesis: “The risks (leverage, integration) of the combination with WBD are high; we remain skeptical that Skydance management will be able to create value from this deal when so many other major media deals have failed.”

Analyst: Robert Fishman, MoffettNathansonStock rating and price target: neutral, $7Takeaways: “Ghosts of the Past, Present and Future” was the title of Fishman’s report, a timely Charles Dickens reference just before Skydance releases Ebenezer, starring Johnny Depp. “After last night’s earnings report, we get a first glimpse of how Skydance views its trajectory in relation to some … questions [we have previously outlined] but still lots of questions remain.”Among them is whether the merged company can balance cost cuts and growth investments. Looking at the areas that Skydance said it would find efficiencies in, the MoffettNathanson analyst offered: “In theory, savings in these areas should get the company to its synergy targets without dipping into its $30 billion-plus of content spending, allowing it to pay down debt without sacrificing growth. However, as we’ve seen with other media M&A, it is a lot harder to see these cost savings translate into real upside to EBITDA and cash flows, likely forcing the company to limit its content spend growth in the future.”And he highlighted a need for investor and analyst patience for a closer look under the hood until Skydance discloses pro forma financials for its business plus WBD early in 2027. For now, he left most of his estimates unchanged.

Here's what the red dot on your Apple Watch means and how to clear it

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you look at your Apple Watch and see a small red dot at the top of its screen, it's telling you one thing: you have notifications waiting for you. Apple sends notifications to your iPhone if it's unlocked, but if it is locked and your Watch is the one that's unlocked, it will send notifications to the wearable instead. That doesn't mean you'll always catch every single one of them as they appear, however. The red dot at the top shows up if you have notifications that you've yet to read and dismiss.

To view them, simply press and hold your watch face to open the Notification Center. Once it shows up, swipe down on your screen to scroll or turn the Digital Crown. You can read notifications from there by tapping on them or delete them by swiping left and then tapping the X button. If you want to quickly delete all your notifications, go to the Notification Center, swipe down or turn the Digital Crown until you get to the top and see the Clear All button. Tap it to remove all unread notifications from your Watch.

In case you'd rather not see the indicator altogether — maybe seeing it gives you anxiety or maybe you don't want to be disturbed — just open the Apple Watch app on your iPhone, tap Notifications and switch off Notifications Indicator.

You can also mute or switch off a certain app's notification's on your Watch instead. Swipe left on a specific notification and instead of dismissing it, tap on the ellipsis icon and then choose from among the options. You can mute notifications from that app for an hour or turn them off completely. You can also choose Turn off Time Sensitive if you want that app to deliver notifications immediately, even if you had switched on Focus mode, which is supposed to delay most notifications when it is on.

Most apps can only mirror your iPhone, so their notification settings are identical for both your handheld and your watch. In the Watch app under Notifications, you can go to Mirror iPhone Alerts and toggle the setting off for any of the apps listed in the section to stop getting alerts from them on your Watch. However, some apps allow you to set custom alerts. Those apps show the option "Custom" when you tap on them in the Watch app. On each individual app's settings page, you can choose Notifications Off or Send to Notification Center. The latter option will send alerts to your Watch's Notification Center without your Watch alerting you.

The appeal is simple: satisfying, screen-free fidgeting. Each piece is an individual keycap that clicks and clacks like a real computer keyboard. Kids clip them onto keychains, hang them off backpacks, or trade different characters with friends.

The Sanrio crossover serves as an easy stocking stuffer and an approachable introduction to the craze. Die-hard Sanrio fans who want a broader character roster right away will get much more out of the Series 1 Hello Kitty & Friends 7-pack. The biggest build in the lineup is the Clickeez Keyboard Mega Pack. Instead of just clipping onto a bag, the set comes with a full set of clicky keys and frames you can snap together into a mini keyboard for a desk or shelf.
