When rain returns after a drought, groundwater reserves don’t always get caught up before the next drought

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Most groundwater refills faster than it drains in a drought, but in about a quarter of places, the next drought hits before recovery ends.

When a drought breaks, the rain returns first, then the soil moisture, the plants and the rivers. Groundwater usually comes last, and in some places the next drought arrives before it has caught up.

A new global analysis suggests that, in most places, groundwater refills faster than it drains during a drought. But across about a quarter of the places studied, water levels tended to slip back into drought before they had fully recovered.

That matters because groundwater supplies roughly 35 percent of all the fresh water people withdraw, and it’s the reserve many communities rely on when rivers run low.

Hydrologist Sandra Hauswirth led the work with three colleagues at Utrecht University in the Netherlands. Their results come from a computer model rather than direct measurements.

It estimates how deep you’d have to dig to reach water, a level called the water table, month by month from 1960 to 2019.

This model divides the world’s land into squares about 0.6 mile (1 kilometer) across. It was developed at Utrecht’s Department of Physical Geography, and it tracks both the rain that soaks into soil and the water people pump out of it.

Checked against real wells, the model gave useful estimates at about 80 percent of wells shallower than about 200 feet (60 meters). For deeper wells, the figure was about 70 percent.

For every square and every calendar month, the team worked out how deep the water table usually was and how much that depth normally varied.

A drought started when the groundwater dropped so far below its usual level for that time of year that only about 16 percent of months would qualify. It ended when the water rose back above that line.

Droughts lasting two months or less were left out, because monthly data can’t capture them reliably. For the rest, the team measured how fast the water fell, how fast it rose again, and what it did in the six months afterward.

Climate did more than anything else to set the pace of recovery. Places where a lot of rain soaks into the ground, such as the Amazon and Southeast Asia, recovered quickly. Their droughts also tended to be shorter and more frequent.

Dry regions recovered slowly. The slow places included Australia, the Middle East and Central Asia, southern Africa, southern South America and the U.S. Midwest.

During drought recovery, the groundwater at a typical location rose about 0.7 inches (1.8 centimeters) a month. In the fastest places it climbed as much as about 3 feet (1 meter) a month.

Those raw speeds partly reflect how much a water table swings in an ordinary year. So the team also compared each place with its own normal range, and the split between wet and dry regions became sharper.

Average speed told only part of the story. Two places could recover at the same average rate and still behave very differently during a drought.

Hauswirth hadn’t expected recovery to vary so much within a single region. In an interview with Earth.com, she said, “I was surprised to see that the groundwater recovery pattern was not necessarily homogeneous within a region and could vary quite a bit locally.”

To capture those differences, the team sorted every location by how its droughts unfolded. About 57 percent were resilient: on average, the water came back faster than it had dropped. Another 26 percent were unstable, slipping back into drought before they had fully recovered.

That makes the unstable group the second largest. The researchers described its behavior as even more critical than that of the vulnerable group, where the water fell quickly and climbed back slowly.

The unstable group covered about 15 percent of places, and only around 2 percent were stable, with the fall and the rise at about the same speed.

Unstable places weren’t clustered in one part of the world, and they weren’t limited to heavily pumped areas. Their droughts were shorter, milder and more frequent than in any other group, often with little time to refill in between.

Terrain set the vulnerable places apart. In every region the team examined up close, they turned up at higher elevations, where the ground stores less water and the water table swings more widely.

In Europe, some may be linked to limestone country, where water drains through cracks and caves.

The close-ups included California, Spain, India and the North China Plain, all places known for heavy groundwater use. In Spain, the vulnerable and unstable spots shared similar pumping patterns. In California, pumping appeared to affect more of the categories.

Sorting places this way, Hauswirth said, made it clear that local features “are an important part of the drivers and should not be underestimated.”

For water managers in vulnerable regions, her advice starts with knowing where the water comes from. Where possible, she said, they should map how much of local demand and supply is covered by groundwater.

That “could help us better understand when groundwater resources are overexploited,” she told Earth.com, and when a wet spell could help restore levels, possibly by cutting back on pumping.

The authors name their own blind spots. They filtered out places where water tables have fallen steadily for decades, often from heavy pumping, because a long decline makes single droughts hard to pick out. Those places may be underrepresented in the results.

Every number here is also a model’s estimate. This model performed well against real wells, but global groundwater models have both overestimated and underestimated water levels in the past.

And because the team averaged 60 years together, the results can’t show whether recovery is speeding up or slowing down.

“I want to dive deeper into how these recovery patterns have changed over time, as the current work has mainly focused on the spatial pattern,” Hauswirth said.

Next, the team wants to pair the four categories with projections of a warmer climate. That could show which places are at risk of shifting from resilient toward vulnerable or unstable.

The full study was published in the journal Environmental Research: Water.

eToro’s Q3 profit plunges 74% as weaker crypto trading weighs on earnings

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

October crypto trades also fell 62% year over year to 2 million, while the trading platform announced a $250 million share buyback program.

Crypto and stock trading platform eToro (ETOR) reported a 74% year-over-year decline in third-quarter net income to $15 million, as weaker crypto trading weighed on its earnings.

The Nasdaq-listed company's net contribution fell 13% to $186 million from $215 million a year earlier, primarily due to reduced crypto trading activity, the firm said in its latest earnings release on Tuesday.

Adjusted EBITDA fell 56% to $34 million from $78 million in the same quarter last year. Revenue from crypto assets dropped to $1.35 billion from $3.97 billion a year earlier, and the associated cost of crypto asset revenue was $1.29 billion, compared with $3.89 billion for the same period in 2025.

In August, The Block reported that eToro had posted $53 million in second-quarter net income on $229 million in net contribution. Both figures were down from its record first quarter, although net contribution grew year over year on stronger equities trading. However, its first-quarter results had already shown a 50% year-over-year drop in crypto trades.

Beyond the third quarter, eToro's October figures showed crypto trades falling 62% year over year to 2 million. The average invested amount per crypto trade also declined 29% to $228 during the month, the firm said on Tuesday. Trading activity was also lower across equities, commodities, and currencies, with 48.2 million trades recorded during the month, down 22% from October 2025.

The platform ended September with 4.39 million funded accounts, an 18% increase from a year earlier, and assets under administration were unchanged at $20.8 billion. eToro's board also approved a $250 million share buyback program, with purchases subject to market conditions and the company's liquidity requirements.

Two years after the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star broke up with Chase McWhorter following seven years of marriage, she has filed for divorce, according to court documents obtained by The Sun.

In a Nov. 9 TikTok video, McWhorter—with whom she shares two kids—reacted to the news, joking, "You may have won the battle, but I will win the war."

"Obviously, I've known about this," he clarified. "Mostly took so long because of the laziness. Really, it's really all you can chalk it up to. Biggest supporter, I promise. I'm not gonna be a loser anymore."

In fact, McWhorter confirmed that he hasn't "dilly-dallied" with any of Hope's friends in the last eight months after previously kissing her costar Jessi Draper and briefly dating Layla Taylor.

"That is the bare minimum, I understand that," he teased. "But also, let's celebrate progress. Wish you the best."

The 31-year-old also emphasized that Hope's filing wasn't a shock to him. Referencing The Sun's coverage, he noted, "This is not how I found out."

Meanwhile, Hope—now in a relationship with Montana Boyz' Kaleb Winterburn—responded to his video with equal playfulness.

"'Laziness,'" she teased under the video. "Yeahhh i'm the one that had to fill out all the paperwork and such. If you read it, you would know i took everything. sorry this is how you're finding out."

Leaning into the bit, McWhorter responded to her comment, "This is fine just let me have some of ur cosmetic PR so I can feel cute. Thx."

Although the exes felt it was best to part ways in 2024, their mutual respect is anything but a secret.

"The reasons why we decided to split were things that were issues far before that," Hope, 28, explained on the Weekly Trash podcast in August 2024, noting that the infamous MomTok soft swinging scandal was not the reason. "That might not have helped, for sure, but it definitely did not play a part in the decision process."

And their co-parenting is sweeter than a dirty soda, even with the natural ups and downs of such a dynamic.

"With co-parenting, you're always having to work on it," she reflected. "The thing is that if we got along perfectly all the time and it was just rainbows and butterflies, we probably wouldn't have gotten divorced."

"The thing with him and I," she continued, "we've always had a pretty good foundation of a friendship prior to even having a romantic relationship. I feel like that's something that set us up for some level of success when it comes to co-parenting."

What Happens When Stars Break Character On SNL? That Time Ryan Gosling Made It Happen

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Upcoming SNL Hosts And Musical Guests, The Saturday Night Live Season 52 Cast And More

SNL’s Ashley Padilla Felt ‘Guilty’ About One Aspect Of The Show. Kristen Wiig Changed That

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

There is a time-honored tradition of Saturday Night Live cast members and hosts “breaking” on the show, when they just can’t contain their laughter. It’s often some of the best moments of any episode, but it can really stress out cast members when it happens. Ashley Padilla recently opened up about it and how she reacted to a specific example of that happening to her while working in a sketch with host Ryan Gosling and fellow castmate Mikey Day.

In a recent interview, Padilla talks about how she writes sketches and thinks of them like a song, with beats to follow. If every sketch has a rhythm to follow, fellow actors going out of their way to make her laugh is like someone banging out of rhythm with a cowbell. It happened to Padilla in a sketch called “Cyclops” last season while Gosling was hosting (and it wasn't the only time in that episode). She talks about how it affected her and what goes through her head when it happens to her. Padilla told Time:

It depends because if someone is making you laugh, the laughter's coming from like, ‘What are you doing?’ I felt that in Cyclops. There was this like — Mikey Day [and] Ryan Gosling were just messing with me like two older brothers and I was laughing out of sheer — I couldn't believe what was happening. I was like, ‘How dare you guys. Stop it.’ They were making me laugh, you know?.... That was not planned.

In the sketch, you can see Padilla begging the two of them to stop, though they are clearly entertained enough to keep it going. Honestly, they pretty much abused her, though Day did (kind of) warn her about what they would be up to. Padilla explains:

Mikey said, ‘We might come at you once.’ I went, ‘OK, bring it on,’ you know? That, where I couldn’t get a word out, that was me begging. I have a cue card in front of me. I got to read this thing. I couldn’t believe [it]. I was laughing so hard. So that was because the audacity from these two was killing me.

I have to be honest here: the sketch is actually much funnier with Padilla trying as hard as she can not to lose it as Day and Gosling keep moving towards her, as she literally begs them to “stop.” I’m not sure the sketch would’ve been funny otherwise. Clearly Day and Gosling (plus Kenan Thompson) are having a blast hazing Padilla. They even cheer when it seems they got her to break. Lorne Michaels might not like it (though that might be a misconception), and the cast members get frustrated by it, but as a member of the audience, I love it.

Saturday Night Live Season 52 is well underway on the 2026 TV schedule, and cast members have been breaking for decades. Gosling himself broke in a previous hosting gig, so it’s not like it’s ever going to stop. I hope it never does anyway. Heck, I’d love to see it happen more often!

Poland’s lower house backs sanctions bill covering crypto freezes

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The bill is now before the Senate and would broaden the grounds for adding people and companies to Poland’s national sanctions list.

Poland’s lower house passed a bill that would broaden the country’s sanctions framework and explicitly include crypto assets among the funds authorities can freeze when imposing sanctions.

The Sejm approved the government-backed Act on Restrictive Measures on Friday by 240 votes to 202, with one abstention, and forwarded it to the Senate on Monday, according to official parliamentary records. The bill explicitly includes crypto assets, as defined under the European Union’s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA), among financial funds that authorities could freeze when imposing sanctions.

Poland’s interior minister would oversee the national sanctions list, with sanctions-listing decisions taking effect the day after authorities publish a designation. Affected individuals and companies could challenge the decision in administrative court, but the bill allows enforcement before a court ruling.

Poland already maintains a national sanctions list under a 2022 law aimed at countering support for Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. The bill would expand the grounds for imposing national sanctions to include certain foreign interference and information manipulation that pose a serious threat to national security.

The Senate has 30 days to approve, amend or reject the legislation. If it takes no action, Poland’s Constitution treats the bill as approved in the form passed by the Sejm, allowing it to proceed to the president for consideration.

New framework grades AI jailbreaks across capability gains, scope, and how easy they are to deploy

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

One ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In recent months, high-profile headlines about AI models letting loose from their safeguards dominated headlines. Now, the Frontier Model Forum (FMF) has released a new Jailbreak Rating Framework that aims to track how easy it might be for a given AI model to get around safeguards. The framework also estimates the scale of the collateral damage, should an AI model wreak cyber-havoc across the internet.

The project started in 2023 with the support of four founding companies: Anthropic, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI. The published Jailbreak Rating Framework is a rough analog to the well-known CVSS security rating system, but specifically targeted at classifying the type and severity of LLM jailbreaks across various axes and multiple frontier LLM providers.

Many AI companies and evaluation entities already have their own rating systems, but the FMF intends for its scale to be a cross-vendor standard that teams developing patches can rely on.

The FMF's approach is to have a classification method for jailbreaks that can make a model perform cyber-warfare operations beyond what's already out, across both the regular and black market. Version 1.0 of the proposal is a work-in-progress, and the group is taking comments and suggestions on it until December 21, noting that relying on its system in the meantime is inadvisable.

Right now, the framework has four scoring components, split across two main categories: severity, measuring how serious a jailbreak technique is, and likelihood, or how easy it is to find and weaponize it.

A combined final rating adds the scores obtained from the two categories into a final low-to-critical rating. The scientists note that the capability gain is the most important dimension and acts as both a floor and a ceiling for the remaining scores — a jailbreak that doesn't offer results beyond what can already be obtained via other means is meaningless within the purview of the grading system.

The individual ratings pertain to capability gains, capability scope, ease of deployment, and ease of discovery. They're all graded from zero to three, though the FMF stresses that a rating of zero doesn't mean a jailbreak is harmless or ought to be ignored — rather, it means the flaw should be addressed by standard practices like the platform's policies and standard cybersecurity ratings.

An important feature is that the FMF's baseline implicitly changes as models evolve, meaning that a grade of zero for a jailbreak in 2026 likely isn't going to mean the same thing as it would in 2028, or isn't better than alternative tools. A capability-gain rating of zero means that the jailbreak under analysis doesn't offer any improvement beyond what can already be done with existing models or tooling right off the bat.

For example, jailbreaking a model to perform a DDoS on a site isn't all that helpful to an attacker, since there are a number of black-market sites for that — or even other LLMs with fewer safeguards. This doesn't mean the jailbreak isn't handy, only that it's no better than the alternatives. However, the rating can be higher if the attacker can leverage the jailbreak to get a better DDoS than they could otherwise shop for.

On the other end of the capability-gain scale, three means that a jailbroken model can perform cyber-attacks with capabilities similar to those of a model operating without safeguards.

The capability scope measurement is easier to interpret: zero means that the jailbreak only works for one specific action or target and can't be tweaked for other applications, or in other words, is a one-trick pony. A jailbreak gets a rating of three when it can, without specific redesign, make a model perform work across at least two entirely unrelated categories of cyber-warfare — like getting the model to both autonomously discover vulnerabilities or, separately, write malware.

For ease of deployment, the FMF considers the amount of human interaction, or the degree of automation that the jailbreak allows. A zero means that the jailbreak must be steered by humans with a high degree of knowledge, providing additional instructions to be effective, while a three means that, with little effort, it can be used by a novice and results in an automated cyber-warfare task.

Last but by no means least, ease-of-discovery is how hard it is to find the jailbreak. At a zero, the attacker must spend copious amounts of money on inference tokens, need elevated access (like a privileged account given to a white-hat hacker), or specialized infrastructure like a legion of AI servers. A rating of three simply means that anyone can look up the technique and use it.

If all of this sounds familiar, you may be thinking of Anthropic's safeguard-breakage classifiers the company proposed in July 2026, after an incident that led to Claude Fable 5 being temporarily unavailable. Much like the FMF's effort, Anthropic's system would also use a moving baseline and only consider jailbreaks that improve on cyber-warfare capabilities already available.

That document is strikingly similar to FMF's proposal. Anthropic is an FMF founding member, and as such may have had a hand or two in developing the new standard — though we have no confirmation either way.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

“You Can Have My Middle Seat!” Frontier Passenger Swipes Another Man’s Aisle Seat To Sit With His Wife, Crew Does Nothing

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A Frontier Airlines passenger reportedly found another man sitting in his assigned aisle seat because that man wanted to sit beside his wife. The proposed solution? The displaced passenger could take a middle seat two rows behind him. According to a witness, a flight attendant became involved but did not resolve the situation, and the passenger ultimately gave up. If that account is accurate, the airline failed him and owes him more than an apology.

The seating dispute occurred aboard Frontier flight 1550 from Atlanta (ATL) to Jacksonville (JAX).

According to the witness, a passenger arrived at seat 28C and found another man occupying it beside his wife. When he pointed out the assignment, the seated passenger reportedly responded:

“Well, I wanted to sit next to my wife. You can have my middle seat (30E).”

The witness says a flight attendant heard what had happened, but the man remained in the aisle seat. The displaced passenger eventually accepted 30E, saying:

The original Reddit post has since been deleted, though the discussion remains. This is an unverified witness account on reddit…it might be all malarkey…but at worst it makes for an interesting theoretical discussion.

I have no objection to asking another passenger to switch seats. Sometimes couples end up separated despite trying to arrange seats together. But there is a proper way to ask. Start in your assigned seat, explain the situation, and accept the answer. Offering a comparable or better seat certainly helps and is something I view as a starting point.

Taking someone’s aisle seat before he arrives, then informing him that he can have your middle seat instead, turns a request into a confrontation: wanting to sit beside your spouse does not give you authority to make that decision for him.

Why not ask the person beside your assigned middle seat whether they would like to move forward and take your wife’s seat? That would at least give someone a genuine choice while leaving the unwilling passenger out of it.

But the broader problem is the alleged response by Frontier flight attendants. Especially on Frontier, which has totally unbundled fares and has made seat selection a major part of the booking process. The practice of charging for seat selection makes indifference to these disputes even harder to defend. Still, I’m suspicious because even on Frontier I find it difficult to fathom that the crew could reasonably leave the rightful occupant to negotiate for it.

I recognize that airlines sometimes must move passengers for operational or safety reasons. But that is quite different than allowing one traveler’s preference to override another’s confirmed seat assignment merely because he sat down first. If the witness’s account captures the full exchange, the straightforward response would have been to check the boarding passes and ask the man to return to his seat or be ejected from the flight. The crew could then explore a voluntary swap if someone wanted to help.

It is easy to read this story and declare that you would never surrender your seat.

I would have calmly asked the flight attendant to confirm my assignment and help me sit there. If that went nowhere, I would have asked whether another crew member or a gate agent could assist. And yes, I would have filmed it all.

But I also understand the exhaustion behind “I just want to get home.” Sometimes a passenger has already endured enough that a middle seat seems preferable to another argument.

That does not make him foolish and certainly does not establish that the swap was fair.

Frontier (or any carrier) should not create a situation in which the most persistent passenger gets what he wants while the more accommodating one absorbs the inconvenience. That rewards precisely the behavior crews should be discouraging.

This account comes from a witness, and the original post is no longer available. We should leave room for details that may be missing and be open to the possibility that this story is a fabrication.

But the principle is simple and it’s still worth repeating: you may ask someone to switch seats so you can sit with your spouse. You may not decide that his aisle seat is yours and that he should accept your middle seat instead.

When that happens, the crew should help resolve it. Airlines cannot spend the booking process telling us how valuable seat assignments are, then treat those assignments as optional once we board.

Love Is Blind Season 11 star Rob Feeley is breaking his silence after a truly messy season. “Now that the reunion has aired and this chapter comes to a close, I’ve been reflecting on everything this experience has taught me,” Rob wrote on Instagram. “I’m truly grateful for the people I’ve met, the memories made, and the personal growth that came with both the incredible moments and the challenges. Looking back, there are moments I wish I had handled differently. There were times when I felt overwhelmed and let my emotions get the best of me. While those moments don’t reflect the person I strive to be, I recognize that they happened, and I take responsibility for how I reacted and where I could have communicated better.”

Shopify Noses Further Into Ad Tech With Campaign Autopilot Expansion

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

No major retail media player has taken as circumspect an approach to advertising revenue as Shopify.

As the payment processor and site infrastructure for so many online sellers, and with the greatest supply of such first-party data outside of Amazon and Walmart, Shopify holds a unique key for some of the biggest ad platforms.

Initially, the company forged data partnerships with other big platforms so they might target and attribute based on its data – but blindly, with no retargetable ID. Then, Shopify created addressable audience segments and used its data science to create audience segments for other platforms.

In the past quarter, the company has taken its next important step with a product called Campaign Autopilot, Samir Pradhan, Shopify VP of product for merchant marketing, told AdExchanger.

Unsurprisingly, it’s an agentic, AI-driven optimization product. Akin to how other walled garden AI ad products work, merchants set the price they’re willing to pay per sale, including some further controls, such as having a higher incentive for new customers or being able to target by region or time of day.

The current Campaign Autopilot partner platforms are Meta, Microsoft Advertising and Snapchat, with X in the pipeline, Pradhan said. Email marketing and ads on Shop, the Shopify-owned app that aggregates its merchant network, are also available.

And in the past few weeks, the company has added SEO optimization to Campaign Autopilot, he said. “That is our first foray, so to speak, outside of ads toward this broader vision of helping automate more of a merchant marketing.”

(“We don’t currently have the open web,” though. Womp.)

The easy-to-activate advertising solution is important to new merchants, according to Pradhan. Sometimes an ecommerce seller is an expert in one or more channels. But often the entire category is unfamiliar. Small sellers (businesses of one or a few people) aren’t going to hire pro marketers, but they understand the importance of advertising as a core competency.

Campaign Autopilot is a “risk-free ad product,” Pradhan said.

Other big ecommerce marketplaces and retailer or merchant platforms all charge per impression and/or for clicks. They might optimize toward a sale, he said, but it’s not what you actually buy. With Autopilot, though, “Shopify takes that media risk,” he said. The merchant only pays per sale.

There are other guardrails, too, he said. For instance, a campaign can be capped (which sounds commonsensical but has painfully surprised many ecommerce advertisers on other platforms) or an entire channel avoided. And Shopify is working on more “domain-specific controls” (contextual advertising, in other words) for advertisers who want to fine-tune their own campaigns.

Some merchants are sophisticated marketers who’ve honed their own models; others have never spent a dollar on advertising. Pradhan said the idea is to meet a “a broad swath of what marketers try to do from a guardrail perspective.”

Even for veteran ecommerce marketers, though, the industry overhaul to agentic and AI-based ad platforms has happened quickly. For Shopify, he said, “preserving that merchant trust and then making them feel in control was a big part of the ethos of developing this product.”

OpenAI’s head of monetization, Benji Shomair makes the case for ads as a way to bring ChatGPT to more people, with user trust taking priority over advertiser demands.

In Q3, The Trade Desk beat the admittedly pessimistic revenue forecast it set for itself earlier this year. But growth has stalled.

Viant is doubling down on CTV with its $20 million Waymark acquisition, betting that AI-generated ads can bring more advertisers to streaming TV.

TIME is using AI agents to draft proposals and troubleshoot campaigns – but not to replace the human relationships that drive ad sales.

Why the midterms may not spell doom for ad tech consolidation. Plus, how sports leagues are putting ad revenue before player safety.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

STAT+: Dutch antitrust regulators probe Biogen over test for its MS drug

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Ed’s stories explore prescription drug pricing, affordability and access, as well issues surrounding patents, litigation, and legislation. He is also the author of the morning Pharmalittle newsletter and the afternoon Pharmalot newsletter.

Dutch anti-trust regulators are investigating allegations that Biogen may have abused its dominant position in the market for multiple sclerosis treatments and associated blood tests.

The Netherlands Authority for Consumers and Markets, also known by its Dutch acronym ACM, is probing whether the company reserved a test for patients who are treated with Tysabri, its MS drug, and refusing to make the test available to patients on rival medications, such as Tyruko, a biosimilar version that is sold by Sandoz.

Patients who are treated with Tysabri, Biogen’s biggest-selling medicine, must be regularly tested for the risk of developing progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy, an infection caused by a virus that may be fatal or result in severe disability. Biogen developed a test for the virus and provides it free of charge — and exclusively — to patients receiving Tysabri.

Bayern won’t sign Rio Ngumoha after missing their chance in the summer – opinion

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Bayern Munich are reportedly confident that there is a deal to be done with Liverpool for talented youngster Rio Ngumoha.

The Reds were understood to be the subject of some probing from the Bavarian giants, with discussions said to have taken place with the winger’s brother.

Whether Liverpool feel the same way… well, let’s just be clear on this: they don’t.

As Mick Brown noted in his latest report with Football Insider: “From what I hear, Bayern Munich are pretty confident there is a deal to be done.

“When there’s all this talk about making contact with his agents, speaking to the player, what it might cost, that stuff doesn’t happen unless they think they can do a deal.

“Whether Liverpool feel the same way, I have my doubts, because he has such obvious potential and Andoni Iraola has been pretty clear he is part of his plans.

“I don’t think he’s the finished article, but not many players are at 18, there are still areas of his game where he has to improve, so maybe that’s where the speculation comes from.”

Mick goes on to note that the Reds are currently giving Ngumoha everything he could realistically hope for at an elite European outfit. There is a pathway to the senior team and he’s already picked up 642 minutes (Transfermarkt) of action in the famous red shirt this season.

On top of that, his all-round game is being carefully developed, with appearances roughly split between both flanks.

And most importantly? Liverpool aren’t burning the player out — Andoni Iraola is managing the teenager’s minutes, rotating alongside Bradley Barcola, Victor Munoz and Cody Gakpo.

So, Bayern may feel a deal can be done, but realistically, can they offer the 18-year-old substantially more than he’s already receiving at Liverpool?

It’s worth pointing out that, as is indeed the case across Europe’s biggest outfits, Bayern’s shortlist is far from limited to Liverpool’s No.73. Names like Antonio Nusa and Oskar Pietuszewski are also highly rated by the club, with the former thought to be well-admired by Bayern chief Max Eberl.

But the important distinction to make here is that Ngumoha will not come cheap – far from it, compared to the two names alone we’ve just listed. And if Bayern already valued the versatile winger at €70m [£58.3m] in the summer, can you imagine how much his value has skyrocketed since becoming an important part of Andoni Iraola’s Liverpool squad? We’re surely talking in the territory of at least upwards of £7om, at a conservative estimate.

On that basis, options like Pietuszewski – who reportedly has a €60m [£50.1m] release clause at FC Porto – surely start to look more feasible for a club like Bayern that doesn’t necessarily have the same level of cash to splash as its Premier League counterparts.

California Voters Reject ‘Billionaires Tax’ Amid Fears of an Exodus of Wealth from the State

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

California voters have rejected a proposed wealth tax, which could have meant a one-time influx of billions for the state but threatened to trigger an exodus of billionaires in the state.

The ballot measure, one of several housing issues Californians considered, is projected by the Associated Press to narrowly fail, with 53% voting against the measure with most votes counted a week after Election Day. Proposition 40 would have created a one-time 5% wealth tax on individuals with a net worth above $1 billion.

The Service Employees International Union–United Healthcare Workers West, which backed the measure, said it could have offset federal funding cuts. It estimated the one time tax could have brought in $100 billion in one-time revenue.

But the measure could have affected about 200 people, and billionaires in the state have put significant money toward fighting it. The California Budget & Policy Center estimates they hold a combined $2 trillion in wealth. Proposition 40 excludes real property holdings from determining an individual's net worth.

State leaders say tax could trigger some of those wealthy people to flee the state and take their spending with them. The National Taxpayers Union Foundation estimates California could collect $40 billion from the tax. But it could lose $25 billion in other taxes if wealthy people leave.

Governor Gavin Newsom opposed the measure. "This will be defeated, there's no question in my mind," Newsom told the New York Times, as it reported that several wealthy people had begun moving assets out of the state.

Palantir and PayPal co-founder Peter Thiel and Google co-founders Larry Page and Sergey Brin all have reportedly moved some assets to Florida. Voters in that state approved a measure on Tuesday that would reform its property tax system, potentially setting it up to be yet more attractive.

However, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg had pledged to remain in California, where he has spent more than $112 million assembling a megacompound in Palo Alto. Earlier this year, Zuckerberg reached an agreement with Meta board to remain in California regardless of the outcome of Prop 40. He would have faced a potential tax bill of some $12.5 billion if the measure had passed.

Taxes raised by Prop 40 would have gone to health care, education, or food assistance. But the state legislature would have had discretion to determine how those funds are used.

The California Association of Realtors President Tamara Suminski said at an elections forum last month that it opposed the measure. CAR is concerned about the potential loss of tax revenue and the state's economic competitiveness, she said.

"Prop 40 is just a bad idea," Jennifer Barrera, President & CEO, California Chamber of Commerce, said at the event. "Billionaires are the individuals who have the ability to move quickly, they have the ability to live outside the state, they don't have to be in California. And yet they pay a tremendous amount of revenue to our general fund if they're living here."

Two other items on the ballot, Proposition 41 and 42 both aim to block or counter Proposition 40, but their language could have much broader implications, Barrera said.

But even if Proposition 40 were to pass, it would invite years of litigation, said Joe Bishop‐​Henchman, executive vice president at National Taxpayers Union Foundation. Prop 40 applies to all of a California-domiciled billionaire's holdings across the globe, inviting Constitutional challenges.

And then there's the matter of actually calculating net worth. Wealthy individuals hold assets that can be hard to value, such as patents, artwork, and stakes in private companies. So, would likely take years of expensive legal process to determine how much to tax them.

"The Franchise Tax Board currently has no infrastructure for valuing these types of assets," Bishop‐​Henchman said. "Valuing these kinds of assets will entail years of costly litigation on both ends and establishment of an extensive and costly administrative apparatus for an ostensibly 'one-time' tax."

Californians meanwhile also voted on a set of other measures. Voters passed Proposition 1 to authorize California to issue $11.25 billion in bonds to support housing affordability programs. Co-chairs of its organizing campaign Chione Flegal and Ray Pearl called the win "a resounding show of support for affordable housing."

Voters also approved Proposition 37, which creates a $25 billion loan program for middle-income buyers to purchase new homes. Realtor.com economist Joel Berner said this could be a boon for new home sales. But he cautioned it might not touch the more expensive parts of the state.

"The complicating factor here is that the major California metro areas are short on newly built homes below $1.5 million, and the ballot measure does nothing to address the supply of these homes, only bolstering demand for them," Berner said.

"Proposition 37 has the potential to help prospective California homebuyers, but only if they can find new homes to buy," he said.

The California Association of Realtors, a major supporter of Proposition 37, cheered the result.

“This measure will help create more housing, provide low-cost down payment options and give teachers, nurses, firefighters and other hardworking Californians a better opportunity to own a home in the communities they serve," Suminski said. "We look forward to working with stakeholders to bring Proposition 37 to life and deliver on its promise for California families.”

But, voters rejected the controversial Proposition 45. It would have set timelines on environmental reviews for some projects under the California Environmental Quality Act, or CEQA.

California earned an F grade on the Realtor.com® State-by-State Housing Report Card. It tracks how effectively each state balances affordability and new construction.

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

ABC Is Failing ‘High Potential’ and ‘The Rookie’ Once Again

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

ABC's fall procedural lineup has just two weeks left until it airs its mid-season finales and goes on hiatus for the winter, but the premiere dates for the spring lineup have only just been announced. Like 9-1-1, 9-1-1: Nashville, Grey's Anatomy, and R.J. Decker, ABC's lineup of procedurals premiering their new seasons this January all concluded their most recent seasons last spring. Whereas fans of the aforementioned shows only had to wait for the length of the standard summer hiatus, though, fans of the second group of shows will have had to wait up to nine months by the time they return.

For a show like Will Trent, which has been on this timeline since its debut, this is to be expected. The Rookie and High Potential, on the other hand, initially got to air in both the fall and the spring, and have since been relegated to just the latter. For two of ABC's most popular shows, it's already disappointing that they only get to air for a few months of the year. Now, though, there are just under two months until these shows return the first full week of January, and it's frustrating that their return dates have not been announced until last week.

The Rookie has been on the spring release schedule for three seasons now, but High Potential has only just been moved to this lineup. It's a particularly jarring choice for High Potential, considering the series is only on its third season, and the show's second season had monumental records of success. Just as the series was really gaining momentum, it was put on break for nine months, leaving fans to speculate on their own about the fallout of the Season 2 finale's intense cliffhanger ending. Fans of The Rookie, meanwhile, may be used to this by now, but it is still frustrating to watch a beloved Network TV show only air for about a third of the year, then go on break for the rest of it.

The Rookie started as a show about a man starting over at forty-five and turned into a proper ensemble drama somewhere around season three. Here is which of them you are, and the case for yours being the best of the four.

You started late and you are not embarrassed about it. Nolan walks into the academy at forty-five surrounded by people half his age, and the show is honest that this is both his biggest disadvantage and the only reason he is any good at the job.

You are better at this than people expect and you intend to keep proving it. Chen goes from rookie to the best undercover officer in the division, which works because she is genuinely good at becoming somebody else and genuinely bad at pretending she does not care.

You are hard on people because you have decided that is what caring looks like. Bradford is introduced as the worst training officer in the building and slowly revealed as the one who takes the job most seriously, which are the same fact seen from two angles.

You said what you wanted and then you went and got it. Lopez wanted detective from the first episode, said so out loud, and got there by being better at the work than the people who were quieter about wanting it.

There are a lot of benefits to being a fan of a Network TV show, from getting to watch the episodes live alongside other fans to getting to speculate each week about what will happen next. The best part, though, is loving a show with a predictable release schedule. When series then start changing their release schedule to only air for half the time, it is incredibly disappointing to fans. It means potentially fewer episodes for the new season, as well as a much longer wait in between seasons, dampening some of the excitement and anticipation earned by the finales of the previous seasons.

In a perfect world, ABC would move High Potential and The Rookie back to its fall release schedule, but as it stands, the network just needs to do better by the procedurals in its spring lineup. Both of these shows excellently set up suspense ahead of their new seasons by ending their most recent ones with massive cliffhangers after two explosive episodes. For The Rookie, the fan-favorite pairing of Lucy Chen (Melissa O'Neil) and Tim Bradford (Eric Winter) finally got engaged in a beautiful beach proposal, only to both get abducted seconds later. High Potential, on the other hand, introduced another new twist in the ongoing mystery of what happened to Roman Sinquerra. After Captain Nick Wagner (Steve Howey) went to a meetup to get more information on this mystery, Morgan Gillory (Kaitlin Olson) found him shot and left for dead, leaving Wagner's fate up in the air over the hiatus.

When both of these series concluded their most recent seasons, fans were left champing at the bit to find out what happens next. While there are still devoted fans excited for these series to return, there has no doubt been a loss of momentum for both of these series after their unusually long break. Most fans will likely have to do a refresher on the events of the previous seasons to remember the main events ahead of their returns, and while that's become standard for shows on streaming now, this is still atypical of Network TV shows. ABC needs to do better with the shows in its spring lineup, and to stop moving shows to mid-season after they've previously been airing for the entire season.

High Potential returns to ABC on Tuesday, January 5, at 9:00 P.M. EST. The Rookie returns to ABC the following night, on Wednesday, January 6, at 9:00 P.M. EST.

Chelsea are reportedly prepared to consider loaning out Jamie Gittens during the January transfer window as the winger continues to struggle for regular opportunities at Stamford Bridge.

US CPI Released: October Inflation Beats Expectations

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

U.S. inflation came in hotter than economists expected in October, with headline CPI rising 0.4% month-on-month, above the 0.3% forecast. Core CPI also increased 0.3%, beating the 0.2% expectation.

This has pressured markets, with Bitcoin falling below $73,500 as the odds of a Fed rate hike surged.

The latest CPI data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics showed headline inflation rising 0.4% in October, compared with economists’ expectations for a 0.3% increase.

Core CPI, which excludes food and energy prices, rose 0.3% month-on-month, above the 0.2% forecast. The figure matched the monthly increase recorded in September.

Meanwhile, the BLS has not released an official year-over-year CPI rate for October because the 43-day government shutdown in 2025 left it without the October 2025 data needed to make the annual comparison.

The stronger CPI reading quickly shifted attention to the Federal Reserve’s next policy decision. After the data release, the CME FedWatch Tool showed the probability of a 25-basis-point rate hike rising from 84% to 99%.

Speaking at the Bank of England Watchers Conference, Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he would support raising interest rates by 25 basis points to 4%–4.25% in December.

He also warned that if core inflation rose above 0.3%, the Fed could consider a larger 50-basis-point hike.

The hotter reading strengthened the case for tighter monetary policy and increased attention on the Fed’s December decision.

In response to the CPI data release, Bitcoin’s price saw a slight drop of 1%, reaching $73,479, with its market cap falling to $1.48 trillion.

The broader cryptocurrency market also came under selling pressure as traders reassessed the outlook for U.S. interest rates.

Valve opens up the doors to the data it's been collecting.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Steam Deck is the most popular handheld gaming PC, and no doubt a lot of you own one already. It's also far from the most powerful handheld on the market, meaning devs have to get optimisation just right to allow the Deck to maintain 60 or even 30 fps. Now, Valve is letting everyone see exactly how well those optimisations are made by listing average Steam Deck frame rate data on game store pages.

Valve explains that this applies to any game that's been tested for Steam Deck compatibility, not just Verified ones. It applies to 'Verified', 'Playable', and 'Unsupported' ones. Apparently that means thousands of games right now.

The data is based on what different users have opted in to provide, which, despite only being for opt-inners, presumably still gives a big sample size. We've known about Valve collecting this kind of data since at least February this year, and we heard about Valve handing it over to game devs just last month.

Now, Valve is letting us all get in on the action. To look at a game's average frame rate data, go to its store page, look on the right-hand side for where it lists its 'Steam Deck Compatibility', and click 'Learn more'.

Once there, you're shown the average fps, and if you click the down arrow, you can view a "framerate histogram... breaking down the percentage of data samples at each framerate." The bigger a particular bar, the more of the game session (on average) is enjoyed at that frame rate.

I checked the Elden Ring data, for instance, and found that the Steam Deck OLED averages 42 fps and the Steam Deck LCD 38 fps. The latter seems mostly split between 60 fps and 30 fps, with most at 30 and little in between, leading me to believe most players lock their frame rate for more stable gameplay.

The biggest benefit, though, will probably just be the ability to quickly view the average frame rate. It will mean less guessing exactly how well a game should perform on your Steam Deck before you decide to purchase. And that can benefit other handheld gamers, too, as if you have a more powerful handheld, you'll know you can achieve at least that frame rate or higher.

Consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, more than expected, complicating Fed's inflation fight

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Costs for goods and services rose more than expected in October, providing fresh warning signs for the Federal Reserve in its fight to lower inflation.

The consumer price index, a broad gauge of costs across the U.S. economy, increased a seasonally adjusted 0.4% for the month, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. Economists surveyed by Dow Jones had been looking for a 0.3% increase.

Stripping out volatile food and energy, so-called core CPI increased 0.3% against the forecast for a 0.2% gain.

The headline CPI had increased 0.6% in September while core was up 0.3%.

The BLS did not estimate a yearly rate. The government shutdown last year prevented the bureau from collecting sufficient data in October 2025, so there is no comparable figure to use. However, the report did include annual figures for some individual items where it was able to collect data.

Stock market futures drifted lower following the release while Treasury yields jumped. Traders fully priced in a quarter percentage point increase when the Fed next meets Dec. 8-9 and put odds of a half-point hike at 13.6%, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool.

The report comes at a critical juncture for the Federal Reserve as it considers its next move regarding interest rates. At its October meeting, central bank officials voted 10-2 to keep their benchmark rate in a range between 3.75%-4% while awaiting incoming data.

"This was the report the market could least afford to see," said Karen Manna, fixed income investment strategist at Federated Hermes. "A hotter inflation print raises fresh questions about whether the Fed has done enough to contain price pressures and reinforces the risk that interest rates need to move higher and stay there for longer."

Though gasoline prices have been falling in recent weeks, the energy index rose 0.7% and the gasoline index was up 1%. The natural gas index, though, was off 0.8%.

Food costs accelerated 0.5%, pushed higher by increases of 1% for snacks, 0.9% for beef and 0.5% for meats, poultry, fish and eggs. Transportation services jumped 0.8%. New vehicle prices increased 0.2% but used cars and trucks fell 1.2%.

Shelter costs were higher by 0.3%, which the BLS said accounted for more than 30% of the headline increase.

Apparel, a tariff-sensitive sector, rose just 0.1%. Medical care commodities prices increased 0.5%. Airline fares accelerated 0.4%.

Markets already had been expecting the Fed to hike its benchmark borrowing rate next month, but the inflation data may have sealed the deal.

Speaking Tuesday morning in London ahead of the CPI release, Governor Christopher Waller said a hot inflation report could push him toward advocating for a half-point increase in December. Waller added that it's important the Fed follow through on expectations for a hike.

Despite the moderating gas prices recently, Fed officials have expressed concern that the impacts from the Iran war and President Donald Trump's tariffs are becoming embedded in the economy. Inflation has been running above the Fed's 2% target for some 5½ years.

In addition to tariffs and the war, inflation has been pressured by hyperscaler spending in the artificial intelligence buildout. Electricity prices rose 0.9%, a potential complication from the AI story.

Consumers, though, continue to spend — retail sales rose 0.8% in September, according to the Commerce Department — and businesses are still raising prices.

A net 37% of businesses responding to the October National Federation of Independent Business survey reported that they plan to raise prices in the next three months, the highest level since July 2022.

Original Harry Potter Star Officially Confirms Franchise Return Ahead of HBO Series

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The release of HBO's Harry Potter is just a few weeks away at this point, and it feels like the entire world is watching and waiting to rejoin the Wizarding World. However, there are a select few who have already been there, and when given the opportunity to head back to Hogwarts, it seems like they just couldn't say no.

Speaking exclusively with Collider's Steve Weintraub while promoting his upcoming movie Ebenezer, Rupert Grint revealed that his return as Ron Weasley in Broadway's Harry Potter and the Cursed Child will probably be the last time he plays the character. The actor is scheduled to begin his limited engagement at New York's Lyric Theatre on February 2, 2027. Grint explained that, after just over 15 years away from the role, it felt like the right time because, not only does he now have a family of his own, but he's also reached roughly the same age as Ron in the stage production. Grint told Collider:

"It's funny, kind of, it's interesting. Kind of going back, I think I spent so much time after finishing Potter moving away from it and doing other things which I really enjoy doing. But I think now it's a very different Ron in a very different context, and weirdly, it's lined up that I'm kind of the exact age of the character in that story. So it just felt like a good time, and I love New York and being on stage. It's been a while since I've done that, and it's a very important thing to me. It was my childhood, and I have a very strong connection to that character and that world. And I think I'm not going to get many other opportunities to play him again. It probably is my last, and I think it'll be fun."

Grint previously made his Broadway debut in It's Only a Play in 2014, but returning to the stage as a character he's known for most of his life presents an entirely different challenge, and he admitted that he couldn't resist the challenge, while confessing the nostalgia had been a delight. "Well, yeah. I mean, it's very nostalgic," said Grint. "Even for me, it's fun to kind of go back and get back into that world. It's been a while." Of course, Weintraub couldn't resist asking the question that just about every Harry Potter fan will be thinking: could Grint persuade Radcliffe and Watson to join him on Broadway for a reunion?

"It would be fun. I don't know. I mean, it's obviously different for everyone. It was an intense kind of time. When we finished, I really enjoyed stepping away and not really even thinking about it. Because it was such an intense... I mean, it was enjoyable, and they looked after us. But it was a big part of us. So yeah, maybe. I don't know. It'd be fun."

Before Grint returns to Hogwarts, audiences will get to see him in Ebenezer, a new adaptation of Charles Dickens' A Christmas Carol starring Johnny Depp (Pirates of the Caribbean) as Ebenezer Scrooge, and directed by Ti West. Grint plays Bob Cratchit, the father of Tiny Tim. The tale has been adapted many times for the screen. It's a thrilling ghost story set in Dickens’ London that follows one man’s supernatural journey to face his past, present, and future — and his fight for a second chance.

In addition to Grint, the film stars Andrea Riseborough (To Leslie) as the Ghost of Christmas Past, Sam Claflin (The Hunger Games) as Fred, Daisy Ridley (Star Wars: The Force Awakens) as Clara, Arthur Conti (Beetlejuice Beetlejuice) as Young Ebenezer, Ellie Bamber (Willow) as Belle, Tramell Tillman (Severance) as the Ghost of Christmas Present, and Ian McKellen (The Lord of the Rings) as Jacob Marley. The screenplay comes from Nathaniel Halpern, with Emma Watts producing. Ebenezer opens in theaters on November 13, 2026.

Rupert Grint begins his limited Broadway engagement as Ron Weasley in Harry Potter and the Cursed Child on February 2, 2027, with performances scheduled through May 30. Archaeologists Drug-Tested a 2,800-Year-Old Nomad—and Found the World’s Earliest Stoner

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Hair from a royal Scythian tomb holds the oldest direct evidence of people getting high on weed.

Some 2,800 years ago, Scythian nomads in what is now southern Siberia were using cannabis to get high, and researchers believe they finally have the evidence to prove it. Exactly how the horse-riding nomads took the drug is still unclear (the best guess is that they unsurprisingly inhaled it), but the find stands as the oldest direct evidence of mind-altering cannabis use anywhere in the world.

“The findings suggest that the plant was used for its psychoactive qualities across Eurasia, where specific wild populations might have had naturally higher active compound levels,” a group of researchers wrote in a study published in the journal Science. “In this study, we present the earliest direct evidence of cannabis use: offerings of plant parts and chemical compounds in ancient human hair recovered from a monumental tomb in Siberia.”

Researchers from the University of Bern and the Max Planck Institute of Geoanthropology studied the tomb of a high-ranking Scythian in southern Siberia. The tomb contained over 1,000 cannabis seeds, but more interesting was the examination of preserved human hair from the central burial chamber showing cannabis consumption.

“The seeds show that cannabis played an important role in the ritual surrounding the burial,” Gino Caspari, an archaeologist and the study’s lead author, said in a statement. “This study is particularly insightful because it combines the analysis of plant remains with molecular traces in the human body.”

Cannabis is the world’s most widely used illicit psychoactive plant, but prehistoric evidence of people consuming it is rare. Plant remains seldom survive for thousands of years, and most of the archaeological record points to cannabis being used for oil and fiber rather than for getting high. To date the Siberian burial, the team radiocarbon-dated six of the surviving seeds, which put the site at about 2,800 years old. They then analyzed the hair for chemical signs of consumption. If the results hold up, they push back the earliest known psychoactive use of cannabis by at least 300 years. The previous record came from a roughly 2,500-year-old site in western China.

The seeds are likely from wild plants, and the hair samples revealed clear signals consistent with cannabinol, produced by the oxidation of tetrahydrocannabinol, the most intoxicating compound in cannabis. “The molecular traces in the hair and the cannabis seeds come from the same grave and suggest that cannabis was used for its intoxicating properties,” Caspari said.

“It’s only so often as an archaeologist that you do get to drug test a person that’s been dead for almost 3,000 years,” Caspari told the Associated Press, adding the 1,000 cannabis seeds are “not there by accident” but instead show that the plant held great significance for the ancient Scythians.

For roughly 2,500 years, the best-known account of Scythian cannabis use came from a Greek writer. Around 440 B.C., Herodotus described Scythians throwing hemp seeds onto red-hot stones inside felt tents and howling with pleasure in the smoke. Archaeologists have since found burned seeds and cannabis-stained braziers that seem to support his story, but direct evidence that ancient people actually took the drug has remained hard to find.

“There is a lot of speculation about the use of psychoactive substances in the past,” Caspari told Scientific American. “It is extremely rare to find real evidence for it. We bring a bit of realism into these debates, and we show that cannabis has a deep cultural history which might factor into ongoing societal discourse on legalization and medical use.”

Not everyone is convinced. Volker Auwärter, head of forensic toxicology at the University Medical Center Freiburg in Germany, told the Associated Press that the hair analysis is “scientifically weak.” Because cannabis was widely grown for oil and fiber, he argued, the presence of the plant doesn’t prove anyone took it as a drug. He also said the hair may have absorbed traces of cannabis from the seeds buried nearby.

Caspari said the team is sequencing some of the plant samples to learn more about the history of the domestication and spread of the cannabis crop. “We show that the practice of cannabis consumption dates back at least 2,800 years,” the authors wrote, “anchoring debates around contemporary usage and informing discussions on cannabis.”

Tim Newcomb is a journalist based in the Pacific Northwest. He covers stadiums, sneakers, gear, infrastructure, and more for a variety of publications, including Popular Mechanics. His favorite interviews have included sit-downs with Roger Federer in Switzerland, Kobe Bryant in Los Angeles, and Tinker Hatfield in Portland.

This flying home security camera has been in the works for a while

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After years in the works, Ring's flying home security camera drone is finally taking flight. The Ring Drone Cam, as it's called, has gone through several iterations since the company first introduced it as the Ring Always Home cam three years ago.

Now that Ring is ready to ship the device, it gave us a sneak preview in advance. Here's my initial impressions of the Ring Drone Cam, as well as how much it'll cost, and when you can get one for your home.

Unlike the Always Home Cam, which had a large protuberance on its underside, the Ring Drone Cam looks somewhat like the DJI Neo; it has a small rectangular body with four encaged rotors. Weighing 265 grams (5.8 ounces), it's lighter than most phones. While the demo unit I saw was white, the actual product will be a light gray. The Drone Cam docks and recharges in a square base station.

The drone has a 2K camera with two LEDs on either side, which it uses to light up your home as it's patrolling at night.

When activated, the Drone Cam lifts up out of its base, rises to about 4-5 feet in the air, and then proceeds at a stately pace as it moves throughout your home. Even though it's small, you'll definitely notice its presence, as its rotors make a very loud, high-pitched whine.

Knowing how my dog reacts to a vacuum cleaner, I can only imagine his response to this thing flying around the house. Don't even ask what my cats might think.

The Drone Cam navigates your home using a combination of an Alexa Home map (similar to what you'll see on one of Amazon's smart displays) and the setup process, whereby you hold the drone and carry it through your house, taking it on a trip of where you'd like to go. Ring says the Drone Cam can cover multiple stories of your home.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Unlike previous, unreleased versions, the Drone Cam relies on the cloud for navigation, using Amazon's AI Pilot to get around and interpret what the drone is seeing. If the drone loses its connection, it's designed to immediately land — which is why it's important that you make sure its flight path doesn't go over any delicate objects.

The Ring Drone Cam can be programmed to make regular patrols of your house, or to respond to a particular event — such as a window or a door sensor tripping, or a water leak detector going off. Using the Amazon Alexa Home map, it can fly to the location of the event, and give you a live view of what's happening.

Remember the Amazon Astro? Ring took its parent company's home robot and redesigned the internals to make it a security robot for small businesses and enterprise customers.

Now called the Ring Rover Cam, it's meant to patrol warehouses and stores, and alert you if it detects an intruder. Like the Astro, it has a telescoping camera, but Ring also added a light on the back, which lets you see where the robot is at a glance, and flashes red and screams out a siren when there's an unwanted guest.

The Ring Rover Cam will cost $999, and will ship on December 16.

I am more than a bit skeptical about having a camera drone flying through my house. For starters, my pets get nervous enough around vacuum cleaners; I can only imagine how they'd react to a high-pitched drone flying about. And, the idea of a drone autonomously roaming around, potentially recording everything, feels a bit dystopian.

And, if you want the Ring Drone Camera to be able to access every corner of your house, you'll also have to make sure you leave every door wide open before you leave. And, the drone moves so slowly that if it's set up on one side of your house, by the time it responds to, say, a window breaking on the other side, the potential intruder could be long gone.

I can see some utility in it — I don't have, nor do I want, security cameras in every corner of my house, so I can see it being handy if a sensor is tripped in my basement. But, I'm still not sold on the concept; maybe trying it out will change my mind.

What do you think? Would you want a drone security camera for the inside of your house? Take our poll, and let me know in the comments.
